#10653. 1994 Jeep Cherokee owner hacks 32-year-old car with Claude AI to pass California smog tests

Car owner says they just did a bit of wiring and driving, crediting Claude and Codex with the rest of the job.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An AI enthusiast managed to get his old 1994 Jeep Cherokee to pass the California smog tests, all thanks to the help of AI and a little electronics wiring skill. This 32-year-old car had only a primitive engine computer, but after connecting it to a laptop via an Arduino Nano board, many settings could be read and eventually controlled.

The above video documents Muhammad Zulali’s journey from the Jeep failing consecutive smog tests, to managing to hook it up and get readings from the ECU, to tuning and fixing the engine emission issues. All the while, Claude provided guidance, and Codex wrote any required code.

This rather old Jeep was failing the California smog tests due to producing too much NOx at certain test speeds. It initially failed at 25mph. Then, after Zulali made some adjustments to the oxygen mix, it failed at both 25mph and 50mph. Yes, it got worse.

Zulali admits he was effectively making adjustments blind, and subsequent regulatory pollution tests cost nearly $100 each time. That’s clearly a big problem, but we have AI to help us in 2026. Actually, it was AI and a $3 microcontroller, contributing to a total bill of materials of no more than $15.

The first hurdle facing the possible AI solution was the question of how to interface an old car and a new laptop. Claude told the Jeep owner that the engine communicated on a Chrysler SCI bus at ~7,800 baud, which was electrically inverted compared to a typical serial port. An Arduino Nano can handle the required inversion and hook up the data to a modern computer. The Nano was breadboarded, with a few necessary wires, three of which connected to the 6-pin diagnostic plug in the Cherokee.

The first connection attempt was a success, but only coolant temperature and battery voltage were charted by the system to begin with. Zulali wanted more data points plus some controls to fix the smog test problem. After a couple of red herrings regarding the engine “running rich” with 22% extra fuel, based on other car model data, the Jeep owner got Claude to collect data from test drives. The AI logged engine data and fault codes during a drive at 25 mph and at 50 mph to see what was happening. It was also let loose to control things like fuel pressure.

With what looked like a credible diagnosis and solution in hand, Zulali wanted to reflash the car’s main ECU with AI-tweaked code that would mean it could pass all the California smog tests. Unfortunately, this car’s computer was just a few months too old to feature reflashable firmware. Instead, the Jeep owner got Codex to inject and run custom code inside a nearby EEPROM.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

“Claude did most of the diagnostic work and Codex did pretty much all of the memory work,” admits Zulali. “I just did the wiring and the driving.” He added that he didn’t need the services of a mechanic or a Chrysler dealer tool for this feat.

In addition to the video, the Cherokee emissions fixer has created a GitHub repository for this project, dubbed xj-scanner. It is free software distributed under the GPLv3 license.

#10651. Sony’s latest Pulse wireless headset packs planar magnetic drivers at a good price

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A comfortable, minimalist option that supports concurrent low-latency audio and Bluetooth.

A comfortable, minimalist option that supports concurrent low-latency audio and Bluetooth.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s tempting to seek out the do-everything wireless gaming headset with noise cancellation, phenomenal sound quality, multiplatform connectivity, swappable batteries — you name it. But if you just want the basics done well, Sony’s new $99.99 PlayStation Pulse headset is worth considering. It has very good sound and just enough extras, even if you use more gadgets than just your PlayStation 5.

Inside the Pulse’s nondescript ear cup shells are custom planar magnetic drivers, which deliver clean, detailed sound whether you’re gaming, listening to music, or both. They aren’t bass-heavy or too shrill; their default equalizer is pleasing and punchy, though you can also tweak it in the PS5’s interface, like other Pulse headsets. They’re not Sony’s first audio gaming product to feature this type of high-end driver (the first was in 2023), but they are the most affordable at $99.

The Pulse comes with either a white or black headband, with a soft-touch underside and a decent amount of give near the top of your head where it rests its weight. Thankfully, the headset is lightweight and offers size adjustments that go beyond what my large head requires for comfort. These are some of the only headphones I’ve used with ear cups that can rotate 180 degrees in either direction.

Functionally, the Pulse stands out as a semi-affordable wireless headset that can stream audio from PlayStation Link (Sony’s low-latency, lossless wireless connection) and Bluetooth simultaneously. It includes a USB-C Link dongle that lets you pair it quickly with whatever you plug it into, whether it’s a PS5, PC, or Nintendo Switch 2.

The headset’s ear cup controls include a dedicated Bluetooth button that, beyond pairing with another gadget, lets you pause or resume Bluetooth content (it can also answer or hang up calls). You can also control Bluetooth audio volume using the integrated volume rocker, but only when a device isn’t connected via PlayStation Link. In simultaneous audio mode, the rocker adjusts audio solely for the device connected via the Link dongle.

If you have Sony’s PlayStation Portal, the Pulse can connect directly to the handheld without the Link dongle, just as Sony’s Pulse Elite and Explore can. It’s so convenient, and I hope Sony pushes for more devices that integrate the Link wireless technology in the future.

The Pulse isn’t the only headset that Sony has in the works. Its Pulse Edge wireless headset doesn’t have an exact launch date or price, but the company touts that it packs in twice as many magnets in its drivers (eight versus four), active noise cancellation, and a detachable boom mic that will likely provide better-sounding voice audio than the built-in mic in the standard Pulse. It will also include game / chat audio controls, a 3.5mm jack, and a carrying case.

While the $99 Pulse is comparatively light on features, Sony’s focus on providing standout audio quality and comfort makes it worth seeking out if you have a PS5 and are on a strict budget for your next wireless headset.

#10649. Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

LangSmith is closed-source and restricts self-hosting to enterprise licenses, teams that run their own LLMOps stack and want to keep traces on their own servers need an alternative. To find the best fit, we set a baseline by tracing a small TypeScript agent (equipped with two tools) in LangSmith Cloud. Then, we ran the exact same agent through Langfuse, Arize Phoenix, Opik, and AcruxCore using each platform's recommended TypeScript setup. All tests were run on November 5, 2026, and we pulled every trace back through each tool's API to verify the data.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree for the test prompt: a single agent span containing three LLM calls and two tool calls. The real differences between them come down to how much boilerplate code you have to write, how spans are typed, whether costs are tracked, and the overall server footprint.

Why Teams Look for a LangSmith Alternative

LangSmith remains the best choice if you already use LangChain and allow cloud hosting—it required just 25 lines of code in our test. The alternatives below are for teams that don't fit that profile.

To test these platforms, we built a simple customer support agent equipped with two tools:

Both tools use static fixture data to ensure every test run is identical. The agent runs a standard loop: it prompts the model, executes any requested tools, and stops once the model provides a final answer.

We tested the agent with three specific prompts to see how the tracing handled different levels of complexity:

For our comparison, we focused entirely on the third question because it generates a robust, multi-step trace.

Under the hood, the agent uses the standard openai package to call the qwen3.8-flash model. We originally picked this model based on its reliable tool-calling behavior during early probing thought it once skipped a tool call later.

We used LangSmith as our baseline. By tracing the agent there first, we created a checklist of every field it captured, and we judged all the alternatives against that exact list.

For setup, we followed the recommended TypeScript approach from each tool's documentation. Phoenix didn't have specific instructions for using its register() helper with OpenAI in TypeScript, so we adapted the pattern from their Anthropic guide.

Here is the agent with no tracing. Each tool section adds one tracing file that imports these files and changes nothing in them.

LangSmith traces the plain openai package with two helpers. wrapOpenAI records each model call, and traceable records your own functions. Without traceable on the run and the tools, wrapOpenAI alone gave three separate root runs and no tool runs.

This file adds 25 lines and removes 3, and installs one package.

LangSmith showed one root run with five children in call order: LLM, get_order_status, LLM, get_refund_policy, LLM. Every field it recorded became a row of the checklist table under "Results". Cost appeared only after a custom price entry. In our error run the failing tool was marked as an error, but the root stayed success: traceable turned the throw into a rejected promise that our agent did not await, so the agent carried on and answered.

Langfuse self-hosts as six containers, including ClickHouse, a column store built for large scans. In our measurement the stack held 2.60–2.64 GiB of memory at idle. Its Docker Compose guide recommends at least 4 CPU cores and 16 GiB RAM, with about 100 GiB of disk.

The TypeScript path is built on OpenTelemetry. You start the OTel Node SDK with LangfuseSpanProcessor, wrap the client with observeOpenAI, and wrap your own functions with observe:

This file adds 31 lines and removes 5, and installs four packages. We pinned the OTel Node SDK to 0.223.0, because @langfuse/otel 6.1.1 does not accept 0.300.0.

Langfuse successfully recreated the same trace tree, neatly tagging the spans as AGENT, GENERATION, and TOOL. It captured all the essentials: tool calls, tool definitions, and the complete message history. However, for the LLM output, it only saved the assistant's message—dropping the finish_reason, response ID, and the provider name.

We ran our deployment in Langfuse v4's events_only mode, which turns off the v1 trace endpoint. Because of this, the API didn't return a centralized trace object or root-level sums for tokens and costs, even though the UI calculated and displayed them perfectly (we didn't test other modes).

Costs showed up exactly as expected once we added a custom price entry. Since Langfuse calculates prices based on the specific usage types you define, and we only set rates for input and output, the separate reasoning tokens went unpriced. Finally, during our error test, the observe wrapper let the error bubble up to the agent, correctly flagging both the tool and the root span as an ERROR.

Phoenix self-hosts as two containers, one Python server and Postgres. In our measurement it held 0.47 GiB at idle. Phoenix is source-available under the Elastic License 2.0, so it is not open source. Authentication is off until you turn it on.

The TypeScript path uses Phoenix's register() helper and the OpenInference instrumentation for the OpenAI package. OpenInference is a set of OpenTelemetry attribute names for LLM spans. traceAgent and traceTool wrap the run and the tools:

This file adds 32 lines and removes 3, and installs three packages.

Phoenix rebuilt the same tree, typed as AGENT, LLM and TOOL. Each LLM span keeps the full OpenAI response object, plus the messages as flattened attributes. The root span stores no token count, but Phoenix computes a sum for the root and the trace when you read it. Cost and those sums are only in the GraphQL API and the UI, and the REST API has no per-trace endpoint.

Cost appeared after a custom price entry under Settings > Models, and Phoenix priced the reasoning tokens by falling back to the output price. In the error run, the tool span and the root were marked ERROR, and the tool span carried an exception event with the stack trace.

Opik self-hosts as eight containers, including ClickHouse, MySQL and ZooKeeper. In our measurement it held 3.69 GiB at idle, the most of the four. The self-hosted UI opened without a login, so every trace was readable from any machine that could reach the port.

The TypeScript path wraps the client with trackOpenAI and your own functions with track. By default trackOpenAI starts a new trace for every model call, so the file passes the run's span through the documented parent option:

This file adds 33 lines and removes 5. It installs opik-openai 2.2.142, and npm adds opik 1.11.14 as its peer, because opik-openai asks for opik ^1.8.61.

Opik successfully recreated the trace tree, and its trace object neatly tallies token counts across all LLM spans. In the logs, tool arguments are bundled into an array, and the LLM output only retains the assistant's message.

We did notice a quirk with the documented OpenAI wrapper (tested on the npm package opik 1.11.14): it tags model calls as general rather than llm, which incorrectly leaves the llm_span_count at zero. (We didn't test newer Opik core versions to see if this has changed). Additionally, because our synchronous tools execute in under a millisecond, they log the exact same start time as the subsequent LLM call, causing the UI to list the LLM call first out of order.

Cost tracking is entirely manual. Opik showed no costs by default, and the documentation doesn't mention a custom price table. We checked the configuration tabs on our 2.2.52 instance (Feedback definitions, AI Providers, Workspace preferences) and found no model pricing settings. To see costs, you have to manually assign a price to each individual span yourself—only the spans we hardcoded this way displayed a cost.

Finally, error reporting is highly detailed. If something fails, Opik attaches an error_info block—complete with the error type, message, and full traceback—directly to the tool span, the root span, and the overall trace.

AcruxCore self-hosts as five containers: Postgres, Redis, a Node API and worker, and nginx. In our measurement it held 0.39 GiB at idle, the least of the four. Its quickstart leaves sign-up open and ships a committed default GATEWAY_ENCRYPTION_KEY, which encrypts stored provider keys; replace it. We tested a local build from 2026-09-17.

The TypeScript path AcruxCore's docs put first is its gateway. The official openai client keeps working: you point its baseURL at ACRUXCORE_BASE_URL + "/gateway" and call a model registered in AcruxCore, which forwards each call to an OpenAI-compatible provider. The gateway records every model call as an llm span. The SDK's step helpers add the rest: traces.step opens the agent span, traces.wrap wraps each tool, and two headers file the gateway's spans under the agent span:

This file adds 30 lines and removes 5, and installs one package. It builds its own client, so it does not use provider.ts. The gateway refused our team API key, so the client uses a gateway virtual key created in the dashboard.

On this path every model call passes through the AcruxCore server, which none of the other four tools need. If you do not want a proxy in the request path, the SDK's register() helper sends OpenTelemetry spans from your process instead. On that path the step helpers do not join the openai package's spans, so you write the agent and tool spans by hand.

AcruxCore rebuilt the same tree, typed as agent, llm and tool, in call order. Each LLM span keeps the full response, the provider and the cached-token count, and the trace carries a token total. The LLM spans do not keep the tool definitions sent to the model, and no reasoning-token count was recorded. The trace took its name from the question, not from the agent span, and each LLM span is named by its start time.

Cost appeared on every LLM span and as a sum on the trace, from the custom price we set on the model in AcruxCore's model list. In the error run, the step helpers marked the tool span and the agent root as error with the error message, with no status code of ours. The API returned no stack trace.

All five tools accept traces over OTLP, the OpenTelemetry wire format. trace-otel.ts sends the same spans with no vendor package: the OTel Node SDK, the OTLP/HTTP protobuf exporter and the OpenInference OpenAI instrumentation. The exporter reads its endpoint and headers from environment variables:

We ran the two-tool question once per backend and changed only environment variables: the endpoint, the headers, and for Phoenix a resource attribute that sets the project name (without it, spans go to a default project). "Accepted" means the exporter reported success, which it does only for an HTTP 2xx, and we read the spans back through each API.

Opik stored the root and tool spans as general although AGENT and TOOL kinds were sent; the cause is Not tested. LangSmith mapped AGENT to its chain type. If you instrument with plain OTel and OpenInference attributes, your raw spans are not tied to one vendor, but span types, cost and trace views still differ by backend.

The first table covers setup: how much server each tool needs, how much code we changed in the agent, and how cost and the plain OTel exporter worked. Phoenix and AcruxCore had the smallest servers, and AcruxCore needed the fewest lines among the self-hosted tools. Opik was the only one without a price table.

The second table checks each trace against what LangSmith recorded for the same run, one field at a time. All five rebuilt the same tree with tokens and durations. They differ in span types, how much of the LLM output they keep, where token sums live, and how much detail they store for the failing tool.

Token totals for the two-tool question ranged from 1,910 to 2,111. Each tool traced a separate run and reasoning length varies, so these numbers do not compare the tools.

All five tools successfully traced our test agent, so the right choice really comes down to your team's specific constraints, infrastructure preferences, and how tracing fits into the rest of your MLOps tooling.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree. The real deciding factors are boilerplate code, span typing, cost tracking, and server bloat.

LangSmith remains the simplest option for LangChain users who can utilize the cloud. Otherwise, your choice depends on your priorities: Langfuse for community size, Phoenix for a lightweight Postgres footprint, Opik for strict Apache-2.0 compliance with deep error logging, or AcruxCore for combined tracing and tool governance on minimal hardware. Best of all, if you stick to standard OpenTelemetry spans, you aren't locked in—you can try them all and easily pivot later.

#10647. Ocarina of Time is even better if you use a smartphone app

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I never liked hunting for Golden Skulltulas in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. The game has 100 of them tucked all over Hyrule, and even over my many hours with the N64 classic growing up, I can’t remember ever actually finding all of them, or even wanting to. Jumping into the Ocarina of Time remake, I expected to ignore the Golden Skulltulas once again. But while poking around Nintendo’s Zelda Notes companion app, I discovered that its maps are a huge help to tracking down all of the elusive golden spiders — and pretty much every other collectible in the game — and now I find myself constantly opening up the app to make sure I haven’t missed a secret.

Inside Zelda Notes, which is available in the free Nintendo Switch app (you don’t need a Switch Online subscription to use it), you can peruse a big map of Hyrule and tap on individual areas for a closer look. The app offers just enough direction to nudge me toward an elusive pickup; when zoomed in, the app drops icons on the map showing you the locations of different collectibles, and if you tap on an icon, the app will show a popup noting any items or special requirements you need to fulfill to be able to obtain it. There are also a bunch of filters you can apply, like swapping between the maps for child and adult Link and toggling categories of icons on and off.

For Skulltula hunting, the map has already saved my sanity many times. While exploring, I’ll often come across the telltale Golden Skulltula sound but have no idea how to get to the tricky spider, even with the help of additional chimes from the new Echo Gem Skulltula tracking item. But with a peek at the Zelda Notes app, I can pinpoint the exact location of where the Skulltula is supposed to be and if I need to use a certain item or change the time of day to be able to get to it.

I recently delved into the Shadow Temple, for example, and I couldn’t figure out how to get up to a Skulltula that I saw while coming down an elevator. But when I checked the icon on the Zelda Notes map, it said that I needed the Scarecrow Song to create a hookshot point, which I hadn’t gotten yet. As soon as I finished the dungeon, getting the Scarecrow Song was my first priority.

Zelda Notes has been useful for tracking down Pieces of Heart, too, which give Link more health once you collect enough. While running up and down Zora’s River, I kept seeing two Pieces of Heart on faraway ledges that I couldn’t figure out how to get to. (My memories of my extensive time with Ocarina of Time as a kid also failed me.) But tapping one of the icons on the Zelda Notes map revealed that I needed a Cucco to get to it — and suddenly, I understood why there was one of the chirping white birds near the entrance of the zone.

Yes, it can be annoying to have to open my phone and dig through an app to track down all of the Skulltulas and other objects of curiosity scattered around Hyrule. I play video games to try and get away from my phone, not look at it more. And sure, I could just look up an online guide, but that often means wading through walkthroughs of varying quality, trusting an AI summary, or scrubbing through a YouTube video. But with Zelda Notes, I’m usually in and out relatively quickly, putting down my phone after seeing a clue or an icon that pushes me in the direction of the next golden spider. I’ve already found more than 70 Golden Skulltulas, and to my surprise, I now want to hunt for the full 100.

This is the lowest price we've ever seen for this 8BitDo controller, according to Camelcamelcamel's data. It's important to note that there are a few different variants of this game controller that you can buy. They're pretty much identical, except for some differences in the color and button layout. This one is the cheapest right now, and it'll work with your Windows PC immediately.

This is a wireless controller, and you can use it over 2.4 GHz wireless using the supplied USB adapter or via a Bluetooth connection. You can also use it wired, too, over USB. This is a Windows-compatible controller, but this particular variant of the 8BitDo Ultimate 2 is built with the Nintendo Switch in mind. This 8BitDo Ultimate 2 wireless controller works with Windows PCs, laptops, and Nintendo Switch game consoles, with Switch 2 functionality requiring a quick firmware update. You can fully customize this 8BitDo controller to suit how you play. That's thanks to its Ultimate V2 software, which you can use to adjust stick and trigger precision settings, remap the buttons, adjust the RGB lighting, and set your vibration settings. It also lets you set up in-game macros, which you can activate using long presses on different keys.

This is a wireless controller, so you'll need to charge its 1,000 mAh battery to keep using it (unless you decide to go wired). It comes with a charging dock, however, so you won't need to swap out any AAA batteries here. The dock works as a practical stand for your controller when you're not using it, too, and has a built-in light so you know when the charging is complete.

With a sale price of $51.12, this 8BitDo Ultimate 2 wireless game controller is now at the lowest price we've ever seen it. This is a great price for a versatile controller that you can use with both your PC and Nintendo consoles, along with other devices like laptops and handheld gaming PCs like the Steam Deck, too.

#10642. Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs, shame about the price [U]

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

Unfortunately, Ray-Ban Audio Glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

#10640. More game developers are starting to use generative AI

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

More video game developers are using generative AI tools in their jobs compared to last year, according to the GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey. Forty percent of developers surveyed say they use it “as part of their job,” an increase from 36 percent in 2025. Usage was higher with game developers that work on free-to-play games at 58 percent and mobile-only studio workers at 70 percent.

The survey is being published as the game industry continues to reckon with generative AI tools. While many game developers, particularly indie developers, have spoken out strongly against the technology, AI is at the center of indie awards conversations in part because Big Walk developer House House “sparingly” used LLMs to assist with technical challenges in the “final stretch” of development. AI is also increasingly being used to reverse-engineer retro games. And in a post on Thursday, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma seemed to indicate that AI will be central to Xbox’s future, saying that “A small team will be able to build worlds that today demand hundreds of developers and hundreds of millions of dollars.”

Seventy three percent of the survey’s respondents said they used AI for research and brainstorming, while 62 percent used it for data analysis and summarization, and 56 percent for code generation and debugging. Twenty two percent of respondents used generative AI for concept art or visual ideation, and 8 percent used it for production art or final game assets.

The survey also included grim numbers about layoffs, with 34 percent of respondents saying they have been laid off sometime in the past two years.

#10638. How Shanghai fund manager Kang Wei introduced his wife Alice Huang to Jensen and built $7.5 billion Nvidia AI GPU smuggling operation

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The businessman behind Alice Huang's climb into Jensen Huang's inner circle.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Jensen Huang had a stunning fall from grace — from being one of the richest people on Earth and one of the primary drivers of the AI boom to being implicated in two extramarital affair scandals and resigning from Nvidia in under two weeks. While many are focused on the former Nvidia chief’s relationship with Alice Huang, who was allegedly passing on information to Chinese intelligence, the Wall Street Journal dug deeper into the person who introduced her to Jensen — Kang Wei, a star fund manager in Shanghai who also happened to be Ms. Huang’s husband.

There have been reports that Kang Wei met Jensen Huang in the 2010s, when his investment firm was heavily involved in Chinese gaming companies. It’s not clear if this introduction predated Kang’s relationship and marriage to Alice, but sources close to Ms. Huang said that she was able to get close to Jensen by the 2020s. Despite being a couple, the WSJ reported that Kang seemingly accepted Alice’s relationship with Jensen, with people close to the couple saying that he would often joke that she was a personal assistant to the billionaire.

Kang Wei is not just an average business executive, though. He made a name for himself in the finance world as a strategist for Shenyin Wanguo before taking over the Zhonghai Fund in 2007. This made him one of the youngest mutual fund chiefs in China at that time, before finally setting out on his own and founding Shanghai Hexi in 2010.

It was also around this time that Alice Huang, whose Chinese name is Huang Le, entered the financial industry, first working at HSBC’s private banking arm in Hong Kong, before moving to CITIC Securities, where she attained a vice president position. It was during her time at CITIC that Alice crossed paths with Kang. Shanghai Hexi signed a deal with the securities firm in 2014 and 2015, and by 2016, corporate registry records show that Ms. Huang had joined the investment firm.

The Economic Times reports Alice Huang’s move into Shanghai Hexi resulted in her getting a 25% stake in the company, all of which came from Kang. The remaining shares were shared between Kang Wei, with 48.75%, and his business partner, Zhu Xiaoming, with 26.25%, with Alice Huang’s 25% ownership stake being held through Shanghai Yuxi Investment Management Consulting Co., Ltd. Meanwhile, Kang also started introducing Alice Huang as his wife to his business associates and investors.

One year later, Zhu and Kang had a falling out when one of their investments under a Hexi fund resulted in a major windfall, with the two partners disagreeing on how the profits were to be shared. Nevertheless, the massive profit generated by the deal allowed Shanghai Hexi to purchase Hangzhou Shengfeng, a video game company, for around 500 million yuan from Chinese conglomerate Century Huatong.

Two years after the transaction, in 2019, Hangzhou Shengfeng’s ownership was transferred to an offshore entity, which was eventually consolidated under Osmanthus Vale Holdings — a holding company incorporated in the British Virgin Islands and listed Huang Le, Alice Huang’s Chinese name, as its sole owner. It was then purchased by 7Road Holdings less than two months after it was founded. While Ms. Huang was the signatory in this deal, it’s believed that she served as a “white glove,” a person who operates on behalf of another to conceal their identity — but it remains unclear if this was for Shanghai Hexi and Kang Wei or some other third party.

There is not much information on Alice Huang’s activities between 2019 and 2021. However, Shanghai Hexi invested in Shanghai Longjing Information Technology, a data center-focused subsidiary of Century Huatong in 2020, where Ms. Huang served as a director between 2021 and 2022, according to a report by The Wire China. At about the same time, 7Road Holdings expanded into cloud computing, just before ChatGPT brought AI LLMs to the forefront. The U.S. government’s ban on the export of Nvidia chips to China put this investment in jeopardy, though, forcing it to spin off the business as 7Road International in Singapore.

We don’t know when 7Road Holdings sold its Singapore subsidiary, but corporate records show that by the time the company was renamed Megaspeed in March 2023, it was already under the ownership of Huang Le Limited and led by Ms. Huang. It was sold again in November 2023 to Swiftdata, a company owned by Singaporean Tan Yong Pong. Axios said that she left the firm as a managing director soon after this acquisition, but sources said that she kept working with the company afterward, with Kang Wei helping her get it off the ground.

In the same year, Shanghai Century Litong was founded to build AI data centers in Shanghai and Shenzhen, with Jiangsu Lettall Electronic Co., an electronic components manufacturer, holding a 90% stake, while the remaining 10% is owned by Century Longteng, as reported by BigGo Finance. It should be noted that Century Longteng was renamed from Shanghai Yuxi Investment Management, with the firm pivoting its business from finance to cloud computing, and that even though Alice Huang has already exited the company by this time, it’s under the control of Huang Yu, who some sources say is Ms. Huang’s sister.

A year after these data center acquisitions, Alice Huang began to be spotted in public with the former Nvidia CEO. A LinkedIn post showed her having drinks with a leather-clad Jensen Huang alongside other tech executives from NetThunder and Gigabyte at a post-Computex 2024 event. She was once again seen having dinner with him in a Taipei restaurant as reporters tried to interview him while one kid demanded his attention off camera.

But more than these public sightings, Alice herself boasted about her “closeness” to the then CEO. People close to her said that she regularly posted photos with Jensen on her WeChat account, and even shared others with potential customers to help establish her credibility. She even reportedly claimed that Mr. Huang would celebrate her birthdays with restaurant dinners and gifts, with one Nvidia China sales employee also saying that she was a familiar face at the company’s annual event for China partners.

An investigation revealed that from 2023 to 2025, Megaspeed imported $4.6 billion worth of Nvidia AI chips, while another $2 billion was routed through Speedmatrix, Megaspeed’s subsidiary with offices in Johor, Malaysia. Shanghai Century Litong also reportedly bought AI GPUs from other sources, with estimates putting the total number of banned chips moved through Alice Huang’s assistance at around 240,000, putting the shipments’ worth at around $7.5 billion.

But even as Ms. Huang was making her international moves, Kang Wei was facing trouble at home. Chinese regulators fined him 2.5 million yuan, or approximately $380,000, in 2025, for violating sections of China’s Securities Law. This also led to a warning letter against him, issued in August 2026 by the China Securities Regulatory Commission. Despite running into trouble with the authorities, this massive paper trail showed how Kang Wei actively made moves that put Alice Huang close to Jensen Huang.

Some questions remain, though, about the U.S. intelligence community’s claims that she worked with the Ministry of State Security (MSS), China’s primary intelligence and counterintelligence agency. If it’s true that Alice Huang worked with the MSS and passed on information to the agency, did she specifically target Jensen Huang, or did the spy agency simply discover their indiscretion and use that to force her to cooperate? Was all this planned by the MSS, or were they just shrewd business moves that allowed Kang to make more money while going under the radar of regulators? These are questions that only Kang Wei and Alice Huang (or the Chinese spy agency) can answer. But no matter the case, the fact remains that China acquired advanced AI chips that were supposed to be out of its reach because of this couple’s connections.

#10636. How to Build an Accessible Customer Reviews Section with HTML and CSS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Customer reviews are often displayed on business websites, but presenting them effectively requires more than adding a few star icons and customer quotes. Developers need to consider semantic HTML, responsive layouts, accessibility, and how review content fits into the overall user experience.

A well-built review section makes customer feedback easy to read without distracting visitors from the rest of the page. It should also work across different screen sizes and remain understandable to people using assistive technologies.

Let's look at how to build one using standard HTML and CSS.

The first step is to structure review content properly. Instead of placing everything inside generic

elements, use meaningful HTML elements to describe the content.

For example, a review can use

as its container and
for the customer's actual feedback.

This structure separates the review from its attribution and gives the section a clear heading. The rating also includes an accessible label so screen-reader users can understand what the stars represent.

For real websites, developers should display genuine customer feedback and avoid presenting invented testimonials as actual customer experiences.

A review section might look attractive on a desktop but become difficult to read on smaller screens. CSS Grid provides a straightforward way to create a flexible layout without relying on JavaScript.

The auto-fit and minmax() combination allows cards to adjust to the available space. Instead of defining a fixed number of columns for every device, the browser fits as many cards as the container can comfortably accommodate.

Developers working with more complex interfaces can explore SitePoint's guide to responsive web design for additional layout techniques.

A responsive layout should remain readable when viewed on a phone. Avoid fixed card widths, excessive padding, and text sizes that force users to zoom.

You can also adjust spacing at smaller viewport widths:

Test the section at several viewport sizes rather than relying on one desktop preview. Check that long customer names wrap correctly, cards do not overflow, and text remains comfortable to read.

Some review components use animated sliders, automatic scrolling, or hover effects. These features can create problems for visitors who prefer reduced motion or navigate with a keyboard.

A simple approach is to avoid unnecessary animation. If transitions are included, respect the visitor's system preference:

The MDN guide to using media queries for accessibility explains how developers can adapt interfaces to user preferences.

Also make sure interactive controls have visible keyboard focus styles. A carousel should provide usable previous and next controls, clear labels, and a way to pause automatic movement if it is used.

On a production website, reviews may come from a database, a content management system, or an external review service. Keeping the content separate from the presentation makes the component easier to maintain.

Businesses can also use an online reputation management platform to monitor customer feedback and understand how their brand is perceived across online channels.

For example, a developer could store each review with fields such as:

The frontend can then render the same component for every review. This avoids manually creating separate HTML for each customer and makes future design changes easier.

Developers should also validate incoming data, handle missing fields, and avoid displaying private customer information without permission.

Before publishing, check the component in different browsers and screen sizes. Test keyboard navigation, screen-reader labels, text wrapping, and color contrast. If reviews are loaded dynamically, check how the section behaves when there are no reviews or when the data request fails.

A useful review component does not need complicated animations or a large JavaScript library. Semantic HTML, flexible CSS Grid layouts, accessible labels, and reliable content handling provide a strong foundation.

Customer feedback can only be useful on a website when visitors can understand and navigate it easily. By treating reviews as a proper interface component rather than decorative content, developers can create sections that are maintainable, accessible, and consistent with the rest of the website.

#10633. 25 years later, Xbox is trying to reinvent itself again

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft has spent the past 25 years trying to shake up the game console market. The original Xbox, which launched on November 15th in 2001, took on an industry dominated by Sony and Nintendo. Twenty-five years later, Xbox finds itself in a similar position. It’s not starting from scratch this time around, but to reach another 25-year milestone, it needs to do something radically different.

The original Xbox was radically different. Born out of a fear of Sony putting a PC in the living room, Microsoft built its first game console around familiar PC technology. A custom Intel Pentium III processor powered the original Xbox, alongside an Nvidia GPU and 64MB of unified RAM. It was different from the fully custom architectures that Sony and Nintendo used at the time, allowing Microsoft to entice developers to its platform with tools that were more familiar to PC game makers.

Microsoft also included two hardware features that were unusual for a console at the time: a built-in hard drive and Ethernet port. The hard drive let game developers cache content without having to rely fully on optical discs, and Xbox players could also use it for game saves without having to purchase a separate memory card. You could even rip CDs to an Xbox and listen to custom soundtracks while playing games.

Xbox wasn’t the first to bring online gaming to consoles, but Microsoft took a different approach. Instead of relying on a built-in modem like Sega’s Dreamcast, Microsoft included an Ethernet port, anticipating a shift toward broadband internet connections. Xbox Live, which launched in November 2002, really nailed the entire online experience. There were friends lists, unique gamertags, voice chat through a bundled headset, and integrated multiplayer services. Microsoft created an entire platform for online gaming, making it easier for developers to support and fundamentally changing how people played games together.

The Xbox 360 took these ideas a step further, with swappable hard drives, digital game downloads, wireless controllers, and backward compatibility. It also turned Microsoft’s game console into an entertainment hub for the living room, complete with streaming video apps. Kinect was perhaps its most radical addition. Launched in 2010, the motion-sensing device used cameras and microphones to let players control games with their bodies and voices. It quickly became the fastest-selling consumer electronics device in early 2011.

But the success of Kinect and Microsoft’s ambition to turn Xbox into a home entertainment device helped lead to its first major strategic misstep with the Xbox One. Microsoft positioned the successor to the Xbox 360 around TV, entertainment, and an always-online future, instead of focusing on the games that had made Xbox successful. Its plans for online DRM and restrictions on used games sparked a huge backlash, while bundling Kinect pushed the console’s price $100 above Sony’s PlayStation 4. Six months after launch, Microsoft was forced to drop Kinect from the Xbox One console bundle to close that price gap.

The Xbox One showed what happens when Microsoft loses sight of what gamers want. The company spent most of that generation playing catch-up to Sony’s impressive PlayStation 4 sales. But its weakened position also forced Microsoft to once again try something radically different with Xbox Game Pass.

Introduced in 2017, Game Pass shook up the very economics of how people play games. The subscription service lets you access hundreds of games instead of buying them individually. Microsoft went on an acquisition spree to power its Game Pass ambitions, buying up smaller studios, Bethesda, and eventually Activision Blizzard.

These days, Microsoft is spinning many Xbox plates. The Xbox Series X and S consoles that launched in 2020 are way behind Sony’s PS5 sales numbers, and Xbox Cloud Gaming hasn’t yet been able to reach the millions more players that Microsoft has always wanted for its ecosystem.

Xbox Cloud Gaming promised to make games accessible on phones and tablets without needing a dedicated console, and combined with Microsoft’s messy project to bring Xbox games to PS5 and Nintendo consoles, the efforts have raised questions about what the Xbox brand actually represents these days. Microsoft hasn’t quite decided whether it wants to be a full third-party publisher, an ecosystem owner, or an unusual combination of both.

Some of Microsoft’s recent radically different Xbox bets haven’t paid off, so it’s now turning its attention to a much smaller Xbox division, with a restructuring that will see overall Xbox headcount reduced by 20 percent by the end of Microsoft’s fiscal year in June 2027. “I believe the future of gaming will look more like its beginning, when a small team with a big idea could change how the world plays,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma wrote in a message to Xbox employees today.

Microsoft is celebrating 25 years of Xbox today with an event in Seattle for fans, where it’s launching Xbox Playback, a personalized look back at players’ 25-year Xbox history. There will be more fan-focused announcements throughout the day, but I’m not expecting any news on the next Xbox console. The celebration is awkwardly timed, because rumors have suggested that Microsoft is also preparing for more Xbox layoffs in November, during the same month it’s celebrating a milestone.

The reset also comes as Microsoft is preparing for a next-generation Xbox console, codenamed Project Helix, which will play both Xbox games and PC titles. It sounds like an effort to combine Windows and Xbox like never before. The next Xbox could also play every Xbox game ever made, thanks to Microsoft’s impressive bets on backward compatibility over the years.

It’s an interesting full-circle moment. The original Xbox was essentially a PC built for the living room. Project Helix appears to be taking a similar approach, but this time around bringing the flexibility of PC gaming into a console experience. If Microsoft gets it right, it could open Xbox up to a much bigger library of games, and entice people trying to decide between a console or a gaming PC.

The problem for Xbox is getting consumers to care about this next console, especially when its games are available on PC and sometimes on Sony’s and Nintendo’s platforms. “Xbox has the best console ecosystem around, packed with consumer-friendly features like free cloud saves, Game Pass, and my preferred controller,” says Jon Clarke, cofounder and editor-in-chief of DayOne. “None of these things are particularly sexy or flashy. They’re simply features that work, and that can make them difficult to get customers excited about.”

Rebuilding the Xbox brand and launching a new console gives Microsoft an unusual opportunity to make another bold bet on the future, just like it did 25 years ago. But the risks this time around are greater, after more than a decade of trying and failing to catch up to the competition.

“Up against competitors that have firmly established their place in the market, Xbox finds itself in a difficult position,” says Clarke. “Rebuilding the brand will be an expensive endeavor, and the bold ambition of combining the best of console gaming with the openness and flexibility of PC is easier said than done.”

Xbox also needs good games, and after years of studio acquisitions it now has plenty of IP to play with. “Many of those franchises have lost their luster owing to years of mismanagement and potentially a generalized lack of vision,” says Jez Corden, executive editor at Windows Central. “Microsoft from the top will need to be willing to invest here to ensure Xbox’s franchises can not only find new audiences, but simply maintain what they already have.”

Xbox also faces the challenge of RAMageddon’s impact on the hardware industry right now, pushing the prices of components beyond levels that consumers can comfortably afford. “If Xbox is to reclaim any of its former glory, innovating on the costs of the traditional video game ‘console’ will be absolutely paramount, otherwise it’s hard to imagine that it will continue to exist in its current form,” says Corden.

A console projected to cost between $1,200 and $1,800 won’t grow the Xbox ecosystem. It could make more sense to let PC makers build their own Xbox hardware, opening up the platform to a wider range of prices and devices rather than relying on expensive flagship consoles.

Twenty-five years ago, Microsoft built a PC and called it an Xbox. Now it’s preparing to build something that looks increasingly like a PC again, only this time it needs to convince people that it should still be called an Xbox. Microsoft has another opportunity to shake up the console industry, but its biggest challenge might be convincing players that Xbox needs to exist as a console at all.

I’m always keen to hear from readers, so please drop a comment here, or you can reach me at notepad@theverge.com if you want to discuss anything else. If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. #10631. Google’s original Nest Doorbell is broken for many right now

An issue with Google’s original Nest Doorbell has left live view of the video feed broken for many owners, and there’s no word on a fix just yet.

Users are reporting widely in the past few days that Live View video feeds from Nest Doorbells are broken in the Google Home app. The issue is affecting specifically the first-gen Nest Doorbell, which is best known as “Nest Hello,” but Google has been referring to it as “Nest Doorbell (wired, 1st gen).”

The issue has popped up in several Reddit threads in recent weeks, but seems to have spread much more widely in the past few days based on nearly a dozen Google support threads we found.

Some users also report that some first-gen Nest cameras are also not working properly, but we couldn’t find nearly as many reports of this.

Notably, Google did roll out an update to older Nest hardware at the end of October.

I'm so glad to see this article because I've been complaining and complaining to the void about this for weeks now. Google is taking NO responsibility to fix what they obviously broke. Internet searches say that I'm supposed to just upgrade the power supply but that makes no sense.

Interestingly, affected users say that Nest Doorbell notifications on Nest Hub displays are still working properly. Some have deduced that Google’s switch to WebRTC may be the cause of the problem, but it’s hard to say for certain seeing as workarounds based on that theory don’t reliably work.

Outside of generic troubleshooting messages, Google has yet to say anything about a fix, or even confirm there’s an issue. But, based on the number of reports, it’s very obvious there’s a problem on Google’s end.

We’ll update this post as more details come to light but, for now, sound off in the comments below if you’re running into this issue.

#10630. Mass smuggling of up to 1.5 million Nvidia AI GPUs could be the underlying cause of Jensen Huang's departure, analysts claim

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Hardware smuggling to China could be the reason why Jensen had to leave the company he co-founded.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Leading semiconductor research firm SemiAnalysis believes that Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia may have been driven not only by revelations about his personal extramarital relationships, but also by widespread illicit exports of advanced AI accelerators to China. In its latest newsletter, analysts estimate that more than 1.5 gigawatts (GW) worth of restricted Nvidia hardware, which could be equivalent to up to 1.5 million GPUs, has reached the country despite U.S. export controls.

Over 1.5 GW of Nvidia hardware subject to U.S. export restrictions ultimately ended up in China in recent years, according to SemiAnalysis' Accelerator Model, which tracks AI accelerator production and destinations. The estimate excludes processors that Nvidia specifically developed to comply with U.S. export rules, such as the H20 or H800. The firm also claims that the U.S. Department of Commerce has reached a similar conclusion about the scale of unauthorized shipments. Yet, SemiAnalysis does not disclose the exact number of accelerators shipped to China illegally, or their split between Nvidia's product generations.

The scale of the alleged diversions is particularly notable because modern AI accelerators require substantial power and infrastructure. While the report does not explain how it converts accelerator quantities into power figures, it is likely that the company uses package-level nameplate/rated power multiplied by the number of GPUs. Assuming that all of these AI GPUs were Nvidia H200 SXM rated for up to 700W, then China got around 2.142 million of such accelerators, while if all of these processors were B200 SXM rated for up to 1,000W, then we are talking about 1.5 million units. For a mixed deployment, the total would fall between these figures, depending on the proportion of H200 and B200 accelerators. To put these numbers into context, xAI's Colossus One had 220-230,000 Hopper H100 and H200 GPUs, whereas the Colossus 2 has a target of 1.2 million Blackwell B200 and B300 GPUs. That said, 1.5 GW of restricted Nvidia hardware in China represents an enormous amount of computing infrastructure, which is comparable, or even exceeds, the scale of xAI's entire existing Colossus operation.

While SemiAnalysis did not reveal all the cases in which restricted AI accelerators reached the People's Republic of China, it supports its assessment with two well-known cases of alleged GPU smuggling. The first involves Supermicro co-founder Wally Liaw, Taiwan sales executive Steven Chang, and contractor Willy Sun, who were charged in March 2026. According to the indictment, approximately $2.5 billion worth of servers were sold to a company in Southeast Asia, with $510 million worth of equipment subsequently diverted to China and obtained by Alibaba. Prosecutors allege that the defendants used substitute servers and transferred identification labels to conceal the actual destinations of the hardware during audits.

The second case involves nine individuals associated with Nvidia, Albatron, Chief Telecom, Flying Tiger, and Supermicro in Taiwan. The authorities accuse the group of arranging unauthorized exports of 130 Nvidia B300-based servers, 74 of which eventually reached China. The suspects used shell companies, forged documents, and declared Japan as the destination for servers that were actually bound for China, according to prosecutors. Taiwanese authorities discovered the operation during a drug smuggling investigation. Both cases are still before the courts.

Amidst the ongoing fallout and reports into Jensen's Huang's extramarital affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese intelligence asset who was an executive at Megaspeed, one report claims that Ms. Huang was personally responsible for facilitating the smuggling of $7.5 billion of Nvidia AI hardware into China, around 240,000 GPUs, mostly H100 and H200 models.

SemiAnalysis suggests that the scale of these violations may explain why Huang's personal conduct became a major issue at this very point in time. The company's analysts speculate that some members of the U.S. intelligence community sought to remove Jensen Huang because he opposed more aggressive restrictions on China's access to American AI hardware. However, the report provides no direct evidence that intelligence agencies influenced Nvidia's board or that Huang participated in unauthorized exports.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The timing also coincides with congressional efforts to advance the Remote Access Security Act (RASA), which passed the House of Representatives on January 12. The legislation is meant to extend U.S. export-control authority to remote access to advanced AI hardware hosted outside China, including data centers in Europe and the Middle East. SemiAnalysis reports that Nvidia and Oracle have actively opposed the proposal.

The analysts believe that Huang's departure essentially removes an influential opponent of stricter restrictions and accelerates efforts to block Chinese companies from accessing American AI hardware in the cloud. However, SemiAnalysis warns that truly all-inclusive restrictions could encourage Chinese developers to adopt domestic accelerators (something that Jensen Huang has consistently warned about), undermine international AI software collaboration, and ultimately weaken American high-tech companies.

Continued Chinese access to American AI accelerators offers several advantages to the U.S. technology industry, according to SemiAnalysis. Firstly, it generates revenue for American companies such as AMD, Nvidia, and cloud service providers. Secondly, when Chinese companies rent GPU capacity from data centers outside China, U.S. authorities retain the ability to monitor their activities and potentially terminate access if necessary due to national security concerns. Thirdly, prolonged reliance on American hardware reduces incentives for Chinese developers to adopt domestic AI accelerators, which could eventually compete with AMD and Nvidia in international markets.

Another important aspect is the contribution of Chinese developers to the global software ecosystem. On the one hand, Chinese laboratories have developed open-source technologies such as DeepEP, DeepGEMM, and MSA, which improve the efficiency of AI workloads on Nvidia hardware and benefit developers worldwide. In addition, American companies also depend on Chinese engineers, component suppliers, and manufacturers. Therefore, legal curbs that completely restrict Chinese developers' access to American AI accelerators could undermine international cooperation, reduce availability of open-source optimizations, and encourage Chinese companies to develop software specifically for domestic hardware.

On the other hand, access to advanced AI accelerators from AMD or Nvidia enables Chinese laboratories to develop increasingly sophisticated AI models, which will rival leading-edge models developed in America and perhaps create national security risks. To that end, SemiAnalysis acknowledges the need to limit China's access to advanced AI accelerators from U.S.-based companies, as they could, among other things, be used to develop weapons of mass destruction or undermine American national security in different ways. Yet, SA analysts argue that restrictions should focus on the amount and capabilities of hardware available to Chinese entities rather than completely prohibit any access.

SemiAnalysis seems to favor a system in which Chinese developers can rent computing capacity from trusted providers outside China under U.S. oversight. Such an approach could preserve American technological influence and commercial benefits and limit China's ability to acquire advanced AI infrastructure independently.

Microsoft’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, too.

Microsoft’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, too.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

For the 25th anniversary of Xbox, Microsoft is beginning to make good on its paid “next-gen game preservation” promise. Starting today, you can play 25 different original Xbox games on your Windows PC, as long as you have (or are willing to pay) for digital copies.

In July, Microsoft originally launched the idea with just four Xbox games, so 25 is progress, and Microsoft says all these games are now playable with keyboard and mouse.

Plus, Microsoft says it’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, starting with four games: Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic and its sequel, Blinx: The Time Sweeper, and Conker: Live and Reloaded. You can unlock achievements playing them on Xbox One and Xbox Series consoles as well, after a system update. More games should get them in future.

Here’s the full list of Xbox on PC games, with the 21 new additions in bold:

Yes, that’s a lot of Star Wars games, most of which already have a PC version, and few games that were exclusive to Xbox at the time. (But many of those Star Wars games were excellent.)

You also won’t be able to use an original Xbox disc to play these games, I’m afraid, as original Xbox (and Xbox 360) discs aren’t part of Microsoft’s disc-to-digital program; they predate the unique serial number that Microsoft embedded into Xbox One discs. In case you’re wondering, Xbox on PC backward compatibility appears to be powered by an emulator.

Also, most of the new games aren’t currently included with Xbox Game Pass, according to Microsoft; just Blinx, Conker, Crimson Skies, Fusion Frenzy, Morrowind, and Grabbed by the Ghoulies.

Leaked Microsoft documents previously showed us that October 2026 would be the “full launch” of original Xbox games on PC, while Xbox 360 games would have a “gradual” rollout to next-gen consoles between 2027 and 2028, which are when Microsoft is expected to roll out a handheld and a Helix box respectively. The way things are shaping up, the Xbox Helix should play games from every generation of Xbox, even if Helix fundamentally turns out to be a Windows PC.

#10624. Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.

#10623. Build a Hardware Diagnostic Log with Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A device disconnects halfway through a test. The application reports a timeout, the firmware restarts, and the next attempt succeeds. Where should you start?

For developers working with connected devices, browser interfaces, or hardware prototypes, an application error is often only the final symptom. Replacing code without recording the conditions around a failure can leave you chasing the wrong layer.

This tutorial builds a small browser-based diagnostic log. It records measurements, preserves the test conditions, and exports evidence for another developer to review. You need basic HTML and JavaScript knowledge; no framework or connected instrument is required.

1. Define the question before collecting data Consider a hypothetical development board that disconnects when a peripheral starts. Several explanations remain possible: a firmware exception, a transport problem, or a power supply issue. Start with a testable question: “Does the measured supply voltage change when this peripheral starts?” That question tells you what to record and what to keep constant.

Use a known-good board of the same revision where available. Match the firmware, cable, supply, peripheral, and workload. A comparison loses value when several conditions change together.

Record the observation separately from the explanation:

The hypothesis remains provisional. A reading taken after the device restarts does not establish what happened during the failure.

2. Choose evidence that answers the question Application logs, firmware traces, and bench measurements answer different questions. A timeout establishes that an operation did not finish in time; it does not identify a failed component.

For a view of specialist equipment used in phone-board troubleshooting, DIYFIXTOOL’s overview of Chip-Level Diagnostic Tools discusses thermal imaging, BGA X-ray inspection, and inductance testing. Treat it as a vendor-authored introduction to equipment categories; use instrument documentation and board-specific references when interpreting measurements.

The log below deliberately leaves interpretation to the operator. Enter a numeric value, its unit, and the conditions under which it was measured. Do not turn an isolated reading into an automatic “faulty chip” verdict.

For physical measurements, follow the board and instrument documentation and work within your training. This tutorial covers the software record rather than a procedure for probing or repairing a powered device. 3. Build a small, accessible form

Save the following as index.html and serve it through your usual local development server. For example, if Python is installed, run python -m http. server 8000 in its directory, then open http://localhost:8000. 4. Preserve data without hiding failures

The application stores an array of records as JSON. On reload, it parses that JSON and checks the expected field types before rendering it. Parsing alone does not validate the structure of stored data.

SitePoint’s guide to looping through JSON responses provides useful background on working with objects and arrays after parsing JSON. Each table cell uses textContent, so a note containing HTML is displayed as text rather than inserted as markup. The MDN documentation for textContent explains this behavior.

Storage operations sit inside try...catch blocks because browser persistence can fail. The interface keeps new measurements in memory and offers an export when saving fails. It does not claim those measurements will survive a reload. For another practical vanilla JavaScript project, SitePoint’s **Markdown previewer tutorial **explores browser UI construction and persistence.

As **MDN’s localStorage documentation **explains, storage belongs to the document’s origin. Opening the application on a different port may therefore show a different log. This prototype is a single-browser tool, so export important sessions and avoid recording customer identifiers or private device contents.

5. Compare measurements under matching conditions Suppose two hypothetical readings are 3.29 V and 3.08 V. Their difference is −0.21 V. That arithmetic is straightforward; its meaning depends on the rail specification, measurement uncertainty, workload, and timing.

Do not add a universal voltage threshold to the application. A device-specific limit should come from its specification or an established test procedure.

If the failure is brief, a manually entered steady-state measurement may miss it entirely. Record that limitation. The next investigation may require a time-correlated capture rather than another static reading.

When sharing a session, include the firmware revision, board revision, instrument settings, and reproduction steps. A colleague should be able to repeat the comparison without guessing how the reading was obtained. 6. Verify the workflow before relying on it Try adding a decimal reading and a negative reading, then reload the page. Confirm that both values and their units remain intact. Enter a note containing test and check that it appears literally in the table.

Export the JSON and inspect the record count. Then simulate blocked storage and confirm that the interface reports the failure while allowing an export of the current session.

For a larger application, add editable entries, instrument metadata, and explicit baseline records. A shared service would also need access controls, server-side validation, and a policy for retention. These additions should follow the needs of the diagnostic workflow.

Turn a symptom into a reproducible investigation

The finished prototype captures a useful minimum: what was measured, where, when, and under which conditions. Its value comes from preserving evidence that another person can inspect.

When an application error crosses into firmware or hardware, that record helps you choose the next experiment. Repeat the failure, change one variable, and document what the new evidence supports before deciding what to fix.
