#10620. Android tablets are back, but are they for you?

Android tablets have always been a bit of a touchy subject, but it’s abundantly clear that the form factor is making a comeback. But, as someone who’s a fan of the smaller sizes, are you actually using the gargantuan Android tablets that every company seems obsessed with? In other words – are you the customer for the Android tablets these brands are selling?

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

For a while there, Android tablets had hit what felt like a dead end. Bigger smartphones and cooling of the tablet market as a whole, along with the general sentiment that Apple was doing a way better job with the iPad anyway, left the category in a place that felt stagnant – and that’s the nice way of putting it.

But, over the past few years, Android tablets have had a renaissance.

New form factors, big releases from a variety of brands, and flagship-tier devices left and right. Despite Android’s push into foldables and still-huge displays on nearly every smartphone, tablets are very much back. Prior to RAMageddon’s worst impacts, tablet sales were growing for nearly everyone, and Android brands were seeing a larger slice of the pie.

Anyway, this is on my mind right now because I’ve had a lot of Android tablets arrive in my office in the past few weeks, capping off a lot of tablet announcements in the past few months. I just finished up with Redmagic Astra 2, a tablet I think fits the description of “Android’s iPad Mini OLED,” and I’m in the process of moving into tablets from Lenovo and Amazon. Our Jordan Floyd has also been testing out the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra behind the scenes. And if there’s one trend amongst recent tablet releases, it’s that big tablets seem to be the primary focus among a lot of brands.

Take Amazon’s new Google Play-equipped Alexa Tablet lineup, for example.

The Alexa Tablet 12 is the crown jewel of the lineup, packing the most powerful specs and a premium anti-glare display. It’s the only one that gets a “Pro” moniker, while the 11-inch and 8-inch models are noticeable steps down. Unboxing the Alexa Tablet 8 this week, for example, I was immediately reminded this is not a premium device – the screen makes that clear without any hesitation whatsoever. It was a case of whiplash after setting up the Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 with its 4K display.

It’s a weird one for me. I’ve never been a big fan of these super-sized Android tablets (or iPads, for that matter). I can get behind a 10-inch or 11-inch model, but I find myself more at home on the smaller slates. They’re fantastic for travel as an alternative to draining my phone’s battery, while I find myself just reaching for a laptop instead if I want a bigger screen. A big tablet, regardless of specs, can rarely replace a proper laptop for me, so lugging both around just sounds like more trouble than it’s worth.

But, clearly, people are buying these bigger slates. Samsung appears to be seeing enough success with its mammoth Galaxy Tab S Ultra lineup to keep the 14.6-inch tablet around each year while the smaller sizes are now on an every-other-year cycle. Lenovo’s lineup is virtually all underpowered when the screen size goes down, aside from the Legion Tab that’s now $900.

Are you buying these bigger tablets? What are you using them for?

I imagine that most folks opting for a bigger tablet are using these in the place of a laptop. Thinner and lighter, longer battery life, etc. And in cases like the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro and its $550~ price tag, I get the appeal. Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 takes that down a notch for me at $799 – you’re getting into laptop territory there – but it still is a compelling-enough alternative if it fits your needs. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra at $1,400 plus a $200 keyboard, yeah I don’t get that one at all.

Let’s discuss! I want to know if you’re using an Android tablet, what you’re using it for, and what size you actually prefer.

Ladies and gents, we’ve finally got a new flagship in the US. Motorola Signature 27 is launching on November 19 and looks excellent, though its flashy spec sheet won’t come cheap at $1,599.

Finally, something good came out of Googlebook

Googlebook is a disaster, but there’s finally one good thing coming out of it in Henry Cavill’s new “Mustache Protocol” game which, supposedly, was built on Googlebook. I’ll bite my tongue there, but the accompanying video brought a needed bit of joy into my week.

I use an 8" Samsung. Not the best in specs, but fine for browsing and reading books. I had a Nexus 10 years ago and gave it to my sister-in-law as I rarely needed something that big. My wife also uses an 8" Samsung, mostly for reading.

#10618. Xbox owners are getting more customizations for backgrounds and the Guide menu

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox Insiders get to test the new features today.

Xbox Insiders get to test the new features today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft is starting to test new customization options for Xbox consoles today. Xbox Insiders will be able to start testing new rotating backgrounds, new library options for digital games, and the ability to move the Xbox Guide menu to the right-hand side.

“Xbox Insiders can now save favorite dynamic backgrounds and shuffle through them automatically,” says Eden Marie, principal software engineering lead at Xbox. “You can also choose Shuffle at the start of any background category. Have your background change each time you sign in, or set it to change hourly, daily, or weekly.”

Microsoft is also improving how you organize disc-to-digital games on an Xbox console. Xbox Insiders who have claimed a digital license from a disc-based game will be able to see these titles in a separate category in the library section. You’ll also be able to hide the digital license icon on game tiles.

Finally, Xbox Insiders will be able to choose whether the Guide menu appears on the left or right side of a screen. “Alpha Skip-Ahead members can try them first,” says Marie. “We’ll bring them to more Xbox Insiders and players over time.”

#10617. Android Auto cutting off older devices, now requires Android 11 or newer

Google has officially dropped support for some older Android versions in Android Auto, now requiring Android 11 or newer to function.

On a support page, Google says that Android 11.0 or newer is now required to use Android Auto:

An Android phone with Android 11.0 (Red Velvet Cake) or higher and an active data plan.

Previously, Google supported Android 9 and Android 10 for the in-car experience, a minimum requirement that itself was raised back in 2024 (but not enforced until 2025).

Some drivers started seeing a “Phone will no longer be supported” message in October 2026, but Google only updated its documentation this week. Android Auto 18.1, the latest app update, still supports Android 10.

The outage has lasted nearly a full 24 hours.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Japanese workers dealing with the impacts of Microsoft's ongoing 365 outage have reportedly taken to analog measures, including using fax machines to deal with the ongoing blackout, the Japan Times reports. An outage that has now lasted nearly 24 hours was still going on Friday when employees turned up to work.

The downtime has meant businesses reliant on Microsoft 365 are unable to use Outlook, Teams, and Copilot, with workplaces hit with error messages and blank screens, forced to reschedule meetings or delay work. For some, the outage has proven a respite from Japan's notoriously workaholic culture. "I couldn’t open Outlook and panicked … I got in touch with my boss and was told to stay home," one person reported on X, while another remarked that the only task they could carry out at work was to clean the coffee machine in the production room.

But while some employees and companies are throwing in the towel and calling it an early weekend, others are taking a novel, more analog approach. "Microsoft’s server glitch has made it impossible to get any work done since this morning. I guess phones and fax machines really are unbeatable. … We’re going full analog!" one user claimed. The report cites another employee who confirmed that their company was switching to fax machines to send the regular updates the company normally sends out as PDFs. "Analog is strong," they rejoiced.

Some 78% of Japanese companies rely on Microsoft for their digital infrastructure. The outage began on Thursday, with mass disruption to Outlook, Teams, Copilot, OneDrive, and Microsoft 365 as a whole. It's a saga impacting entire sectors, including UK health care, businesses, travel, and transport.

Microsoft's latest update said it was seeing "encouraging recovery progress" in Europe, while the situation in America was improving, but warned some users may still experience intermittent issues. The company has also denied rumblings that the outage was caused by a cybersecurity breach. The outage has even caused some companies, such as Switzerland's luxury Richemont (Cartier) outfit, to delay giving their latest earnings report.

#10614. Meta’s Horizon+ looks to become Meta VR+ and include 3D movies

Meta Horizon+ is rumored to be changing its name to Meta VR+ and may start including movies in addition to games with the subscription price.

Meta’s quest (ahem) to distance itself from the Horizon era is in full swing, and the latest rumor says it’s time for the company’s Game Pass-like subscription to change its brand name. VR leaker Nori posted a screenshot on X showing a handful of 3D movies coming to the Meta VR Plus service. Considering there is no such Meta VR Plus service currently, the obvious conclusion is that Meta Horizon+ is being renamed once again.

Meta Horizon+ currently includes over 100 games with its $7.99 per month subscription and is similar to Microsoft’s Xbox Game Pass service in many ways. Unironically, Meta Horizon+ also includes the basic Xbox Game Pass tier with over 50 Xbox games playable via the Game Pass cloud. Meta offers a 3-month trial with every new Meta Quest purchase.

The new Meta VR+ service is purported to include 3D movies as well, although we don’t yet know if this will be included in the regular $7.99 per month rate, or if Meta will be introducing additional content tiers, as services like Netflix, Disney+, Xbox Game Pass, and others do. Based on the screenshot, these will be big-name movies and are likely positioned to enhance the value of the upcoming $1,300 Meta VR Glasses, although all the same content will work on existing Meta Quest 3 units.

Meta has been slowly distancing itself from the “Horizon” name ever since the beginning of this year, when its VR division got a big reset. In a February blog post, Samantha Ryan, the VP of Content at Meta’s Reality Labs, spoke about the changes to the company’s strategy, including the loss of most in-house VR gaming studios. Since then, several prominent VR developers from Meta have gone on to create independent studios, including the super-popular Supernatural exercise app.

Meta then decoupled the unpopular Horizon Metaverse from its headsets, moving it to mobile-only, and has been removing the “Horizon” name from just about everything since then. Meta Horizon+ looks to be the latest big change, as the company moves toward “Meta VR” branding, which is clearer and separates its VR division from mobile and smart glasses efforts.

#10613. Humble Bundle redesign removes two-factor verification from settings

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A new look for the Humble Bundle storefront is missing an important security feature that was previously there.

A new look for the Humble Bundle storefront is missing an important security feature that was previously there.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Humble Bundle storefront for buying games and other software recently got a new look, but on the settings page, it no longer offers a way to add or update two-factor verification methods, as reported by Kotaku. PC Gamer also points out that there are Humble Bundle support pages discussing two-factor verification, and the writer for the publication said that they had set up two-factor verification on the old version of the site.

However, it does appear that, at least as of now, there is some form of two-factor verification. When I tried logging into my Humble Bundle account while writing this article, I was prompted to check my email for an access code before I could access my account. While logged in, I was able to update my password, but I didn’t have to enter my old password to be able to do so.

For what it’s worth, my passwords app also shows that I set up a rotating two-factor code for Humble Bundle at some point. I was not asked to input that at any part of my login process today.

Humble Bundle owner IGN Entertainment and IGN parent company Ziff Davis didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment.

#10611. Google Workspace vs. Microsoft 365 vs. Proton Workspace: Which Is Best for Developers and Freelancers?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Think about what's sitting in your inbox and Drive right now. Client contracts and invoices. Project specs under NDA. Staging credentials a client emailed you. That API key or password you meant to delete.

For most developers and freelancers, all of it lives in Google Workspace or Microsoft 365. Both are excellent tools, and both encrypt your data in transit and at rest. But in their standard setup, the provider holds the encryption keys, which means your provider (and the integrated apps and AI features connected to it) can access what's inside.

Proton Workspace takes a different approach: the same everyday tools, with encryption designed so that not even Proton can read your data.

Here's how the three compare, what each does best, and which makes the most sense for how you work.

Google and Microsoft are built around ecosystem integration. Proton is built around keeping your data yours, without sacrificing productivity tools.

Google Workspace is the go-to suite for startups, agencies and freelancers, and for good reason. Gmail, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Calendar and Meet work together seamlessly, and several people can edit the same file in real time without emailing versions back and forth.

Google encrypts data at rest and in transit, and offers client-side encryption on eligible editions. But that extra layer requires higher-tier plans and configuration, so in the default experience Google holds the keys. A password manager and VPN aren't part of the suite.

Best for: Teams whose top priorities are real-time collaboration and third-party integrations.

If your work runs on Word, Excel and Outlook, Microsoft 365 needs little introduction. Business Standard includes full desktop apps, and Excel remains the benchmark for heavy spreadsheet work.

That breadth comes with complexity. Licensing, permissions and device management can eat into time a small team or solo developer would rather spend shipping. And like Google, Microsoft's everyday productivity experience isn't built around end-to-end encryption, while Copilot and advanced security features add cost.

Best for: Businesses built on desktop Office apps and established Microsoft workflows.

Everything is managed from one admin console, with one bill.

This is the core difference. Proton uses zero-access encryption: your data is encrypted before it reaches Proton's servers, and Proton never holds the keys. Proton can't read your data. Not won't. Can't.

The honest trade-offs: Proton Docs and Sheets cover core editing and collaboration, not every advanced Excel feature. There's no presentation app yet, and the third-party integration ecosystem is smaller than Google's or Microsoft's.

US prices for comparable plans with annual billing, excluding taxes:

The headline prices are close. The real cost isn't:

Proton Workspace covers business email, 1 TB of encrypted storage, docs, sheets, video calls, a password manager and a VPN. That's one bill instead of three.

Need more? Workspace Premium and adds 3 TB of storage per user, 20 custom domains, meetings for up to 250 participants, Lumo private AI and email data retention policies for only $7 more per month. Both plans include a 14-day free trial, and annual plans are currently up to 20% off.

Choose Google Workspace if your team lives in real-time collaboration and relies heavily on Google's integration ecosystem.

Choose Microsoft 365 if your work depends on desktop Excel, PowerPoint and established Microsoft workflows.

Less than you might expect. Proton's Easy Switch tool imports email, contacts and calendars from Google Workspace and other providers, and you keep your custom domain, so clients won't notice a thing. Smaller teams can migrate on their own, and Proton's business team supports larger moves.

Moving from Microsoft 365? Check the migration options for each data type rather than assuming every workflow transfers automatically.

Is Proton Workspace a good alternative to Google Workspace for developers?

Can Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 read my email and files?

By default, yes. Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 hold the encryption keys, and their default terms give them access to your data.

No. Zero-access encryption means Proton never holds the keys to your stored data. Emails between Proton users are end-to-end encrypted automatically, and you can send password-protected encrypted emails to anyone.

Yes. Proton's apps are open source and independently audited.

Can I use Proton Mail with my existing email client?

Yes. Proton Mail Bridge connects Outlook, Apple Mail and Thunderbird.

Does Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 use my data to train AI?

It can. If your plan allows Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 to hold the encryption keys, they can access your data and use it to train their integrated AI models.

No. Proton can't read your data, so it can't use it for AI training.

Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 are mature, capable platforms, and if your work depends on advanced Excel or a deep web of Google integrations, there's a good case for using them.

But if you're a freelancer or a developer or running a small team, Proton Workspace is the stronger choice. You get the same core tools for less money, a password manager and VPN you'd otherwise pay for separately, and encryption that keeps client data, code and credentials readable only by you.

You don't have to take that on faith, either. The code is open source, and the trial is free.

#10610. Pixel 10a drops to $449, the best deal you’ll find after Google’s price hike

Google may have hiked the price of the Pixel 10a in recent months, but a limited-time deal is the best price you’ll find at $450.

Pixel 10a initially launched for $499 but, like everything else in the chaos of RAMageddon, that price didn’t stick around for long. Google raised the price of Pixel 10a at the start of October, taking the starting price up to $599, leaving most discounts in the past month still higher than the original retail price.

But, right now, you can get a Pixel 10a for $449 from Woot, a rare deal that cuts the price below Google’s original MSRP.

The discount isn’t an all-time low, but it’s still well below any other Pixel 10a deal you’ll find in the near future, and the Pixel 10a is already one of the best deals in mid-range smartphones even at its higher price point. With 7 years of updates, a clean and fast Android build, reliable cameras, solid battery life, and more, it’s just hard to beat.

The one “catch” in this offer is that you’re limited in choices – 128GB in “Obsidian” black is it.

“If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss.”

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has reportedly called himself “a piece of sh**" as he left the company he built over 33 years. Bloomberg reports that the co-founder, known for his legendary energy and outgoing demeanor, surrendered and looked “broken and shattered” during the board meeting where he resigned from the AI hardware firm after admitting to at least one extra-marital affair, including one with a suspected Chinese spy. But despite his resignation and the controversies surrounding him, it seems that Nvidia employees are still rallying behind him, with thousands of comments on the company’s internal “suggestion box” showing support for the disgraced leader, according to Wired.

“If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” one commenter said, while another one wrote, “We are all humans and can make mistakes. Jensen did so many good things and he needs a second chance.” An Nvidia staffer told Wired privately, “Nvidia is Nvidia because of Jensen,” even as another employee sent a company-wide email demanding transparency into his resignation. “I stand by Jensen. Not because I believe any person is infallible, but because I refuse to believe that standing for fairness must mean standing at a distance from someone whose leadership has meant so much to us.” They also added, “If America fails the man who helped build its future, then this world does not deserve Jensen Huang.”

According to the report, internal calls for Huang's reinstatement have drawn more than 1,600 comments on Nvidia's internal forums, "the majority in full support of reinstalling him." The company has more than 42,000 employees worldwide.

Rumors about Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy, being close to Jensen Huang first circulated in late October, with Nvidia vehemently denying there was any impropriety. “Megaspeed has been no different than many other regional cloud customers. Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” an Nvidia spokesperson told Tom's Hardware. However, things took a turn for the worse for the Nvidia CEO when reports came out that he actually had an affair with Ms. Huang.

Nvidia General Counsel and Secretary of the Board Tim Teter confronted Jensen at his house when this came out, after which he confessed. The founder soon released a statement admitting to and apologizing for his actions, while the company’s board formed an independent committee to investigate. However, another scandal broke out revealing that the former Nvidia chief had another affair — this time with a subordinate. This was apparently the last straw for the board, and the Nvidia co-founder was forced to resign soon after.

Reports say that Jensen Huang was a hands-on leader, with over 60 executives at the company reporting directly to him, and that he was known to respond to emails quickly, whether it was day or night. But, more importantly, he was the face of the company who was particularly loved by many people, giving rise to the term “Jensanity” in his home country of Taiwan. Regardless of his status as a billionaire, people still find him trying out street food, eating at fast food chains, and even honoring his humble beginnings as a busboy at Denny’s.

Aside from these latest scandals, many gamers castigate Huang for abandoning the core market that supported Nvidia before it became an AI giant, with consumer graphics cards now drastically more expensive than ever before. He was also often criticized for his all-out push on artificial intelligence and stance on selling chips to China. Still, those issues did not detract from his aura, with his trademark leather jacket raising nearly $1 million at a charity auction. It remains to be seen what will happen to Jensen after his monumental fall from grace, with SpaceX and Tesla mogul Elon Musk noting it would be an honor to hire Huang.

#10606. Samsung’s paid Galaxy AI plan leaks for $5/month, but will you pay?

Samsung has long left the idea of paid Galaxy AI features on the table for Galaxy devices, and now there’s finally some evidence that it’s coming and could cost $5/month.

Since the suite was introduced, Samsung has been offering Galaxy AI features for free, with an asterisk. Originally, they were only free through “the end of 2025,” but Samsung kept saying that it had no plans to start charging and, so far, has not made any announcements.

SammyGuru spotted evidence in One UI 9.5 for a new “Galaxy AI Premium” subscription. Based on strings, Samsung would be charging $4.99/month for its Galaxy AI features, though the good news is that existing features remain free.

This includes Now Brief, Browsing assist, Creative Studio, Samsung Keyboard AI features, and more.

Samsung’s Galaxy AI Premium plan would instead focus on adding new features, including:

No. I'm not interested in it. I'll do without any of that.

Samsung would also boost usage limits in this paid plan, with the sign-up screen somewhat implying that usage limits on free features could be a part of this. On top of a monthly subscription, users would also be able to buy “credit packs” that unlock additional usage for a one-time fee.

Pricing for the monthly plan would start at $4.99/month or $47.99/year.

But the big question here is whether or not you’d actually pay for it. With Samsung having offered Galaxy AI features for free for a couple of years now, it’s certainly a major change on the company’s part. What do you think?

#10604. Xbox announces Wolfenstein 3

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox CEO Asha Sharma revealed a new entry in the Wolfenstein series with a brief tease.

Xbox CEO Asha Sharma revealed a new entry in the Wolfenstein series with a brief tease.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s been nearly a decade since Bethesda’s MachineGames released Wolfenstein II: The New Colossus in 2017, and now Xbox is ready to announce a sequel from the studio. Wolfenstein 3 was officially announced today, just days after Bethesda also confirmed that the next Elder Scrolls game will be called The Elder Scrolls VI: Sentinel.

In a post, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma announced the name and a photo of a script. “Looking forward to sharing more,” she said. MachineGames also announced the game in a post, saying the game is “in production” and sharing a photo from a motion-capture session. IGN reports that Brian Bloom (who plays protagonist William Joseph “BJ” Blazkowicz) and Debra Wilson (who plays Grace Walker) are reprising their roles, and they appear to be the featured actors in the photo.

The last mainline entry, Wolfenstein II: The New Colossus, launched in 2017 and continued the story of Blazkowicz in an alternate 1960s where Nazi Germany had won the Second World War. Wolfenstein: Youngblood also debuted in 2019 as a co-op spinoff, starring Blazkowicz’s twin daughters.

#10603. Walmart raises prices on its entire Google TV streamer lineup

Walmart’s ultra-cheap Google TV streamers were never going to remain cheap in the age of RAMageddon, and now we’re seeing the second big price hike of the year with the whole Onn Google TV lineup seeing higher prices today.

Quietly rolled out over the past few days, Walmart has raised the price of its Google TV streaming devices by $10 a piece. The lineup now starts at about $40, up from just $15 prior to this year. Walmart’s streamers started getting higher prices back in March.

The price hikes have hit everything from the base 1080p streaming stick up to the Onn 4K Pro that rivals Google’s own TV Streamer.

Walmart’s price hikes still leave every device in its lineup as a great deal in the broader scheme of things, but it’s a huge increase compared to past generations.

For the time being, Walmart’s Onn-branded Google TV sets remain at their launch price, starting at around $250.

#10598. Prompt Management in TypeScript: LangSmith vs Git vs 3 Open-Source Registries

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every prompt registry we tested can change a prompt in a running TypeScript app, roll it back, and give production and staging different versions. LangSmith's prompt hub, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore all passed those three tests. The real differences show up elsewhere: which template syntax each one accepts, how many npm packages it pulls in, how long a change takes to reach the app, and what happens when the registry is down.

We tested all of this with one small app and one prompt, which has a condition and a loop. Git files served as the baseline. Every number below comes from a recorded run.

Most teams start with prompts in code: a template string, or a .txt file next to the code that uses it. That works until a product manager wants to change one sentence, and the change has to wait for a full release.

A prompt registry moves the text out of the deploy. The app fetches the prompt by name and by a label such as production. You change which version the label points to, and the app picks up the new text without a deploy.

LangSmith's prompt hub is the best-known registry, but it is not the only one. Several of the LangSmith alternatives also manage prompts, and three of them are open source and self-hostable: Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. We covered how those tools handle tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent. This article covers prompts only.

Every tool got the same prompt, support-reply. Its template takes three variables: a customer name, a plan, and a list of help articles. It has one condition: a priority line appears only for premium customers. It also has one loop: one line per help article. In Jinja2 syntax, the template looks like this:

One help article contains the text Settings > Export. We included it on purpose, to catch any tool that HTML-escapes > into >.

Every tool then ran the same sequence from a TypeScript script:

Each tool used its own documented TypeScript SDK. The four registries passed all six steps. The rest of this article covers how each one got there, and what it cost.

Keeping prompts in the repository costs almost nothing. The template sits in prompts/support-reply.njk, and nunjucks, the JavaScript port of Jinja2, renders it:

The autoescape: false line matters. Our first version called nunjucks.renderString() directly, and the test failed. The default environment HTML-escapes its output, so Settings > Export reached the model as Settings > Export. Escaping is correct for web pages and wrong for prompts.

Git gives you full Jinja2, code review on every prompt change, and history in the same place as the code. What it can't do is change a prompt in a running app. Every edit, and every rollback, waits for a deploy.

LangSmith stores a prompt as a serialized LangChain object (ChatPromptTemplate), not as plain text. LangSmith's own documentation says you must use the LangChain npm package to pull prompts in TypeScript (Manage prompts programmatically). So a plain TypeScript app that uses the openai package still needs langchain and @langchain/core:

Those three packages installed 23 packages and 70 MB. Most of the size is @langchain/* (27 MB) and js-tiktoken (22 MB).

Templates. In TypeScript, LangChain accepts f-string and mustache templates. Mustache sections ({{#docs}}…{{/docs}}) handled our loop. Mustache has no ==, though, so the premium check became an is_premium boolean computed in code. We also tried templateFormat: "jinja2", which LangChain's Python package supports. In TypeScript it threw DEFAULT_PARSER_MAPPING[templateFormat] is not a function.

Labels and rollback. production and staging are reserved tags, and the documentation moves them in the UI. The langsmith JavaScript client has no public method that moves a tag. Our script called the same REST route the SDK uses internally (POST /api/v1/repos/-//tags). It worked, but it is not a documented API.

Caching. The SDK caches prompts for 300 seconds by default. In our test, a running app saw the new version 300 seconds after we moved the tag.

Langfuse's TypeScript client is one package, @langfuse/client. It installed 5 packages and 8 MB:

Creating a version, moving a label and reading by label are all public SDK calls: prompt.create, prompt.update and prompt.get (Get started with prompt management).

Templates. The documentation shows only {{variable}} substitution. But compile() calls the mustache library, so sections worked for our loop. The type signature accepts only string values, so passing a list needed a cast. The Langfuse UI colours the section tags red, and it lists title and body as the prompt's variables, not docs or is_premium. We would not depend on behaviour the documentation doesn't promise. When we sent the Jinja2 version instead, Langfuse returned the {% if %} and {% for %} tags unchanged, with no error. The model would have received them as text.

Caching. The cache time is 60 seconds by default (Caching). Our app saw the change after 62 seconds. Langfuse is also the only client with a fallback option: a prompt string the SDK uses when it cannot reach the server on a cold start.

Phoenix's TypeScript client fetches a prompt by name and tag, and toSDK() converts it into the message format the openai package expects. Phoenix templates only replace variables, so the condition and the loop have to run in your code before rendering:

That is 16 lines, against 6 to 8 for the other registries. All the extra lines are template logic that now lives in the app.

Templates. Phoenix offers MUSTACHE, F_STRING and NONE formats (Prompts reference). Even in MUSTACHE mode, though, rendering is a plain replacement of {{name}}. When we sent mustache sections, they came back unchanged, with no error.

Errors. getPrompt() catches every error, logs it and returns null. You can see this in the client source, src/utils/getPromptBySelector.ts. A missing prompt and an unreachable server look the same to your code, which is why the snippet checks for null.

Size and caching. The client installed 57 packages and 90 MB. Most of that is OpenTelemetry, which comes in through its @arizeai/phoenix-otel dependency. The client has no prompt cache, so every render is a network call. That is also why a label change showed up immediately: 0 seconds in our test.

AcruxCore stores Jinja2 templates and renders them on the server with nunjucks. The SDK sends the variables and gets back finished messages:

The template from the test section works as it is, condition and loop included, with no is_premium flag computed in code. A label is called an alias here, and promoteAlias() moves it (Manage prompts via the SDK). The SDK installed 3 packages and 12 MB. Most of the 12 MB is one OpenTelemetry package, @opentelemetry/semantic-conventions.

Templates. Jinja2 only. When we committed the mustache version, AcruxCore rejected it at commit time with Template parse error: unexpected token: #. That is a clear error, but it also means that moving mustache prompts from LangSmith or Langfuse requires rewriting them.

We sent the same two templates, a Jinja2 one and a mustache one, to each tool's own renderer:

The rows that matter most are the ones where the tags were left in the output, because those failures produce no error. The model receives raw template syntax, and the only symptom is a strange answer. Whatever tool you pick, test one prompt with logic in it before you move the rest.

Putting logic in the template is not automatically better. It lets someone change the condition without a deploy. It also lets them break the condition without a deploy. Keeping logic in code, as Phoenix forces you to do, puts every branch under code review and in your tests.

A registry is a new runtime dependency, so we tested an outage. The app reached each registry through a local proxy, and we switched the proxy off. A separate process then started while the registry was still unreachable.

LangSmith, Langfuse and AcruxCore keep serving the last good version while the registry is down, even after the cache time has passed. The risk is a cold start: a new container or a serverless function that boots during the outage. Langfuse's fallback option is the built-in answer to that. With the other tools, keep a copy of each prompt in your code as a fallback, which brings back part of the Git setup.

Keep prompts in Git if engineers make every prompt change and your deploys are quick. Set autoescape: false, and you get Jinja2, reviews and history for free.

Use LangSmith's prompt hub if you already use LangChain, or already trace with LangSmith. If your app doesn't use LangChain, budget for the extra dependency and for the tag moves that only the UI supports.

Use Langfuse if you want a small client, a local compile(), and a fallback for cold starts. Keep to {{variable}} substitution unless you are comfortable relying on mustache behaviour that the documentation doesn't promise.

Use Phoenix if you already use it for tracing, or if you prefer all template logic in code. Remember the null check, and that every render is a network call.

Use AcruxCore if you want real conditions and loops in the template and rendering on the server. Expect one network call per new set of variables, and a personal API key for writes.

Before you choose, run one of your own prompts with real logic in it through the tool's SDK, not through its playground. Two of the four registries we tested failed on template syntax without any error, and an SDK run is where you would see that.

#10597. Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When Apple CEO John Ternus announced the first touchscreen Mac last month, the biggest reaction in the room wasn’t for the PROLED MacBook Pro’s OLED touchscreen or the thinner and lighter chassis; it was for the illuminated lid logo. It’s a throwback to the light-up logos on the old MacBooks, which Apple started phasing out in 2015, and which were completely gone by the 2018 MacBook Air redesign. It’s a nostalgia play, but boy did it work. It’s fun!

Just like in the old days, the new logo’s brightness is linked to the screen’s: The brighter or dimmer the screen, the brighter or dimmer the logo. But the light-up logo on the old MacBooks was illuminated by the screen’s backlight, and an OLED display doesn’t have one. Thanks to iFixit, we now know it’s lit by four white LEDs and synced with the screen’s brightness by software. But that means Apple could have used RGB LEDs.

Red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, violet, or anything in between? On the lid of your new MacBook Pro? Why not? After all, Apple insists it’s still serious about gaming, and nothing says “serious about gaming” like RGB lighting.

I was secretly hoping users would be able to customize the illuminated lid cutout much like the Xbox logo on Microsoft’s Elite Series 2 controllers. But alas, no luck. You can turn off the illumination with some Terminal commands or take control of it with a third-party app. But when it’s lit, it’s only lit with white light. And iFixit’s teardown also showed the difficulty of accessing those LEDs (the screen delaminated and broke in the process). So we’re unlikely to see LED RGB mods for it unless someone’s brave enough to void their warranty and risk a pricey screen repair on their $3,000 laptop.

The most fun you can have with the Apple lid logo is to install a free third-party app like LogoLight and have it pulse, breathe, match the beat of any music you’re playing, or flash some Morse code. There’s some fun there, but if you combined these features with a splash of color we’d really be cooking.

MacBooks aren’t gaming laptops, so RGB lighting may seem like a mismatch, but multicolor LEDs could have been a cool Easter egg. Nobody thought Apple would put a touchscreen on a MacBook either, and the iPhone Duo and HomeView also show Apple is in a new age of experimentation during the early Ternus era. Maybe if the light-up lid logo ever comes to future MacBook Airs or a MacBook Neo, Apple could cut loose a little.

Does it mean, illegal activities won't happen again?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Malaysia and Singapore have intensified scrutiny of AI hardware shipments amid concerns about the potential circumvention of U.S. export restrictions on China. Malaysia has launched an investigation into whether AI hardware imported by a subsidiary of the ill-fated Megaspeed was subsequently exported to other countries, reports Reuters. Meanwhile, Singapore has charged another executive over questionable purchases of 917 servers that may contain Nvidia accelerators, according to The Straits Times. The news follows the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang over claims he had multiple extra-marital affairs, including with a possible Chinese intelligence asset who facilitated GPU smuggling into China. Analysts speculate that systematic chip smuggling may be the reason behind his departure.

Malaysia's Ministry of Investment, Trade and Industry is examining whether Speedmatrix, the Malaysian subsidiary of Singapore-based Megaspeed, re-exported any of the AI processors and servers it had imported for use in local data centers. Apparently, after months of investigating, authorities have yet to find out whether any such shipments occurred and, if they did, whether they complied with Malaysian laws. Malaysia introduced restrictions on the export, transit, and trans-shipment of advanced U.S.-developed AI accelerators in July 2025, so it now requires companies to obtain appropriate permits.

The Malaysian ministry said it was cooperating with customs authorities as well as officials in Singapore and the U.S., and could introduce additional restrictions. Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim said there was no evidence that Megaspeed-related AI hardware had left Malaysia, but he promised to cooperate with international investigations.

Speaking of Megaspeed, the company said on November 12 that it had hired a U.S. law firm to conduct a comprehensive inventory of its AI GPUs and independently assess its compliance with U.S. export-control regulations, reports DigiTimes. The company also confirmed that it had cooperated with inspections by the U.S. Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) and was prepared to cooperate with other U.S. government agencies if necessary.

James Tan Yong Pong, chief executive of Megaspeed, stressed that compliance with U.S. export rules as well as transparent operations were always a priority for the company. However, Megaspeed did not disclose how many GPUs it owns and which GPU models it has, what BIS examined during its inspections, or whether the authorities had identified any violations.

Meanwhile, Singaporean authorities have brought 41 criminal charges against Shi Bingzhe, a 34-year-old Chinese citizen who served as director of Superphi and chief operating officer of NJ Global Financial. The two companies purchased 917 servers containing restricted Nvidia hardware from Aperia International in 41 transactions between May 2024 and February 2025, according to prosecutors. Authorities allege that the companies had reason to believe the servers were linked to criminal activity by Aperia International. Superphi now faces six charges, while NJ Global Financial faces 35.

The latest charges expand a case that now involves five individuals and six companies. Four firms — Aperia International, A-Speed Infotech, Aperia Cloud Services (II), and Luxuriate Your Life — were charged in July over allegedly making false claims when purchasing servers. Meanwhile, the exact nature of those claims was not disclosed in the report. Authorities have frozen the disposal of a property worth approximately S$55 million and confiscated around S$1 million from bank accounts. Shi faces up to 10 years in prison and a fine of S$500,000 per charge if convicted. His next court appearance is scheduled for January 4, 2027.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The two cases largely highlight the significance of Malaysia and Singapore in the supply chain of restricted AI hardware to places where it should not be, such as China. Singapore's investigation focuses on alleged fraud and transactions involving proceeds of criminal activity, whereas Malaysia seeks to find out whether imported AI equipment was subsequently shipped abroad in violation of local regulations.

Neither investigation has found that the hardware ultimately reached China, but both illustrate difficulties that authorities face as they attempt to enforce restrictions on advanced AI hardware across international supply chains.

Some of the specs of the chip are still fuzzy.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Update 11/13/26 at 12:10pm ET: Intel has confirmed the 260K Plus as a "limited-availability" SKU and says it's not currently available as a boxed retail chip. Specs should go live on Intel's website within the next few days.

A new Intel Arrow Lake SKU is listed for sale in multiple prebuilt PCs on Newegg, despite no official acknowledgement of the model from Intel. The Core Ultra 7 260K is a downclocked version of the Core Ultra 7 265K from Intel's main Arrow Lake stack, not the Arrow Lake Refresh stack that's infiltrated our list of the best CPUs for gaming. Outside of a few areas on Newegg's website, you'll find no mention of the CPU elsewhere online (including Intel's official Core Ultra Series 2 specs page).

Although retailer-exclusive and late-generation variants are common from AMD and Intel — we recently reviewed the Ryzen 5 5500F, for instance — they almost never fly completely under the radar, as is the case with the Core Ultra 7 260K. The chip itself is identical to the Core Ultra 7 265K based on the specs we know, fitted with 20 cores, eight of which are P-cores. The 260K tops out at 5.4 GHz, however, while the 265K reaches 5.5 GHz.

Otherwise, the specs are all the same. It comes with the same 125W TDP, same 30 MB of L3 cache, and same support for up to 256 GB of memory running at DDR5-6400. We only know of these specs from an image shown on the two ABS prebuilt listings available with the 260K. It's available in the ABS Kaze II paired with an RTX 5060, as well as the Flux II Aqua with an RTX 5070.

The image is below, captured hastily, as you can see, from a Microsoft Teams call. Again, we weren't able to find any information from Intel about the chip outside of this slide.

One critical detail this slide confirms is that the Core Ultra 7 260K belongs to the Arrow Lake family, not the range of Arrow Lake Refresh processors like the Core Ultra 7 270K Plus. It tops out at DDR5-6400, while Arrow Lake Refresh climbs up to DDR5-7200. That means the chip doesn't have access to the hardware-level enhancements available in Arrow Lake Refresh, nor the software-based Intel Binary Optimization Tool that boosts performance in select games.

Although that's enough to categorize the 260K, there are some details that are unclear. In the two PCs it's available in, the chip is referred to as the 260K across the full product page except in Newegg's option selector (pictured below), where it's referred to as the U7-260KF. Intel's KF models are unlocked for overclocking but lack integrated graphics.

Additionally, the slide from Intel referenced above shows the chip boosting up to 5.4 GHz, but the product page lists the chip at 5.2 GHz for maximum boost clock.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Newegg has told us that the chip is exclusive to the retailer and will only be sold through its in-house ABS prebuilts. ABS machines are normally sold with components Newegg already sells piecemeal, offering a convenient way to get a full rig with parts you can identify. The ABS Flux II Aqua, which is available with the chip, is on sale at Newegg now for $300 off.

The ABS Flux II Aqua gaming PC comes with an Intel CPU, an Nvidia GeForce GeForce RTX 5070 graphics card, a 1TB NVMe SSD with Gen 4 speeds, and 32GB of DDR5-6000 RAM. This is a 1440p gaming powerhouse with the potential for 4K.

We've reached out to Intel to get more details about the Core Ultra 7 260K, and we'll update this story when we hear back (see update log above). Although the specs suggest the 260K should perform almost identically to the 265K, we haven't had a chance to test the CPU ourselves. And, as you can see from the rankings in our CPU benchmark hierarchy, we recommend most Intel shoppers move toward an Arrow Lake Refresh SKU.

#10588. Technical Aspects of AI-Generated Video Assets for Web Design

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated videos are becoming an important part of modern web design. Designers can now create animated backgrounds, product demonstrations, hero section videos, and visual storytelling elements without relying entirely on traditional video production. However, generating a visually appealing video is only one part of the process. To use AI-generated videos effectively on a website, developers and designers must also understand video formats, visual quality, file optimization, and website performance.

These technical factors directly influence how videos appear across devices, how quickly pages load, and how smoothly visitors interact with a website.

Video format selection is one of the first technical decisions when preparing AI-generated assets for the web. Different formats offer different levels of compression, compatibility, and visual quality.

For most websites, MP4 with H.264 remains a dependable default. WebM or AV1 can be offered as additional formats where appropriate.

Developers should also consider browser compatibility before selecting a format. Providing multiple sources allows browsers to choose a supported option.

AI-generated videos can sometimes contain visual inconsistencies, unnatural movements, distorted objects, or changes in lighting between frames. These issues become more noticeable when videos are displayed in large website sections.

Resolution is an important factor in maintaining visual quality. Common resolutions include:

However, higher resolution does not automatically mean better website performance or user experience. A 4K video may look impressive, but loading it unnecessarily can increase bandwidth consumption.

Frame rate also affects the viewing experience. Many website videos work well at 24 or 30 frames per second. Higher frame rates may be useful for fast-moving scenes, but they also increase the amount of visual data that needs to be processed.

Designers should review AI-generated footage before publishing it. Checking transitions, motion consistency, sharpness, and color accuracy helps prevent distracting visual problems.

Large video files can significantly increase website loading times. This is especially important for websites with video backgrounds, animated landing pages, and multiple visual sections.

Video optimization involves reducing file size without making the footage noticeably worse.

Some practical optimization techniques include:

Compression: Video compression reduces unnecessary data while preserving important visual details. Developers can use encoding tools such as FFmpeg to adjust bitrate, resolution, and codec settings.

Bitrate control: Bitrate determines how much data is used to represent each second of video. A higher bitrate generally preserves more detail but increases file size. Selecting an appropriate bitrate depends on the video's resolution, movement, and intended display size.

Trimming unnecessary footage: Website videos rarely need to be several minutes long. Removing unused sections helps reduce file size and keeps visual content focused.

Removing unnecessary audio: Background videos that do not require sound should be exported without an audio track. This reduces unnecessary data and supports a cleaner user experience.

Responsive video delivery: Websites should serve appropriately sized assets for different screen sizes. A smaller video may be sufficient for mobile users, while desktop visitors can receive a higher-resolution version.

These techniques help developers balance visual quality with efficient content delivery.

Even a well-optimized video can affect website performance if it is implemented incorrectly. Developers should consider how videos are loaded, displayed, and processed by browsers.

For example, automatically loading several large videos when a page opens can consume significant bandwidth. Instead, developers can use lazy loading strategies for videos that appear further down the page.

For background videos, the HTML video element offers several useful attributes:

Developers should also avoid autoplaying large videos unnecessarily. A static image can often provide a better experience on mobile devices or slower connections.

Performance testing tools such as Google Lighthouse and Chrome DevTools can help identify whether video assets are affecting loading speed, bandwidth usage, or rendering performance.

Technical optimization should not come at the expense of accessibility. Background videos should not distract users or make website content difficult to understand.

Developers should respect the prefers-reduced-motion media query, which allows users who prefer reduced animation to receive a less motion-intensive experience.

A suitable static background image can be displayed as an alternative.

Mobile compatibility is equally important. Smaller screens, limited data plans, and varying network conditions can make heavy video assets less practical. Testing videos on different devices and connection speeds helps identify potential problems before publication.

AI-generated video assets offer exciting possibilities for modern web design, but their successful implementation requires more than creative output. Choosing suitable video formats, maintaining consistent visual quality, compressing files, and considering website performance are all essential parts of the development process.

By combining thoughtful video production with responsive delivery, accessibility considerations, and performance testing, designers and developers can create engaging websites without compromising loading speed or usability. As AI video technology continues to evolve, understanding these technical fundamentals will remain valuable for building efficient and visually appealing web experiences.

#10586. Samsung bets that you’ll either buy a Galaxy S27 Pro or Ultra next year

Samsung is teeing up to kick off 2027 with an all-new lineup of flagship smartphones, and despite rising prices at your local Best Buy, the brand is still hedging that you’re going to pony up for the most premium model available.

As reported by ETNews, Samsung is currently allotting over 70 percent of its total production capabilities to just the Galaxy S27 Ultra and the new Galaxy S27 “Pro” model expected to launch alongside it. That leaves the plain Galaxy S27 and the larger S27+ on the outside looking in. In comparison to this year’s launch, the Galaxy S26 Ultra made up about 50 percent of this series’ production volume, for a grand total of 18 million units.

Historical performance implies Samsung is probably correct here. While big displays are typically what consumers are drawn to, it does appear that the S27 Pro won’t be all that much smaller than the S27+. Considering the Pro should slot in right around the typical price point as the Plus model, it’s not hard to imagine plenty of would-be S27+ buyers moving to the Pro.

For what it’s worth, Samsung betting big on high-end devices usually pays off. We’re on year two of the Fold series seeing big successes — first with the redesigned Fold 7 in 2025, then with the reshaped Fold 8 this year — and the Galaxy S26 Ultra managed to become the best-selling Android phone in Q2 of this year in a list typically populated by iPhones and cheap Galaxy A-series models. Even with prices constantly on the rise, the people yearn for flagship smartphones. You just have to wonder where that ceiling actually is.

Upgrading your setup doesn't have to cost the earth

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The PC hardware scene has taken a real beating during the AI explosion, which means routine upgrades to PCs are drastically more expensive than they used to be. Rather than spending hundreds of dollars on more RAM or a new SSD, why not grab yourself (or a loved one) a cheaper sub-$100 upgrade?

As noted, you're probably not going to be able to find a $100 component upgrade to increase your frame rates or jump from 1440p to 4K; at best, you might be able to source yourself a new power supply. So what can you add to a PC for $100? And what makes a good sub-$100 upgrade?

Well, the early obvious candidates are improved connectivity, such as a Wi-Fi card or USB-C expansion slots for memory card readers. You could also add a snazzy LCD screen (albeit not Corsair's $250 Xeneon), new RGB fans, or even an AIO cooler.

Crucially, just because you're spending less than $100 doesn't mean you have to cheap out on budget or unknown brands. You can still find great quality down at this price bracket from reputable companies like Noctua, TP-Link, and beyond.

Here are some of the best PC upgrades you can buy for less than $100.

This Lian Li universal LCD screen can be used either standalone or mounted to a compatible PC case. With vertical or horizontal orientation, its 8.8-inch screen can be used as a USB-powered secondary display for your PC. You can use it to show off PC performance metrics like temperatures, keep an eye on a Twitch stream chat, or control music via your media center of choice.

Got a dusty old air cooler or a stock cooler that came with your CPU? If you want to upgrade your cooling, this is one of our very favorite air coolers for CPUs, with impressive heatsinks and fans. Compatible with both Intel and AMD, this air cooler will be an upgrade over any stock or older model, giving you some overclocking headroom or extra performance if thermals have become a bottleneck.

If you're already using a decent air cooler and you have a compatible case, you can also upgrade to an AIO, and the Arctic Liquid Freezer III is the best one on the market by some distance. It dominates our testing and will keep even the most powerful CPU cool during intense workloads. Remember to check you've got a compatible case; this one requires room for a 360mm radiator. If you don't have that kind of room, you could opt for the 240mm model.

These Noctua fans rank as the best we've tested in our extensive best PC fans roundup. These silence-optimized fans deliver a ton of airflow while running whisper-quiet, either on the radiator of your AIO or anywhere else in your case. Pricey for individual fans, a couple of these would make a great gift or upgrade for any gaming rig.

Perhaps you've got a PC with an older Wi-Fi standard, or perhaps no Wi-Fi at all. This PCIe card can be fitted to any compatible motherboard, equipping your PC with speedy Wi-Fi 7 with little to no hassle. If you want to save a bit more, there's also a Wi-Fi 6E option that will still provide a decent upgrade.

No matter what platform you're on or what your specs are, there's no GPU or CPU that can't benefit from a fresh application of thermal paste or some pads. There are a ton to choose from, but this new Noctua Carbice Carbon Nanotube Thermal Pad promises unparalleled longevity for your CPU on AMD's Ryzen 4 and Ryzen 5.

If you've got an older build, or your hoping to make any future upgrades to your PC, having a good, reliable power supply is a must. For less than $100, you can pick up something like this Thermaltake GF1. Upgrading a PSU could mean going from a non-modular to a modular design, improving your cable management. Or increased capacity could mean better support for a powerful GPU. Upgrading could even improve your noise levels depending on your current setup.

No matter which components you're using, the chances are that they're connected to your GPU by dull, boring cables. Extensions like this Lian Li Strimer don't require you to buy a new power supply or invest in a new cable, they simply attach to the end of your existing cable and add a dash of addressable RGB color to your build. This is the ATX cable for a motherboard, but there are also versions for the GPU cable too, as well as CPU.

Another great option for an upgrade, if you have the spare PCIe slot, is a USB expansion card of some description. This USB-C one adds four high-performance external USB-C ports to your desktop, and one internal USB-A slot. With 10Gbps transfer speeds, this is great for improving your PC's connectivity for external hard drives or other storage solutions.

If you want to start adding some RGB spice to your PC case, Corsair's iCUE system is a well known and easy to use option. A unit like this Commander Duo controller unit is a great starting point to begin connecting up fans and lights, letting you sync your cooling and lights across your PC. It supports up to 12 daisy-chained PWM fans, and comes with two flexible temperature sensors.

#10578. Hunter is killing Google Home, Alexa controls for its smart ceiling fans

Hunter, a major brand for ceiling fans in the US, is pulling the plug on its Google Home and Amazon Alexa bridge for controlling “HunterSMART” models in just a few weeks.

Effective January 1, 2027, Hunter will pull support for the cloud-reliant “HunterSMART” connection used for Google Home and Amazon Alexa controls. Hunter’s app and the included remote will continue to work, but connections to smart home ecosystems will not.

Beginning January 1, 2027, the legacy cloud-to-cloud connections used for app linking between the HunterSMART™ mobile apps and Google Home and/or Amazon Alexa will no longer be supported.

After this date, Google home and Alexa functionality that relies on this legacy cloud connection will no longer work for HunterSMART fans, including voice commands, scenes, and automations.

This bridge is going away in favor of a Matter connection, with Hunter having been upgrading select models to be Matter-compatible over the past year. But this is only available to “eligible” models, not everyone who was already using Hunter’s existing bridge. A list of compatible models is available on Hunter’s website, with firmware updates rolling out via the HunterSMART app.

Some models, such as the one I reviewed a few years ago, require updated hardware. Besides that, the one I have can’t even be re-connected to Wi-Fi to check for an update as Hunter’s Android app consistently crashes during the setup process.

Once the fan has been updated, it won’t be available via the existing link and will instead require re-pairing via your smart home app of choice, whether that’s Google Home or another.

…Hunter is transitioning eligible HunterSMART™ products to Matter, an industry standard designed to support broader smart-home compatibility.

If your fan is eligible for Matter-compatibility, you will be able to continue using all smart functionality within the Google Home and/or Alexa ecosystems after completing the applicable Matter migration & setup.

Once your HunterSMART fan is Matter-enabled, connecting your fan to Google and Alexa will work differently than it does today. Instead of linking your HunterSMART account to Google Home or Alexa, you will need to setup your Matter-enabled fan directly into these compatible ecosystems using the Matter setup available via the Google Home and/or Alexa apps. Matter-enabled devices do not use the legacy cloud linking service for smart controls that is being discontinued.

How can I migrate my HunterSMART fan to Matter?

Depending on your fan model and hardware configuration, a firmware update or updated hardware components may be required for Matter compatibility. Available Matter upgrade options and instructions will vary by product. You can visit our HunterSMART™ Matter resource page for the latest information about Matter compatibility, available upgrade options, and next steps.

#10576. Googlebook apps built by Googlers add macOS-like status bar shortcuts, keyboard commands

Googlebooks are a bit starved of desktop-class features at launch, but one of Google’s pitches around this platform is how apps can be built for it, and the company’s own employees are showing off what that looks like with some clever apps that just so happen to add features that feel a lot like they’re replicating macOS staples.

In a Reddit thread, three Googlebook apps built by Googlers through Antigravity are shown off, and they’re pretty cool, regardless of how you might feel about the platform itself.

“BentoBox” is a utility that gives you control over the status bar on Googlebook, enabling you to fill the status bar with shortcuts, mini-apps, and more. It’s full of glancable information such as weather conditions, battery charging speeds, and more, but also includes useful tools such as being able to access a clipboard history, set a timer, and more. The app requires permissions including accessibility overlays, but it works like a charm.

I had no idea the status bar on Googlebook could even be used like this, and it really makes that space a lot more useful. Of course, you have to draw a comparison here to macOS, which also uses this space for app shortcuts and tools, though BentoBox offers a different type of customization.

BentoBox is available for download via Google Play for free.

The other Mac-like app here is “Booklight,” a launcher that’s basically Apple’s Spotlight, just for Googlebook. It has a transparent design and lets you search “apps, settings, and the web.”

Technically, Googlebook already can do this in its built-in search bar (in the app drawer), but Booklight has more customization and functionality, including being able to write out shorthand commands all in one sentence with the app then completing the command in the appropriate apps, as well as having a clearer UI for experiences such as typing in quick math equations. It’s also good at finding symbols (so you don’t have to remember the U+ codes that carried over from ChromeOS). Not one I found myself wanting to use, but the potential is there.

Both BentoBox and Booklight were developed by Alexander Kuscher, VP over Laptops & Tablets at Google.

The other app highlighted in Google’s thread was Wayfinder, a file manager app described as a “desktop-grade file manager.” The big bonus here is network file access for NAS boxes and other uses, but the app also just has a more advanced UX compared to Googlebook’s built-in Files app.

Googlebook’s app gap is a real problem at launch, but these apps do show there’s a lot of potential in the platform, it’s just a matter of getting developers on board – which itself will require Google and its partners to actually sell some of these laptops… classic chicken-and-egg situation.

#10574. Best AI Humanizer Tools Tested and Compared: 7 Options for Web Content

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best AI humanizer for web content is the one that changes how a draft reads without changing what it says. If you need a specific tone, Humanize Max gives you four styles and three lengths in one run. If you already work in QuillBot or Grammarly, their humanizers fit the account you have. GPTinf, Humanize AI and Undetectable AI put an AI check next to the rewrite, and Ahrefs gives you quick variants of a short passage.

To compare them on equal terms, we ran the same 103-word AI-written release note through each tool in November 2026. It contains a product name, two image formats, a settings path and the word "must", so it shows whether a tool keeps the details a reader acts on. We also checked every feature, limit and price on the vendor's own pages on the same day.

A humanizer earns its place only if its output needs fewer fixes than the draft you started with, and these six checks show whether a tool will do that for your content.

Humanize Max is a single-purpose humanizer: you paste an AI draft, upload a file or load the built-in sample, choose a style and a length, and get a rewritten version in a few seconds. The four styles are Academic, Casual, Creative and Formal, and the three lengths are Shorten, Standard and Expand, so you can turn a stiff feature description into casual blog copy and trim it in the same run.

The result highlights every phrase it changed, which makes it quick to check whether a product name or a settings path came through. If the rewrite doesn't land, Run it again produces a different version of the whole text. At the time of writing, it takes up to 2,000 words per run and works without an account.

Pricing: Plans and limits are still being finalized, so check the site for current terms.

Best for: Rewriting AI drafts of blog posts, product pages and emails into a specific tone.

QuillBot's AI Humanizer sits next to its paraphraser, grammar checker and AI detector, which makes it a natural pick if you already use QuillBot for rewording. Basic mode is free for up to 125 words per run, while Advanced mode, which rewrites more deeply, comes with Premium (the pricing page also lists three Advanced uses on the free plan). In our test, it asked us to create a free account before it showed the first result.

Premium removes the word limit and adds Freeze words, which keeps the terms you choose unchanged, along with tone insights, a history of past runs and a Chrome extension that humanizes text inside ChatGPT and Gemini.

Pricing: Basic mode is free. Premium is listed at $8.33 a month, billed annually.

Best for: Quick fixes to a paragraph or two for writers who already use QuillBot.

GPTinf is a writing toolkit built around its humanizer, with an AI detector, plagiarism checker, grammar checker, paraphraser and readability checker on the same site. The humanizer has two modes, Standard and Academic, and takes pasted text or a PDF, Word or plain-text file. File-to-File mode returns the humanized document in its original format, so a long brief keeps its layout.

Custom Style lets you paste 150 to 1,200 words of your own writing, and later runs are shaped to match it, with no account needed. After each run, an AI-likelihood estimate appears under the output, so you can see whether a draft needs another pass. Treat that number as a probability, not proof of who wrote the text.

Pricing: A free plan is available. Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $24.99 and Unlimited at $59.99.

Best for: Freelancers and content teams that want to rewrite and check drafts without switching tools.

Grammarly's AI Humanizer comes in two layers. The tool on its web page is free, works without an account and offers basic humanization of pasted text or an uploaded document. A free Grammarly account adds the Humanizer agent, with four preset styles, a custom voice built from a writing sample, and support for English, Spanish, French, German, Portuguese and Italian.

Grammarly says the humanizer focuses on clarity and readability rather than on AI detectors, which matches how most web teams use these tools. If you need to show how a piece was written, Grammarly Authorship shows which parts came from AI, which came from online sources and which you wrote yourself. The basic tool's word counter allows up to 1,500 words per run.

Pricing: The basic humanizer is free. Grammarly Pro is listed at $12 on the plans page, which doesn't state the billing period.

Best for: Teams that already write in Grammarly and want one voice across documents.

Humanize AI is built for people who want to tune the output. Without an account you get the Free tone, a Rewrite level slider with three stops (Quality, Balanced and Max) and Custom style, which learns your voice from 150 to 1,200 words of samples. A free account opens eight more modes, including Formal, Informal, Academic, Expand and Shorten, and on desktop a Writing level menu sets how plain or dense the result reads.

View changes highlights every edit, and File-to-File accepts PDF, DOCX, TXT and MD documents and returns them in the same format. Paid plans also include a public API that supports the same styles and rewrite levels as the web app, so a developer can add a humanizing step to a CMS or publishing pipeline.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (10,000 words), with Standard at $19.99 and Unlimited at $59.99. Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Teams that need precise tone control or want to humanize text through an API.

Undetectable AI keeps its humanizer simple: one text box, one button and no style or length settings. Its free allowance works differently from the others, with 1,000 words in total before you need an account and another 1,000 once you create a free one.

Under the output, a Check for AI button runs a detection check on the result, and the same site has a separate AI detector and plagiarism checker. Its FAQ says pasted text isn't published or shared but doesn't say whether it's stored, so keep confidential details out of the drafts you run through it.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $25 (25,000 words) and Unlimited at $59 (1,000,000 words). Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Trying a humanizer on a full-length draft before you commit to an account.

Ahrefs, best known for its SEO platform, offers a free AI Text Humanizer among its writing tools. You paste up to 2,048 characters, choose one, three or five variants, and pick the version that reads best. It ran without an account in our test.

Variants are the useful part. For a hero headline, a meta description or a product blurb, comparing three rewrites side by side is faster than prompting a chatbot three times, and you can export all results as a text file. The character limit, which fits a few short paragraphs, rules it out for full articles.

Best for: Short marketing copy where you want several options to choose from.

The best AI humanizer tool for a documentation team isn't the one a growth marketer needs, so match the tool to the content in front of you.

There isn't one winner, because these tools solve different problems. The quickest way to decide is to run a paragraph from your own work, ideally one with a product name, a number and an instruction, through two or three of the tools above, and keep the one whose output you'd publish with the fewest fixes.

Google's guidance on using generative AI content is about value rather than the tool: mass-producing pages that add nothing for readers can count as scaled content abuse. A humanizer changes phrasing, not usefulness, so the same rules apply. Google also recommends fact-checking AI-generated titles, meta descriptions and image alt text before you publish.

The best humanizer for your team is the one whose controls match your content: tone presets for blog posts, variants for short copy, change highlighting for docs and a custom voice for marketing. Run one of your own paragraphs through two or three tools from this list, then read every highlighted change, especially names, numbers and words like "must", before anything goes live.

#10573. Fitbit Sense & Versa losing 3rd-party watch faces this month, existing downloads remain

Google announced today that Fitbit Sense and Versa smartwatches will soon lose the ability to “install or update third-party apps and clocks.”

This change to the Fitbit App Gallery applies to the Versa, Versa 2, Versa 3, Versa 4, Sense, and Sense 2.

Existing “third-party apps or clock faces installed on your Fitbit device” will remain available, but you can no longer download new ones (or update them) starting November 28. As such, “if you choose to delete an app or clock face, or factory reset your device, you will no longer be able to re-install it.”

Instead, the Fitbit App Gallery will “exclusively feature apps and clock faces created by Fitbit and Google.”

The November 28 deadline also applies to third-party developers. Google will “no longer support uploading new apps and clock faces or updating existing ones.”

Google winding down Fitbit OS for smartwatches is not particularly surprising as the company has been all-in on Wear OS and the Pixel Watch. That said, the Sense 2 and Versa 4 should probably no longer be on sale.
