The best gifts to get keyboard lovers — even if they already have a keyboard they love.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Keyboards are one of the most important, if not the most important, PC peripherals. You use your keyboard constantly — it's the primary input source for just about everyone (there are a few exceptions). And everyone deserves a keyboard that's a little nicer than the $5 board that was tossed in for free with your last PC purchase. Or — god forbid — whatever's built into your laptop.

If you've got a gamer, a writer, or, well, anyone who uses a computer for any reason in your life, they could probably use a keyboard upgrade. Even keyboard enthusiasts with multiple mechanical boards will appreciate a new one, especially if it fills a niche — say, a low-profile board that's portable and perfect for traveling, or a nostalgia-inducing retro-inspired board that's reminiscent of their favorite old-school game console.

Mechanical keyboards don't usually come cheap, but there are several budget-friendly boards that any keyboard lover would be thrilled to receive. And if they've already got the board of their dreams, pick up a fun accessory — a cool-looking cable, some pretty keycaps, or a wrist rest — to really elevate their typing experience.

There aren't a ton of budget-friendly mechanical keyboards built for keyboard enthusiasts, but there are a few. These are all pre-built, so your recipient won't need to know how to assemble anything, but you can also often pick up bare-bones keyboard kits for cheaper than their fully built counterparts.

This full-size wired mechanical keyboard comes with red (linear) or brown (tactile) switches and has a hot-swappable PCB, PBT keycaps, and up to a 1,000 Hz polling rate — so it's great for both work and play.

This compact 75% keyboard comes with Leobog Reaper switches, PBT keycaps, and a programmable knob. The shell is mostly plastic, but the layers of sound-dampening material make for a nice "thock."

This 75% wireless mechanical keyboard features a gasket mount design, PBT keycaps, bright RGB backlighting, and a customizable TFT screen, as well as a programmable knob. And a hot-swappable PCB so you can bring your own switches.

This low-profile wireless 75% mechanical keyboard comes with a wrist rest that doubles as a keyboard stand to adjust the angle. The board comes with low-profile linear switches and features a full aluminum body, so it looks and feels premium — but still costs under $100.

This 87-key Bluetooth/2.4GHz/USB keyboard has all the retro vibes of a classic IBM-style keyboard, but without the huge price tag! Kailh Box Switch V2 White mechanical key switches, dye-sub PBT keycaps, and multiple connection options make this a great keyboard for retro computing and getting work done.

An OG-Xbox-inspired 87-key keyboard with RGB backlight! You get 87 keys with Kailh Jellyfish X switches, double-layered ABS keycaps, and a lovely translucent green plastic shell! The colored cursor keys are beautiful.

If you're shopping for a keyboard enthusiast — or anyone who already has a keyboard they love, getting them a keyboard as a gift probably won't go over quite as well. But that doesn't mean you can't still pick up an accessory or two to complement their favorite board.

Cables are a part of life, but that doesn't mean they have to be ugly. This coiled USB-C cable from Keychron comes in four colors and is specifically designed to fit Keychron's K, K Pro, K Max, and K HE series boards, but will work with most USB-C boards. It comes with a 14cm coiled section and a 94cm straight cable that connect via a detachable aviator port, plus a USB-C to USB-A converter.

Not all keyboards have hot-swappable switches, but almost all of them have swappable keycaps. Glorious' cherry-profile keycaps are made of double-shot PBT and come in a variety of pretty, ombre colors.

Just about every keyboard user could use a wrist rest, and this cooling gel-filled memory foam wrist rest from HyperX is one of our long-time favorites. It's durable and comfortable, and comes in multiple sizes to fit everything from compact to full-size keyboards.

Gel/foam wrist rests are comfortable, but wooden wrist rests are aesthetic (and comfortable, not everyone needs cushion under their wrists). This wooden wrist rest is designed to put your wrists in a neutral position and features non-slip feet to keep it from moving around. It comes in four sizes to fit everything from 60% to full-size keyboards.

Can you be Nvidia's next CEO, old friend?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia's board of directors has approached Arm chief executive Rene Haas as a potential successor to Jensen Huang, according to a report from the Financial Times that cites people familiar with the matter. Haas is among several candidates under consideration, although it remains unclear whether he will get a formal offer and/or agree to take the position.

The search for Nvidia's next chief executive comes as the company undergoes a leadership transition following Jensen Huang's swift departure from the CEO position earlier this week. For now, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress serves as interim CEO. The company's board is currently searching for a permanent replacement and is reportedly evaluating both internal and external candidates. Neither Arm nor Nvidia commented on the matter to FT.

Rene Haas is no stranger to Nvidia, as he spent approximately seven years at the company before leaving in 2013. During his tenure as vice president at Nvidia, he was responsible for multiple projects, including notebook GPUs, Windows-on-Arm, and Tegra system-on-chips. Given that he held senior management positions, he certainly gained experience with Nvidia's operations, corporate culture, and relationships with customers and partners.

Furthermore, Haas has experience with the development of consumer and data center CPUs as well as GPUs and GPU IP, a combination that is hard to find in the industry. Haas reportedly maintains close ties with Jensen Huang and several members of Nvidia's board, which could make him a particularly attractive candidate for a company whose strategy and management structure have orbited around its founder for more than three decades.

While at Arm, Haas has demonstrated a willingness to make substantial strategic changes at the company. Since Haas took the reins in 2022, Arm has introduced its Compute Subsystems (CSS) initiative, providing customers with complete and validated processor platform design, making it easier for newcomers to enter the market. Arm also introduced its first own-brand AGI CPU, putting the company in direct competition with some of its traditional customers. Both initiatives represent significant departures from Arm's traditional business model; however, they demonstrate Haas' readiness to pursue new revenue opportunities despite risks and the possibility of damaging relationships with long-term partners.

Another advantage is Haas' extensive experience with the semiconductor industry in general. Arm's CPU and GPU technologies are used in system-on-chips developed by hundreds of companies that have very distinctive requirements, including Nvidia itself, Apple, Qualcomm, and MediaTek, just to name a few.

In addition, Haas led Arm through its initial public offering in 2023 and therefore has experience with investors and regulators. Also, Haas happens to be the CEO of SoftBank, the parent of Arm.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Nevertheless, Haas' potential appointment would not be without challenges, primarily because he has never managed a company the scale of Nvidia. Arm has around 10,000 employees worldwide; it develops and licenses various semiconductor IP, primarily CPU and GPU technologies, to a wide range of customers with distinct needs. By contrast, Nvidia designs CPUs, GPUs, networking equipment, complete AI systems, and software platforms and has a headcount of about 42,000. As a result, if Haas becomes the boss of Nvidia, he will have to manage a considerably bigger organization with a dramatically more complicated product portfolio and supply chain. Furthermore, replacing Huang, who has led Nvidia since its establishment in 1993 and personally shaped its technology strategy and business tactics, would be a formidable task for virtually any executive.

Ironically, Haas' appointment (if it happens) would create an unusual situation in which Nvidia will be led by the chief executive of a company it attempted to acquire in 2020 and abandoned the transaction in 2022 amid regulatory opposition.

#10564. Samsung Galaxy S27 series camera specs leak, and they’re disappointing

New leaks reveal the camera specs for Samsung’s upcoming Galaxy S27 series which includes three lenses across the lineup, but not the upgrades you want, nor what Ultra owners might expect.

SammyGuru reports that Samsung is “evaluating” camera setups for the Galaxy S27, S27+, S27 Pro, and S27 Ultra, and it looks like a mixed bag across the board.

Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, meanwhile, have a new 200MP main camera on deck, a 50MP ultrawide shooter, and a mere 12MP telephoto. The Ultra might get 5x compared to the Pro’s 3x optical zoom, but with a secondary telephoto lens going missing, existing users of the lineup are sure to be disappointed.

Personally, I’m very “whelmed” on this one. As RAMageddon rages on and Samsung hunts for new ways to balance specs vs price, cameras are a place that’s obviously going to be cut, but competitors are already running laps around Samsung on the hardware front. Cutting lenses and skimping out on upgrades (again) doesn’t help matters.

#10562. From User Feedback to a Better Prompt: 4 Tools Tested in TypeScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When an LLM app gives a wrong answer, nothing in your logs shows it. The request returns 200, the JSON is valid, and no exception fires. Usually the user is the only one who notices. A thumbs-down button records that moment, on the exact trace that went wrong, while the user still remembers why.

That button is easy to add. The hard part is what comes after: turning the thumbs-down into a reviewed test case, a judge score, and a better prompt.

We ran that loop in TypeScript in four tools: LangSmith, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. All four handled the first four steps, which are user feedback, reviewer comments, a dataset and an LLM judge. Only AcruxCore rewrote the prompt from TypeScript. On 15 support tickets that no tool had seen, the rewritten prompt routed 13 correctly, against 7 for the original.

Our test app is a support-ticket router. It reads a ticket and returns JSON with a category (account, billing, bug or feature_request) and a priority (P0, P1 or P2). The first version of the prompt describes only the output format:

The support team has a policy that this prompt never states. Sign-in problems are always account. A customer who can't use the product at all is P0. Any billing error is P1, even when the customer says there is no rush. Feature requests are P2, however angry the customer sounds.

Without that policy, the model guesses priority from the customer's tone. Here are three real replies from the first version:

Every reply is valid JSON, so no schema check catches the mistake. A person has to read the reply and say it is wrong. The loop in this article takes that judgement and turns it into a better prompt.

All four tools cover evaluation. We compared the self-hosted ones on tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent, and on prompt registries in Prompt Management in TypeScript. For a broader comparison of self-hosted options, see this LangSmith alternatives roundup. This article covers the feedback loop only.

We wrote 30 tickets. Fifteen are the "production traffic" that each tool's app processed. The other 15 are a holdout set. No tool saw them, and we used them only for the final before-and-after check.

Each tool ran the same sequence from one TypeScript script:

The reviewer comments follow one template, so the wording is identical in every tool:

The comments name one rule each. The full policy appears nowhere in the data any tool received.

The app model isn't fully deterministic at temperature 0. Each tool's run got between 6 and 8 of the 15 traffic tickets right, so the tools received 7 to 9 thumbs-down each. The holdout check below repeats every measurement three times.

All four tools accept feedback on a trace from TypeScript. They differ in how a second person, the reviewer, adds the correct answer.

LangSmith stores both as feedback on a run. The thumbs-down runs go into an annotation queue, where a reviewer adds feedback with a comment:

Langfuse stores both as scores. An annotation queue needs a score config first:

Phoenix stores both as span annotations. The reviewer's comment goes in explanation:

Phoenix stores spans asynchronously. Our first run annotated a span before Phoenix had stored it, and the API returned a 404. The script now waits until all 15 spans are visible.

AcruxCore stores both as feedback rows on the trace. The source field separates the end user from the reviewer:

This is the first step where the code differs a lot. Each tool can make a dataset, but only one builds it from feedback in one call.

In LangSmith, a run query finds the thumbs-down runs. Then createExamples copies them into a dataset. You decide where the reviewer's comment goes. We put it in the reference output:

LangSmith can also do this without code. An automation rule in the UI can add runs that match a filter to a dataset.

In Langfuse, the scores API finds the thumbs-down traces. Two details cost us a run each. A boolean score is filtered with value: "false", not 0. And the trace ID is returned only when you ask for the subject field group:

In Phoenix, getSpans can't filter by annotation. So the script lists the app's spans, reads their annotations, and keeps the ones with a thumbs-down. Then createDataset takes the examples. We kept the source span ID in the metadata.

In AcruxCore, buildFromFeedback takes the reviewer's feedback IDs. Each comment becomes that example's grading criteria, and the input comes from the variables captured on the trace:

Each tool scored the first version of the prompt on its own dataset. The judge read the reviewer's comment and the router's reply, and returned 1 for a match and 0 for a mismatch.

LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix each have an experiment runner that takes your evaluator function, and we gave all three the same judge prompt. AcruxCore grades with its built-in judge inside the optimize run. All four used the same judge model.

The first version fails almost every reviewed case. That is expected, because these are the cases users marked wrong. A score of 1 means the app model got that case right on the second call, or the judge accepted a wrong reply. We didn't check which.

The judge can also run without your code. Besides the in-code evaluator we used, Langfuse has a managed LLM-as-a-judge that runs on production traces and on experiments, with your own judge prompt. LangSmith has online evaluators, which you set up in its UI, for the same job.

An optimizer reads the failing cases and the reviewer comments, writes new versions of the prompt, and scores each one with the judge. This is the step that turns feedback into a better prompt, and it's where the four tools differ most.

LangSmith's API reference lists an optimize prompt job (POST /api/v1/repos/optimize-job). It takes a prompt name, a dataset name, evaluator IDs and an algorithm, promptim or demo. No method in the TypeScript SDK wraps it, so we called it with fetch. We gave it a code evaluator that checks the reply against the reviewer's correct answer.

The job was accepted and reported successful about ten seconds later. It ran no experiment on the dataset. With auto_commit set to true, it added no new commit to the prompt. We found no guide for this endpoint, so we may have configured it wrongly, but the job record and its logs gave no error to act on.

LangSmith's documented route to prompt optimization is promptim, which its README calls an experimental library. It is a Python package with a CLI, and it can add a human review step through LangSmith annotation queues.

Langfuse has no optimizer. Its blog shows a workflow with Claude Code agent skills that reads Langfuse data and edits the prompt. That workflow runs in a coding agent, not in your app's code.

Phoenix's Optimize Prompts Automatically tutorial uses Arize's prompt-learning package, which you clone from GitHub and install with pip. The Phoenix TypeScript client has no optimizer export, and the tutorial runs outside Phoenix. Arize's commercial platform, Arize AX, has prompt optimization built in.

For Python teams, there are more options: Opik's Agent Optimizer and DSPy both rewrite prompts from a dataset and a metric. This article covers TypeScript.

Its optimize run takes the dataset and drafts three rewrites of the production prompt. It runs each rewrite and the current production version on every example, and the judge grades all of them:

The three rewrites tied, so we took candidate A. Its rationale: "Added explicit priority rules for each category and clarified that access-blocking issues are P0, billing errors are P1, and feature requests are P2, overriding tone/urgency." The Improve a prompt from feedback guide covers the dashboard version of this step.

A rewrite that passes the cases it was built from can still fail on new tickets. So we ran both prompts on the 15 holdout tickets, three times each, outside every tool. The script calls the model directly and checks the category and the priority against the expected answer. No judge is involved.

Both come from one line in the rewrite: "P1 for bugs without workaround." That rule isn't in the team's policy, and no reviewer wrote it. The optimizer made it up to explain the examples it had. There was also no outage in the traffic set's thumbs-down cases, because the first version routed that one correctly. So no comment ever said that a full outage is P0.

The optimizer learns only from what users marked wrong. Anything they never marked stays a guess, and the rationale won't tell you which rules are guesses. Read the rewritten prompt before you promote it.

In LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix, shipping a new version is a tag or label move. We showed those calls in the prompt-management article. Here there was no rewrite to ship, so the step didn't apply.

In AcruxCore, promotion commits the candidate as a new prompt version and moves the production alias to it. Nothing else moves:

The app renders production on every call, so the next ticket used version 2 without a deploy.

Use LangSmith if your team already works in it and writes Python. Feedback, queues, datasets and experiments all work from TypeScript, and promptim covers optimization from Python.

Use Langfuse if you want a managed judge that runs on live traces with your own prompt. Plan to write the dataset glue code and the rewrite step yourself.

Use Phoenix if you already trace with OpenTelemetry and annotate spans. Plan to move to Python, or to Arize AX, for optimization.

Use AcruxCore if you want all six steps from TypeScript in one tool, including the rewrite. Keep a human on the promote button.

#10554. Samsung’s paid Galaxy AI plan has a giant Gemini problem

Good morning. Today I’m trying desperately not to buy Sony’s new nostalgia-bait headphones, reeling from how emotionally invested I was in Spider-Man: Brand New Day (yes, I know I’m late), trying to figure out what a “private, agentic notebook” is, and slowly coming around to the idea of the New York Times owning Letterboxd.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

The relationship between Samsung and Google has, over the years, waxed and waned depending on their respective interdependence. Samsung is Android’s physical emissary, powering a plurality of all its handsets globally. But Google, and in particular its services, is the glue that holds Samsung’s smartphone experience together.

The back and forth is over a decade old; back in early 2014, Samsung shipped a refreshed magazine-style skin with its Galaxy tablets that eschewed many of the design tenets Google had been pushing its partners to adhere to. Not only was Samsung willfully defying its most important partner, but it made a point of barely mentioning Android during its press conferences.

Around the same time, it transitioned its smartwatches from Android Wear to Tizen and began shipping duplicative apps on its devices (many of which persist to this day). The goal was to distance itself from Google as much as possible.

While we don’t know exactly what happened behind closed doors, we saw the outcome quite clearly: Magazine UX lasted one product cycle, and the two companies signed a 10-year cross-license patent deal, which may have helped Samsung prevent any future lawsuits with Apple. Google then sold Motorola to Lenovo, clearing the way for Samsung to continue building its burgeoning hardware business on the back of Google’s mobile ecosystem.

Since then, the two companies have grown even closer. In 2021, they co-announced a new Wear OS along with an overarching wearables partnership that has since grown to include Android XR as well. And Google is still the default search engine for Samsung devices, despite reported inroads made by Microsoft in 2023 to convince the Korean giant to switch to a ChatGPT-powered version of Bing Search. (Samsung’s ongoing relationship with Microsoft, which bundles its Office suite on most Galaxy devices, could be seen as a point of leverage for negotiations with Google, too.)

This brings us to today, and to AI. Samsung was among the first companies to adopt Google’s full suite of Gemini Intelligence products, and its phones often get new AI features before anyone else. But like a decade ago, when Samsung shipped Bixby to kind-of-sort-of compete with Google Assistant, it still wants to offer its own services alongside Google’s.

So a rumor that Samsung may launch a paid Galaxy AI Premium subscription next year for $5/month piqued my interest for the same reason Magazine UX did 12 years ago. Galaxy AI features today are a blend of first-party and third-party capabilities, but none of them are especially useful, particularly when compared to Google’s own Android equivalents powered by Gemini Intelligence. Now Bar and Now Brief, two heavily promoted features in Samsung’s marketing, are wildly disappointing.

At I/O this past May, Google announced Spark, an agent that goes out and performs actions on your behalf. And while it’s since been somewhat eclipsed by more capable mobile options like Meta’s Muse, it’s obvious that Google is working on a follow-up that will perform functions both on the phone and inside apps.

That’s exactly what Samsung is rumored to be working on with Bixby Action, a spiritual successor to its namesake that, when it debuted, prioritized on-device tasks like turning on Wi-Fi over web-based actions Google Assistant did better.

According to leaks from SamMobile, Galaxy AI Premium will offer agentic upgrades for phone users to “create commands triggers and system routines for daily tasks” or “automate routine daily tasks using Galaxy AI.” Gen Video will reportedly “convert static photos into dynamic video clips,” while a new Daily Briefing likely does what it says on the tin.

Will Samsung users see enough value in this hodgepodge of features paywalled only because they use a surfeit of compute? And what models will power them on the backend?

Right now, Google is in the enviable position of being able to offer its AI at three distinct layers: the model itself, which is invisible to the user; the OS level, which can promulgate Gemini across every Android phone that runs Google services; and the hardware level, where Pixel lets it specialize and optimize.

Samsung, not particularly known for its software prowess, will likely need to step gingerly around Google’s AI shadow while also justifying the cost to shore up its much-needed services revenue. Based on this leaked roadmap, though, I’m not confident it will work out.

Instead, Samsung may want to acquire one of the many up-and-coming AI agent startups whose valuations are rising more quickly than their user bases. While Muse and OpenAI’s Dots have taken up most of the recent conversation, I’ve been using Hark, Town, and Instinct pretty regularly for a month or so and could see them being brought in-house by Samsung and offered either as standalone products or customized for Galaxy devices. As Google is doing with its own AI plans, Samsung could provide Galaxy users a year of free service, after which they would be expected to pay.

Samsung doesn’t have a great history of building software, but many of its acquisitions, from SmartThings to Viv Labs (which became “next-gen” Bixby) to LoopPay (which became Samsung Pay) to Whisk (which became Samsung Food), have proven successful. Given the current urgent state of mobile AI, I’d forgo the hodgepodge and bring in some outside expertise.

Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy is the only thing tech media can talk about right now, likely because it has implications far beyond just Nvidia’s stock price. The Nvidia CEO, who is one of the world’s richest and most powerful tech executives, is believed to have been targeted by Chinese intelligence to potentially influence American trade policy around AI.

Alex Kantrowitz’s Big Technology piece is a useful starting point for why this matters at a global scale:

Beyond the affair, the real issue is that Huang’s love interest, ex-Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang, is someone the United States government believes passed information to the Chinese government while building data centers with Nvidia’s help in Asia. There’s simply no getting away from this detail. This isn’t just an affair, it’s a corporate and national security issue.

Whether Huang can survive the impending investigation by Nvidia’s board remains to be seen, but the CEO has been a stalwart in the tech space for 33 years, and there is no clear successor to his position should he be forced to resign.

Damien reviewed DroidDeck, which I wrote about last week as part of a broader look at Android’s gaming renaissance.

Over the last few days, things have become even more preposterous: the Ocarina of Time remake, which was released on Thursday, has already been recompiled to run natively on Android (though it runs better on PC), largely with the help of frontier AI models.

All of these AI-powered shenanigans come after Anthropic said it would stop making it possible to recreate copies of games, claiming that “recreating someone else’s specific game using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.” Good thing there are no other models capable of doing the same thing.

I think when you zoom out and look at production, AI really has the potential to be the biggest creative unlock in modern history. The last time we saw anything close to this was really the, you know, advent of CGI and digital composites in the early ’90s. That technology gave birth to amazing stories like Jurassic Park and Terminator 2, and it’s hard to imagine that AI is not going to have a more significant impact than that. And I personally believe that’s going to be an incredible unlock on creativity.

—David Ellison, Skydance CEO, when asked about how AI could impact the movie-making process

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Thursday.

#10549. Friendly Fraud Prevention for Developers: What to Log, Block and Automate

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Friendly fraud prevention comes down to three jobs you can do in code: log the right checkout data, block risky payments before they settle, and automate your response when a dispute still lands. Get the first one wrong and you can't win the dispute later, no matter how good your case is.

In this tutorial, we'll build each piece in Node.js in a way that works with any payment service provider (PSP). Your code owns the data and the logic, and a thin adapter talks to Stripe, Adyen, Checkout.com or whoever processes your payments.

Friendly fraud happens when a customer makes a real purchase, then disputes it with their card issuer. They might claim they didn't authorize it, that it never arrived, or that they canceled. Sometimes it's honest confusion. A spouse used the saved card, or the charge showed up under a name they didn't recognize. Sometimes it's a free refund with extra steps.

Either way, the money leaves your account. A dispute (also called a chargeback) reverses the payment, and your processor charges a dispute fee on top. Lose too many, and the card networks put your account into a monitoring program with higher fees.

It's also common. Visa puts friendly fraud at around 20% of fraudulent disputes globally, and up to 30% for high-volume online merchants. Mastercard goes further: it says first-party fraud makes up 75% of the fraud online businesses experience.

So why is this your problem and not finance's? Because the evidence that wins these disputes is created at checkout, by your code. If your app never stored the customer's IP address, device ID or login history, nobody can produce it three months later when the dispute arrives.

Friendly fraud can be stopped at four points, and each one is a different piece of code:

Most guides only cover stages 1 and 4. The rest of this tutorial walks through all four.

Log four things on every order: the customer's IP address, a device ID, their account ID and the shipping address. Those are the data elements Visa's Compelling Evidence 3.0 (CE3.0) rules check when you fight an "unauthorized" fraud dispute.

Here's how CE3.0 works. You win the dispute if you can show two earlier, undisputed purchases on the same card, made 120 to 365 days before the disputed one. All three purchases must share at least two data elements, and one of them must be the IP address or device ID. In plain terms: same card, same person, same device, and they never complained before.

Most PSPs support CE3.0, including Stripe, Adyen, Checkout.com and Airwallex. But none of them can submit data you never captured. And because the window reaches back a full year, you need to keep these logs for at least 13 months (Mastercard's rule, below).

Mastercard has its own version, called First-Party Trust, for disputes filed under reason code 4837 (no cardholder authorization). It asks for one data point from each of three categories: a device factor (IP address, device ID or fingerprint), a delivery factor (shipping address, email or phone) and a different identity factor (such as account login ID or billing address). On the dispute route, Mastercard calls a lookup service that you host, with named fields such as deviceIpAddress, orderDeviceId, orderEmail and userIdName. That service has to answer in about 2.5 seconds on average and search 13 months of sales data.

First-Party Trust is live in the US. Per Mastercard's October 2026 announcement, it reaches Eastern Europe, the Middle East and Africa from October 2026 and the rest of Europe from July 2027. If you sell into Europe, the logging work is due now.

The good news: one evidence table serves both networks. Capture the data once, then keep the eligibility checks for Visa and Mastercard separate.

Start with a table that stores this data per order, separate from your PSP:

Then capture it in your checkout route. If your app sits behind a proxy or CDN, set Express's trust proxy so req.ip returns the customer's address, not your load balancer's:

The psp object is your adapter: one small module per provider that maps its API to the same shape. Swap providers, and this route doesn't change.

For digital products and SaaS, log usage too. Login timestamps, downloads and feature activity prove the customer used what they paid for, which beats "I never received it" claims.

The cheapest dispute is the one that never happens. Three checkout changes do most of the work: 3D Secure on risky payments, a billing descriptor customers recognize, and a few rules that stop obvious abuse.

Use 3D Secure where risk is higher. 3D Secure (3DS) asks the cardholder to confirm the payment with their bank, often through an app prompt. For authenticated payments, liability for fraud disputes generally shifts from you to the card issuer. Every major PSP supports it, and most let you request it per payment instead of forcing it on everyone. Forcing it everywhere adds friction and costs conversions, so trigger it on risk signals.

Make your billing descriptor obvious. The descriptor is the text on the customer's card statement. If it reads PMT*SRV 8812 instead of your product name, expect "I don't recognize this charge" disputes. Use the brand name customers know, and add a support URL or phone number where your PSP allows it. If you sell several products, use a dynamic descriptor that names each one.

Add a risk check before you charge. Most PSPs include a rules engine, but a small check in your own code keeps the logic portable:

Store the signals array with the order. If a dispute arrives later, you'll know whether the payment looked risky at the time, which helps you decide whether to fight it or accept it.

Keep the rules few and specific. A rule that blocks 5% of good customers to stop 1% of bad ones costs more than the disputes it prevents.

Yes, if you ship physical goods. The window between a successful payment and the moment an order ships is the cheapest place to stop friendly fraud: nothing has left the warehouse, so canceling costs you nothing but the sale. Most fraud tools decide at checkout and never look again.

The pattern is a fulfillment gate. Instead of sending every paid order straight to your warehouse or 3PL, route it through a decision step first:

The verify path does two jobs. Legitimate buyers confirm and their order ships. Buyers planning to dispute see that the store is watching, and a confirmed verification becomes evidence if they dispute anyway.

The hard part is screenOrder. Your own data only shows how a customer behaved with you. A buyer who has filed "item not received" disputes at 10 other stores looks brand new on their first order with yours. That's why post-purchase screening works best on shared data across many merchants.

Chargeflow Prevent is one example of this kind of friendly fraud prevention. It scores each order after checkout and before fulfillment, links device, IP, email and payment behavior across a network of 20,000+ merchants, and returns approve, verify or cancel. Today it runs as a Shopify app, with no checkout changes. A public Prevent API isn't available yet, so on other stacks you'd build the gate above with your own rules and data.

Build an order lookup endpoint. When a cardholder taps "I don't recognize this" in their banking app, the issuer can now ask the merchant for order details before a dispute is filed. Visa does this through Verifi Order Insight, and Mastercard through Ethoca Consumer Clarity. Show the customer a receipt with your logo, the items and the delivery date, and many disputes are never filed.

One endpoint can serve both networks, plus Mastercard's First-Party Trust dispute route. Mastercard's published service levels for this kind of lookup are strict:

The tricky part is matching. Issuers often send partial data, so match on whatever arrives: acquirer reference number (ARN), authorization code, date, amount, currency, and card BIN plus last four digits.

On top of lookups, Visa and many PSPs offer rules-based auto-refunds. Verifi's Rapid Dispute Resolution (RDR) and Visa Resolve refund pre-disputes that match rules you set, such as "refund anything under $25". You don't write code for these, but you do pick the thresholds, so treat them like the REFUND_THRESHOLD below.

If you sell subscriptions, many friendly fraud disputes aren't about stolen cards at all. The customer forgot about a renewal, couldn't find the cancel button, or thinks they canceled. Each of these has a code fix.

Here's a daily job that sends renewal reminders and records that each one went out:

That evidenceLog row matters. When a customer later claims they never knew about the renewal, you can show the exact date you told them.

Automate two things: refunding payments that get flagged before they become chargebacks, and building evidence packets for the ones you fight. Both run off webhooks, and both have hard deadlines that humans miss.

Card networks can warn you before a dispute is filed. Visa runs this through Verifi and Mastercard through Ethoca, and many PSPs pass these alerts or their own early fraud warnings to you by webhook. Refunding at this stage costs you the sale, but you skip the dispute fee and the dispute doesn't count against your ratio.

The threshold is a business call. Refunding a $20 order is cheaper than fighting it; a $900 order with strong evidence may be worth contesting.

When a fraud dispute arrives, check whether it qualifies for CE3.0 by looking for two earlier undisputed orders on the same card that share data with the disputed one:

If you get two matches, pass them to your PSP's CE3.0 evidence fields. On Stripe that's evidence.enhanced_evidence.visa_compelling_evidence_3; other PSPs have their own equivalents. Your adapter handles the mapping. If not, fall back to standard evidence: the IP, receipt, usage logs, renewal reminders and terms acceptance you've been logging all along.

Always submit before the deadline your PSP returns with the dispute. A late response is an automatic loss.

The code above covers the basics. A production system also needs evidence templates per reason code, network deadlines per card brand, retries, and win-rate tracking. That maintenance multiplies with every PSP you add.

PSP-native tools only see that PSP's data, so they miss the usage logs, renewal reminders and support threads in your own app. A dedicated platform connects to your PSPs and lets you send that data in.

Here's what the buy path looks like with the Chargeflow API. Chargeflow ingests disputes from connected PSPs (Stripe, PayPal, Adyen, Checkout.com, Braintree and 20+ others), fires a dispute.created webhook, and you enrich the dispute with the evidence you logged:

Chargeflow then builds the response and submits it to the PSP before the deadline. Pre-dispute alerts work the same way: Chargeflow can refund flagged transactions for you, or send alerts.created and alerts.transaction.linked webhooks so your code issues the refund and reports the result to POST /alerts/{id}/outcome.

Using Claude, ChatGPT or Cursor to build this? Chargeflow publishes its whole developer documentation as one machine-readable file, docs.chargeflow.io/llms-full.txt. Point your assistant at it and it can write the webhook handler and field mapping against the real API, not a guess.

Then your merchants get the disputes, and you need to give them a dispute product without building a dispute team. Chargeflow Connect is built for this. It exposes the same prevention, alerts and recovery engine to your platform in a few delivery models:

EaaS is the most developer-friendly entry point. You POST /public/2025-04-01/evidence with a merchant account_id and a dispute, get back an in_progress record, and receive an evidence.ready webhook with the PDF link, typically in about 30 seconds. Store the evidence id, not the URL, because the download link expires after 7 days. You can run it on auto-pilot (Chargeflow generates and submits) or co-pilot (your merchant approves first).

Don't wait for a customer to dispute a charge to find out your evidence pipeline is broken. Most PSPs give you test cards that create disputes on demand.

One more check before launch: confirm your webhook endpoints verify signatures and return 200 fast. PSPs and Chargeflow retry deliveries that fail, and a slow handler can process the same dispute twice.

If you only do one thing this week, start logging IP address and device ID on every order. Everything else builds on that data.

True fraud is when a criminal uses a stolen card. Friendly fraud is when the real cardholder makes a purchase, then disputes it. Prevention differs: true fraud is stopped at checkout with risk checks and 3DS, while friendly fraud is won with evidence after the fact.

Mostly, yes. Both describe a real cardholder disputing a legitimate charge. "Chargeback fraud" usually implies the customer did it on purpose, while friendly fraud also covers honest mistakes, like a forgotten subscription or a family member's purchase. Visa calls it first-party misuse, and Mastercard calls it first-party fraud.

Check the reason code first. For "unauthorized" fraud claims, submit Visa CE3.0 or Mastercard First-Party Trust evidence if the customer qualifies. For "not received" or "canceled" claims, submit delivery proof, usage logs and your cancellation records. For low-value orders, accepting the dispute or refunding on an alert is often cheaper than fighting it.

Partly. 3DS generally shifts liability for fraud disputes to the issuer, so "I didn't authorize this" claims on authenticated payments rarely cost you. It doesn't help with "item not received" or "I canceled" disputes, which need delivery proof and cancellation records.

No. CE3.0 needs two undisputed purchases on the same card made 120 to 365 days earlier, so it only protects returning customers. For first-time buyers, rely on 3DS and standard evidence.

Yes. Mastercard's First-Party Trust program covers reason code 4837 disputes. It needs one device, one delivery and one extra identity data point, and Mastercard checks them against the cardholder's past purchases. The same evidence table you build for Visa feeds both.

It depends on the stage. At checkout, your PSP's 3D Secure and rules engine. After payment, post-purchase screening such as Chargeflow Prevent holds risky orders before they ship. Before a chargeback, Verifi Order Insight and Ethoca Consumer Clarity show cardholders the order details, while Verifi and Ethoca alerts (offered by many PSPs and by Chargeflow Alerts) and RDR let you refund early. After a chargeback, Visa CE3.0, Mastercard First-Party Trust and dispute automation tools submit the evidence. For developers, the Chargeflow API covers the alert and dispute stages across PSPs, and Chargeflow Connect does the same for platforms.

#10540. Integrating TorchTPU into TorchTitan and Optimizing Models for TPU

Over the past years, Meta’s TorchTitan has emerged as the PyTorch ecosystem’s premier clean-room reference platform for large-scale generative AI model training. Built entirely on PyTorch-native scaling primitives, TorchTitan provides an unencumbered, highly modular showcase for pre-training flagship dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures like Llama 3, Qwen 3, and FLUX.

Historically, developers looking to train PyTorch models on Google Cloud TPUs faced some challenges: either undertake a complete codebase rewrite into JAX, or grapple with legacy torch_xla. That paradigm has officially shifted. With the launch of TorchTPU, Cloud TPUs now feature a native backend (torch.device("tpu")) integrated directly into PyTorch's core interface. Enabling a training script to use TPUs now only requires minimal changes to your device initialization code. Providing device compatibility, however, is only half the battle, developers also need high performance production-grade, distributed training recipes. This is where TorchTitan on TPU comes in. TorchTitan TPU is a fork of Meta’s TorchTitan implementation that is adapted to enable model training on Google Cloud TPUs. The fork is available at https://github.com/google-pytorch/torchtitan/

Rather than requiring engineering teams to invent custom TPU sharding strategies or hand-craft low-level XLA communication passes from scratch, Google and Meta co-developed TPU-native execution passes directly inside TorchTitan. TorchTitan serves as the definitive vehicle for scaling PyTorch on Google Cloud TPUs.

TorchTitan provides official, battle-tested recipes for pre-training large dense models (such as Llama 3 70B/405B and Qwen 3 8B/32B), Mixture-of-Experts (such as Qwen 3 235B MoE), and diffusion transformers (such as FLUX.1 12B).

Beyond initial pre-training, TorchTitan serves as the scalable trainer inside post-training pipelines like VeRL for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) alignment. In these workflows, TorchTitan handles the parameter update step while synchronizing weights with high-throughput serving engines like vLLM-TorchTPU.

Parallelism is also kept separate from the model code: techniques such as fully sharded data parallelism and tensor parallelism are layered onto a parallelism-agnostic model implementation. The exception is expert parallelism: the MoE layer performs explicit communication through a pluggable token dispatcher that each backend can implement in its own way.

TorchTitan builds a model from a configuration tree, and that tree can be rewritten before the model is constructed. This offers a clean way to replace individual blocks of a model with versions that run better on TPU, without touching the model definition or the shared trainer loop.

We use this actively. The TPU recipes replace standard attention with Splash Attention, a Pallas kernel that handles causal, sliding-window and packed-document masks without materializing the attention matrix; replace the final projection and cross-entropy loss with a fused Pallas kernel that never writes the full logits tensor to HBM; and replace MoE expert matmuls with grouped Pallas kernels. Each kernel is a JAX function turned into a PyTorch custom op by TorchTPU's torch.tpu.pallas.jax_op decorator, which is all the glue a new kernel needs. Each replacement is a config flag, so a recipe can turn it on, and a test can compare it against the plain PyTorch path it replaces. Kernels can come from tokamax, the OpenXLA kernel library for TPU and GPU.

FSDP2 is built around the GPU execution model. Its all-gathers and reduce-scatters are issued from module hooks as explicit collective calls, and overlap with compute comes from running them on dedicated CUDA streams synchronized with events. None of that is visible to a compiler: the collectives sit outside the traced region, so on TPU a torch.compile region breaks at every FSDP hook and a whole-step graph cannot be captured at all. TorchTPU provides no-op stream and event objects only so that FSDP2 runs; XLA has no use for them, since it schedules work from data dependencies.

SimpleFSDP takes the compiler's view instead. Parameter sharding is a DTensor parametrization: reading a module's weight performs a redistribute from sharded to replicated, which is the all-gather, and its autograd backward is the reduce-scatter. There are no hooks and no explicit collective calls, so both collectives are ordinary nodes in the traced graph, whether that graph is a per-block torch.compile region or the Graph Trainer's single make_fx step. The XLA compiler schedules the all-gathers ahead of the matmuls that need them and overlaps the reduce-scatters with the backward compute, inside one program. Mixed precision is part of the same mechanism, a policy that sets the gather dtype (bf16 parameters) and the reduce dtype (fp32 or bf16 gradients) on that redistribute. Hybrid sharding for multislice is the same code on a 2-D mesh: shard within a slice over ICI, replicate across slices over DCN.

On FLUX this is what brought TorchTitan to 99% performance parity with JAX MaxDiffusion: the per-layer HLO for a SimpleFSDP block is a single module with the weight all-gathers, matmuls, attention and norms fused together, which is the shape of program XLA was built to optimize.

Many PyTorch trainers either run in eager mode with CUDA graphs to cut launch overhead, or apply torch.compile to isolated regions, typically one transformer block at a time. TorchTPU does not yet support the PyTorch torch.Stream abstraction, which limits the performance of mixing eager-based FSDP2 with layer-wise torch.compile. SimpleFSDP helps by expressing parameter sharding in a way that is compatible with compilation, whether using layer-wise torch.compile or full-graph capture.

TorchTitan's Graph Trainer provides full training-step capture (forward, loss and backward), producing one FX graph ahead of time with PyTorch's make_fx tracer, skipping AOTAutograd partitioning, so the whole computation is visible to XLA). GraphTrainer also runs optimization passes: to implement graph-based activation memory policy that decides what to save, recompute or offload. The train step then executes as a single XLA program.

This is the model XLA was built for, and it makes PyTorch training on TPU conceptually the same as JAX, where the whole train step is one jitted function. Owning the whole step as one graph is also what makes the large-model compilation techniques possible: scan over layers, which runs the decoder layers as a single loop body so that compile time and memory stop growing with depth, and the graph splitter, which cuts the step into separately compiled forward and backward programs with activations offloaded to host memory in between. On Qwen3 235B this path reaches 29%+ MFU.

The optimizer can join the graph as well: While the fused_adam optimizer is very performant, a functional AdamW update can be traced into the train-step (functional_adamw_step) and ensures an iteration has no host-side work between the first forward op and the weight write-back. On Qwen3-8B this path reaches 51-52% MFU.

Every lever below is described in the TorchTitan TPU optimization guide, and nearly all of them are already wired into the recipes. For most models, getting a good MFU on TPU is a sequence of configuration changes on an existing recipe rather than new code. The order matters, because each step changes what the next one measures. A lot of optimizations in TorchTitan are available as ready-to-use building blocks that can be enabled by a flag flip (see illustration below).

4. Tune Compiler, Communication and Profile

To check that TorchTitan runs efficiently across different classes of models, we tuned and scaled several of them on TPU v7x (Ironwood). At scale, Llama 3 405B (large dense model) holds about 57% MFU from 128 to 1,024 chips. Qwen3 235B (large MoE model) reaches 32.42% MFU on a single 1,024-chip slice and 30.51% on 8,192 chips across eight slices. Muse Glimmer 30B (modern dense model) reaches 43.4% MFU on 128 chips (4x4x8). FLUX.1 (diffusion model) reaches 19.4% MFU on 1,024 chips (8x8x16).

The following case studies show how we got there for three of these models, each representing a different class:

FLUX.1 is a 12-billion-parameter diffusion transformer, which makes it a useful check that the playbook above is not specific to LLMs. We trained it at 1024x1024 resolution on a TPU v6e-8 with FSDP in bf16 and a batch of three samples. Out of the box, in eager mode with FSDP2 and full activation checkpointing, a step took 11.49 seconds. Applying the playbook in order brought that down one stage at a time. Deferred-and-fused eager execution cut the number of HLO module invocations by 28% and the step to 9.95 s (15.5% improvement). Replacing FSDP2 with SimpleFSDP reduced the step to 6.52 s (52.6% improvement): the trace was dominated by FSDP2 copying parameter shards into a flat staging buffer before each all-gather and back out into per-parameter tensors afterwards; SimpleFSDP all-gathers each parameter directly, so the staging buffer and both copies are gone (see the SimpleFSDP section above). The splash attention kernel, with sequence packing and segment ids so that no MXU cycles go to padding, took the step to 5.55 s (17.5% improvement), and per-block torch.compile, which hands XLA large sub-graphs instead of fine-grained modules and moves control flow out of the Python interpreter, took it to 3.02 s (83.8% improvement).

The final step came from three small, local rewrites in the model code that the profile pointed at: splitting a fused projection into two separate linear layers so its output no longer had to be sliced apart, simplifying the rotary embedding to plain element-wise math, and unpacking Q, K and V with a view instead of a transpose. None of them changes what the model computes; each removes copies and lets XLA fuse more of the graph. Together they brought the step to 2.78 s (8.6% improvement), 4.1x faster than the starting point and about 99% of the throughput of the JAX-based MaxDiffusion on the same hardware.

We also scaled FLUX from 8 to 1,024 v7x chips, keeping three images per TPU. The best configuration went from 2.53 s per step (20.76% MFU) on 8 chips to 2.71 s (19.4% MFU) on 1,024, which is 93% weak-scaling efficiency. Past one 64-chip cube, pure FSDP slows down due to the model’s compact size. Hybrid sharding via HSDP fixes this: it shards within each 4x4x4 cube and replicates across cubes. We used the topology-aware device mesh, which maps the parallelism optimally to the physical 8x8x16 torus.

Muse Glimmer 30B is a modern dense decoder: 52 layers, grouped-query attention with 32 query and 2 key-value heads, a 202k-token vocabulary with a soft-capped output head, and a layer pattern that mixes sliding-window and global attention, with RoPE skipped on every fourth layer. It is the case that shows how much works out of the box. The model definition, the parallelization and the training loop ran on TPU unchanged; the port came down to two model-specific pieces. Upstream implements the sliding-window pattern with FlexAttention, which has no TPU backend, so the first step was a windowed SDPA inner attention that reproduces the window exactly and gives a correctness baseline, checked against upstream on CPU. The second piece was the fused cross-entropy kernel, which we extended to apply the soft-cap to each vocabulary tile as it computes the loss, so the 202k-wide logits never have to be materialized; without that the LM head alone dominated memory. Everything else, including the Graph Trainer wrapper, was the standard onboarding ladder, with each feature behind a flag and its own test. The resulting recipe trains the 30B on 64 v7x chips (a 4x4x4 slice) at sequence length 16,384 with one sequence per device, Graph Trainer plus SimpleFSDP and fp32 gradient reductions, and reaches 5,848 tokens per second per chip at ~44% MFU.

Qwen3-235B-A22B is the largest MOE model in this set: 94 layers, 128 experts with 8 active per token, 235 billion parameters of which 22 billion are active per token. It is the case that exercises the large-model half of the playbook. At this depth compiling the whole unrolled step as one program is not an option: XLA plans every buffer of a program at compile time, and a 94-layer graph laid out flat asks for more HBM than the chip has before a step can run, while its compile time grows with every layer. Getting it to work at all meant enabling one of the two Graph Trainer paths for large models. Scan over layers is the XLA-native answer, the same construct JAX and MaxText rely on: the 94 identical layers become one compiled loop body, so compile time and program size stop growing with depth. The graph splitter is the more flexible alternative: it keeps the layers unrolled but cuts the captured step into per-layer executables, which works no matter what kind of layers you have (and does not require layers to be the same). We run both for this model and they land within a few percent of each other. Either way the activations do not fit in HBM at sequence length 4096 with 16 sequences per device, so both paths offload layer activations to host memory and prefetch them back for the backward pass.

From there the playbook applied, one change at a time, each measured on the same 64-chip slice. We raised the scoped VMEM budget so the compiler could keep more kernel intermediates on chip, which also removed an exposed update at the tail of the optimizer step. We enabled a set of libtpu scheduling flags that let XLA's latency-hiding scheduler overlap the FSDP all-gathers and reduce-scatters with the surrounding compute and offload the reduce-scatters to the SparseCores, so most of the collective time is hidden behind compute, leaving only a small exposed reduce-scatter tail in the backward pass. We switched attention to the tokamax splash kernel, and replaced the expert matmuls with specialized paired grouped-matmul kernels backed by a K-tiled weight-gradient kernel, with hierarchical reduce-scatter and the SparseCore latency-hiding scheduler to hide the expert-gradient collectives. A fused route-score backward removed a large fp32 copy from the MoE backward pass. The last round of fixes came from profiling at 1,024 chips: keeping the blocked activation layout under scan offload, raising the backward layer-scheduler threshold so the expert reduce-scatters overlap with compute again, and pipelining small reduce-scatters across the scan loop's back-edge. The recipe runs at ~32% MFU on 256 v7x chips with fp32 gradient reductions kept throughout.

We also used this model to test how far TorchTPU scales, from 64 chips up to 8,192-chip v7x pod scale, in both single-slice and multi-slice configurations. A TPU slice is a set of chips connected directly over the inter-chip interconnect (ICI); multislice lets one training job span several slices connected over the data-center network (DCN), which is how a job grows past the largest single slice. Within a slice we run FSDP over ICI, sharding parameters, gradients and optimizer state across the slice's chips; across slices the model is replicated and the gradients are all-reduced over DCN once per step, so the only traffic on the slower network is a single gradient reduction, which overlaps with the backward pass. That is hybrid sharded data parallelism (HSDP), and it is what the 2,048- to 8,192-chip runs use, with 1,024-chip slices as the sharding group. The framework behaved as expected throughout: per-chip throughput dropped by 8% going from 64 to 8,192 chips (4,568 to 4,208 tokens per second per chip), near-linear scaling across 128x more chips. One caveat: for a model of this size, data parallelism beyond about 1K chips is a scaling experiment rather than a training recipe. This is a weak-scaling run: the batch per device stays fixed as chips are added, so the global batch grows with the chip count and at 8k chips is larger than one would use to train this model. What the run shows is that the framework, the compiler and the collectives hold up at that scale, not that this is the configuration to train with.

The three case studies above are deep dives: one model at a time, with a parity test and a profile behind every change. A team arriving on TPU usually has a different problem. They have a model they already train, often a Hugging Face one, and quite likely it won’t be the model that TorchTitan ships for TPU. They may well want every last percent out of it eventually, but first they need an easy way to get it running at all. That is the problem the Transformers modeling backend solves: it builds the model from the Transformers library by its Hub id and runs it through TorchTitan's trainer and parallelism instead of its own. On TPU the backend has a TorchTPU lane with a single configuration that applies the first rungs of the playbook optimizations to any supported checkpoint, with no change to the model code: SimpleFSDP in place of FSDP2, per-block torch.compile, the splash attention kernel registered as an attention implementation with the transformers attention interface so the model's own attention calls land on it, the fused Pallas cross entropy fed the final hidden states and the head weight instead of materialized logits, and, for mixture-of-experts checkpoints, the TPU grouped-matmul experts substituted for the portable implementation. Most of the dense architectures the backend supports train this way unmodified; mixture-of-experts checkpoints run through the same path but without expert parallelism yet. None of this reaches the hand-tuned numbers above, but it is a working, reasonably efficient first run for a model that has no TPU recipe yet, and a way to find out whether it deserves one.

Sooner or later a model deserves more than a generic path: it is the one you will train for months, it has an architecture the backend cannot express, or the out-of-the-box number is not the one you need. Porting a model to a new accelerator used to be a specialist project measured in weeks, with the hard-won lessons living in one engineer's head. On TorchTitan TPU it is a repeatable procedure. The repository ships a model-onboarding skill for coding agents that captures how the case studies above were done and walks an engineer, or the agent itself, through the same ladder, with the tests, the acceptance bars and the traps built in.

The roadmap runs along five lines: production readiness, precision, hardware, parallelism and models. On production readiness, the recipes gain the operational tooling that long runs on Google Cloud expect: integration with the ML Goodput Measurement library, so a run reports how much of its chip time turns into preserved training progress and where the rest goes, and with Multi-Tier Checkpointing on GKE, which keeps checkpoints in local memory and in-cluster replicas with Cloud Storage as the durable tier, so large jobs save often and resume quickly. On precision, the float8 recipe that today ships for Llama 3 70B, built on torchao's Float8Linear and v7x's native fp8 matmuls, extends to the rest of the model set. On hardware, Google has announced its eighth-generation TPUs, TPU 8t for pre-training and TPU 8i for inference and reinforcement learning, and TorchTitan will support them as they become available on Google Cloud, with the same recipes carried forward. On parallelism, expert parallelism for the MoE kernels is the open item named earlier, and long-context training is next after it, now that million-token context windows are the norm for frontier models. On models, the targets are the next size up from Qwen3-235B: Qwen3.8-Max, a 2.4-trillion-parameter mixture of experts, and Kimi K3, 2.8 trillion parameters with Kimi Delta Attention in place of standard attention on most layers, which will exercise the kernel plug-in path as much as the scaling one.

TorchTitan on TPU is no longer an experiment. The same codebase and plain-PyTorch model definitions train Llama 3 405B at about 57% MFU on up to 1,024 chips, and run Qwen3 235B, a mixture-of-experts model, on up to 1,024 chips in a single slice. In a weak-scaling test with a fixed per-chip batch, Qwen3 235B kept 92% of its per-chip throughput from 64 to 8,192 chips. That shows the framework, compiler and collectives hold up at pod scale, even though a model of this size would not be trained at that scale. The pieces that make this possible are not buried in one recipe. SimpleFSDP, the Graph Trainer with scan and the graph splitter, host offload, the Pallas kernel library and the optimization playbook are building blocks, each behind a flag and each documented with the number it is worth. For models that TorchTitan does not ship, the Transformers modeling backend runs 500+ Hugging Face models through the same trainer with the first optimizations already applied, and for teams that want more than that, the onboarding skill turns porting a model into a repeatable procedure that an engineer or an agent can follow.

That leaves two ways to use it. The first is to adopt TorchTitan as the training framework: pick a recipe, launch it on a slice, and build on a configuration whose performance is already known. The second is to keep the training code you have and take what you need: the kernels are standard PyTorch custom ops, SimpleFSDP and the Graph Trainer are modules you can import, the compiler flags and the playbook apply to any TorchTPU workload, and every recipe spells out what it enables and why. Either way, the fastest way to find out what this does for your model is to run it.

#10536. Waze rolling out cyclist alerts based on real-time locations from Garmin trackers

Waze is officially rolling out alerts for cyclists along your route that leverages location data from Garmin trackers.

In an effort to “boost driver awareness” of nearby bicycle riders, Waze will start showing real-time alerts for nearby cyclists to drivers in the area. The feature shows both a location on the map ahead as well as a visible alert on screen.

Waze knows the location of a nearby cyclist through a partnership with Garmin, with “LiveTrack” location sharing from the Garmin Edge bike computer or Garmin smartwatches users to share location data with the Google-owned navigation app.

Cyclists need to opt-in to the feature to have their location shown, and the alerts drivers see are temporary and anonymous. When cyclists end their ride, Waze alerts also go away.

This is rolling out starting today to the Waze apps on both Android and iOS.

#10531. What AI Gets Wrong About the Front End, and Why You Still Need to Know

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The product carousel looks finished. The image fills the card, the price sits under the product name, and the arrow on the right takes you to the next item. The demo goes smoothly.

Then you put the mouse down. You press Tab, and focus skips the arrows entirely. If you rely on a keyboard, the first product is the only one you can reach.

That’s a front-end bug hiding in plain sight. It’s also a good illustration of where AI fits into front-end development. A prompt can describe what a component should look like and what should happen on click. The implementation still has to account for how people reach that control, activate it, and understand what changed.

Imagine asking an AI coding assistant for this:

Build a React product carousel with previous and next arrows. Show one product at a time and wrap around at the ends.

Here’s an illustrative excerpt from the sort of implementation that can satisfy a quick mouse-driven demo:

With some CSS, this can look exactly like the arrow in the design. The click handler advances the slide. A screenshot shows the right layout.

But a plain div sits outside the normal Tab sequence and has no native button semantics or keyboard activation. Adding tabIndex={0} would make it reachable while leaving Enter and Space behavior to be implemented separately. The browser already provides those behaviors through a native button.

The real omission was in the requirement. “Show arrows” described the visual design. “Let someone reach and operate both controls with a keyboard” describes behavior you can test.

Here’s a small version of the same carousel. The product data is fixed for the example, and the slides change only when the user requests it.

The buttons have visible labels and work with both Enter and Space. When a slide changes, focus stays on the button you pressed, so you can press it again to keep browsing. The status message gives assistive technology a way to announce the new product and its position. Only the current product link is rendered, so Tab never lands on a link from a hidden slide.

Those are deliberate interaction choices. W3C’s carousel pattern provides guidance on using native buttons, keeping focus on the slide controls after activation, and handling automatic rotation when a design requires it. W3C Carousel Pattern

I’d revise the original prompt like this:

Build a manual React product carousel. Use native Previous and Next buttons. Both must work with Tab, Enter, and Space. Keep focus on the activated button after a slide change. Announce the new product name and position through a status message. Keep only the current product link in the Tab sequence. Show what happens when the user advances past the last product.

The extra detail also gives you a review plan. For this carousel, I’d use four checks:

AI can help draft automated tests for the active link and wraparound behavior. I’d still run the keyboard and screen reader checks on the integrated page. A global stylesheet can erase a visible focus outline. A surrounding component can introduce an extra tab stop. The experience depends on the page the customer actually uses.

A checkout form can look complete with sample data and lose the customer’s entries when a request fails. A responsive header can match the design at two widths and overlap when a translated label is longer. A component can render correctly in isolation and shift the page when a slow image loads.

Each example gives AI a concrete job. Ask it to preserve form values after a failed request, check the header with longer labels, or reserve space for the image. Then reproduce the condition yourself. Knowing which conditions matter is part of knowing the front end.

The next time AI gives you a carousel that looks ready, start the review with your hands off the mouse. Tab to Next, press Space twice, and see where focus lands. That short test gives you evidence about how the component works for someone who relies on a keyboard.

#10524. Nintendo Switch 2 emulation on Android already has The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake up and running

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake now runs surprisingly well on phones after just a week of additional emulator development.

Legend of Zelda fans all over the world have been marveling at the Ocarina of Time remake’s visuals and enhanced gameplay systems on the Nintendo Switch 2, but the Android emulator scene has been busy marveling over something just a little bit different. One Redditor got the latest Zelda game running on Banana Native, a free emulator that’s available right now on GitHub.

Ocarina of Time remake performance has now reached a major milestone into the 30 FPS territory with frame gen, which means the game is actually running at around 15 FPS, but then scaled and interpolated using AI frame generation to appear like 30 FPS. Banana Native isn’t a traditional emulator, as it doesn’t emulate hardware. Rather, it uses the game’s own ARM64 code to run on the phone’s CPU. The Switch 2 is powered by a mobile Nvidia chip, after all.

The user is running the Ocarina of Time remake on a Poco F7 Ultra, which sports a two-generation-old Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite (Gen 4) processor. The latest phones that are coming out, like the Redmagic 12 Pro, run a 6th-gen Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme chip, so performance could be a lot better on a newer phone. Apps like Banana Native require specific driver support for each phone to run, and as I pointed out in my Redmagic 12 Pro review, platforms like Droid Deck and Game Native don’t yet have proper drivers for the newest chips.

9to5Google’s Damien Wilde recently reviewed Droid Deck, another native-type emulator that lets you legally play Steam games you own on your phone by just signing into your Steam account. It works extremely well on lots of Android phones and helps showcase just how far Android emulation has come this year.

#10522. Google Pixel Tag Initial Review: The gold standard for Find Hub trackers, whatever that’s worth

Google’s Find Hub had a tumultuous launch for its performance, but also for a lack of clear direction in hardware. Over two years later, that’s finally arriving in the Google Pixel Tag which, even just a few days in, is the obvious gold standard in the race to build an AirTag for Android devices.

Google Pixel Tag is now available for purchase at the Google Store and Amazon.

There are plenty of really good Android Find Hub trackers on the market. Pebblebee and Chipolo have been long-standing examples of how to build good dual-network trackers that work on both Android and iOS, while Motorola’s Moto Tag series has delivered two Android-only trackers that deliver better performance and more focused features. But even with the Moto Tag, there’s a clear compromise. Features like the ability to ring your phone are tied to a separate app and, in turn, have some inherent clunkiness.

Pixel Tag is the device that finally brings it all together, and it’s brilliant so far.

I’ve only had the Pixel Tag for a week and, frankly, it’s impossible to properly review a tracker in that time. There are only so many ways to artificially test out how a tracker works, as most cases where you’ll find value in this sort of product can only occur in real life.

So, with that said, let’s focus on the fundamentals.

Google Pixel Tag pairs to your Android phone just like any other Find Hub tracker – though much faster compared to some others I’ve tested – but the differences are obvious towards the end of that process. The final screen shows options for setting ringer volume and an option to set up “Ring this phone.”

Those new features are what make the Pixel Tag so good, and so appealing compared to all of the competition. A quick double-tap on the Tag’s button rings whichever phone you connected it to, assuming it’s in range, and it works pretty reliably. You can also set up “left behind” alerts (now available on other Find Hub trackers too) for when the phone leaves the tracker behind – you can set up exclusion zones such as your home or work address.

One of my favorite examples of the added polish from Google is the ability to adjust the ringer volume on the page where you actually want to ring the tracker. In a crowded but quiet space where you know the tracker is close by, being able to quickly set the volume to low is a great addition. In my book, it’s the reason to buy Pixel Tag over any other Android tracker.

UWB, of course, only makes up-close finding all the better. I found this decently reliable on my Pixel 11 Pro XL, but it is a little bit frustrating that the directional cues vanish within about 10-12 feet. Fine for a backpack or something like that, potentially a problem if your Tag is attached to a set of keys.

On that note, my biggest hardware complaint is that Google took the Apple route of having no built-in mounting mechanism. The super thin and light hardware of Pixel Tag is wonderful, don’t get me wrong, but being forced to buy a separate accessory just to attach it to my bag is frustrating! One of Google’s partners, Bellroy, built one of these accessories and it’s simple enough – a silicone case with a rope. I wish I didn’t need it, but it works, and at least you get two of them in a pack for $25.

The hardware itself is pretty good across the board, really, with the battery door also being slightly easier to open compared to the Moto Tag. Inside is a standard CR2032 battery that Google says will last “over one year.” I’d personally still prefer a rechargeable battery with USB-C or wireless charging, but this is a good enough solution given the amount of time the battery lasts – hopefully Google’s claim lives up.

Rounding out some of the key aspects here, it’s worth saying that the built-in speaker is more than loud enough at its highest setting, but the value for me so far has really come in that the low setting is actually low enough to not be a nuisance. I do find it a little strange that there’s no visual indication of where the Tag’s button is, but the packaging and app both do a good enough job of letting you know.

I really just wish it would have a rechargeable battery, preferably via USB-C. It would have been the only tracker on the market to support UWB and be rechargeable.

I’m looking forward to seeing how Pixel Tag performs as the travel season spins back up, so stay tuned for a later long-term review of Google’s tracker in the coming months. Initial impressions, though, are solid. Sound off in the comments below if you have any questions around Pixel Tag, I’ll answer everything I can!

Google Pixel Tag is now available at the Google Store and Amazon.

#10519. Pixel Tag tidbits: Surprise NFC, Find Hub setup, & Bellroy keychain cases

The Made by Google 2026 lineup is finally complete with tomorrow’s retail launch of the Pixel Tag. You can find our review here, while there are some additional details about the device since the August announcement.

The Pixel Tag is the first Find Hub tracker with NFC. Google did not mention this connectivity specification in August, with Near Field Communication letting you tap the tracker to the back of your phone to immediately open device settings. The NFC area on the Pixel Tag is below the circular battery door, which you hold down and twist counterclockwise to open.

On Android 17 QPR2, tapping immediately opens the details page. On QPR1, you first get a system notification that you need to click. NFC joins Bluetooth 6.0 with Channel Sounding and Ultra Wideband (UWB).

The setup process adds a new screen after the initial pairing and terms of use. It lets you configure: Notify when left behind, Ring this phone, Edit name, Edit category, and Adjust volume for this tag.

The Pixel Tag has a physical button on the rear side below the battery cover. Clicking twice plays a loud sound on your phone if both devices are nearby. Once found, you can tap the button once to silence, or turn it off from your phone. This feature can be disabled from the Pixel Tag’s device details page on a tracker-by-tracker basis.

Google does not have a first-party case for the Pixel Tag. Instead, it’s working with Made for Google partner Bellroy.

There are two models available from the Google Store, with the first made of silicone. The Bellroy Lite Case Duo costs $24.99 and comes in a pack of two: Mist and Shadow colorways. It wraps everything but the face of the Pixel Tag (and its speaker cutout), while giving you a hole in the corner and color-matching cord.

There’s also the Bellroy Leather Tag Case in Artichoke, Azalea, Black, Pewter, and Red Ochre for $29.99.
