#10518. How to Reduce Hallucinations in LLMs

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask one of the newest language models whether a backpack comes in green, and it'll usually admit it doesn't know. Ask it how to return that backpack, and it may send the customer to a returns page, an account screen, and a return label that the store has never had. That's still a hallucination: an answer that sounds right, comes with total confidence, and isn't true.

Whatever you're building, the fix is the same: stop trusting the model with the parts it can get wrong, and check its work before anyone sees it.

In this tutorial we'll build a small support bot for a made-up outdoor gear store, three times over. The first version is the prompt plenty of bots start with. Then we'll apply five fixes, run six tricky customer emails through every version, and finish with a bot where the model can't slip a made-up price, date, or product past the code.

An LLM hallucination is output that's fluent and confident but not backed by the facts: a product that doesn't exist, a policy nobody wrote, a date that's a week out, or a citation to a paper that was never published. The word covers two different failures:

Each one needs a different fix, so it helps to keep them apart.

A language model writes by predicting likely text, one token at a time. It has no separate store of facts it checks against, and nothing in that process says "stop, you don't know this". Training adds to the problem. OpenAI's research on why language models hallucinate points out that when models are graded only on how many answers they get right, a guess scores better than "I don't know", so they learn to guess.

Newer models are much better at the first one. They'll often say they don't know a price rather than invent one. But they still fill gaps with what's typical, and what's typical isn't always what's true for your business. You can't train that out of a model you call through an API. What you can change is what you ask it to do, what you give it, and what you check.

A made-up answer costs more than one bad reply. Some of the costs are already on the record:

In a support inbox, the cost is quieter but adds up: a refund you didn't owe, or a customer who goes looking for a live chat button that isn't there and doesn't come back.

Northwind Outdoor sells four things: two packs, a rain cover, and a tent. It has two orders on file and three short policies. Here's all of it:

Six emails come in, and each one is there to trip the bot in a different way:

The traps: the 40L pack doesn't come in green, but the 28L does. The 40L is water-resistant, not waterproof. There's no 60L. Whether the return is allowed depends on a membership, a sale price, and a count of days since delivery. The tracking details are in one order record. And the store has no price match policy at all.

Make a folder, add "type": "module" to its package.json, and install the Anthropic SDK and Zod:

Every version calls the model through one small helper:

We're using Claude Opus 5.5 at low effort. These are short, simple replies, so the model doesn't need to think for long. Swap in any model you like, because none of the fixes depend on it.

Opus 5.5 didn't invent a single price, colour, or delivery date across the six emails. Every reply said it couldn't see the store's details. That's real progress compared with older models, but look at what it said instead. Here's the reply to the email about a pack the store doesn't sell:

It treats the Ridgeline 60L as real and offers to help pick a fit. The other replies made up the store around the customer:

Northwind has none of those. None of it is a wild guess: it's what a typical online store has. That's exactly why it's dangerous. A customer who goes looking for the live chat or the newsletter discount won't find it, and the bot never answered the question anyway.

The model can't know Northwind's prices, so give them to it. This is the idea behind retrieval-augmented generation (RAG): find the records that matter and put them in the prompt. Our store is small enough to send all of it.

Two things changed. The store data goes in the system prompt, wrapped in tags so the model can tell data from instructions. And the prompt tells it to answer only from that data. Here's the green pack email again:

Right on all three counts, and it even pointed to the pack that does come in green. The return, which needs four facts from two places, came back right as well:

Delivered October 6, a Trail Club member, so 60 days, so December 5. Bought on sale, so store credit for what was paid, not the list price. Version 2 got all six emails right.

The last line of that prompt matters as much as the data: "If the answer isn't in the data, say you don't know and that a team member will reply by email." Without it, a model that can't find the answer has to choose between letting the customer down and guessing, and training has taught it to guess. With it, "I don't know" is a right answer, not a failure.

The price match email is the test, since the store data says nothing about price matching:

It said it didn't know, passed the question to a person, and answered the parts it could from the data.

If version 2 got everything right, why build a version 3? Because "right on this run" isn't the same as "right every time":

The next three fixes take the facts and the rules away from the model completely.

Version 3 splits the work into three steps. First, the model reads the email, but instead of writing a reply, it fills in a form. Every field is a choice from a list, a yes or no, or text copied from the email:

Zod describes the form, and z.toJSONSchema() turns it into a JSON schema for the API's structured outputs. The API holds the model to that schema, so product can only be one of our four product IDs, not_sold, or none. There's no way for it to answer ridgeline-60. Zod then checks the answer again, so the rest of the code gets a typed object. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema goes deeper on this pattern.

The model is still reading natural language, which it's very good at. It just isn't trusted to know what the store sells anymore.

A form also makes the model's mistakes easy to see. The first time I ran this, the form had no asks_something_else field, and the model filed the price match email under product. The reply didn't make anything up, but the email never reached a person. In version 2, a mistake like that would be hidden inside a paragraph. Here it was one wrong value in a JSON object, and the fix was one more field.

If all you need from this step is decisions like these, a model built for typed answers can do it faster and for less. Our typed AI pipeline tutorial builds the same kind of email sorter with Jev and Clairic.

Second step: plain code takes the form, looks up the product and the order, applies the rules, and does the maths. What comes out is a list of facts in plain sentences:

Every number in there was worked out by code that does the same thing every time, and that you can unit test. The 34 days, the December 5 deadline, and the $151.20 in store credit come out the same on every run, whatever the model does.

The last rule in decide() matters most. If an email asks something the rules don't cover, the facts say so and the email goes to a person. Code decides when to say "I don't know", not the model.

Third step: the model writes the reply from the facts and nothing else. Writing is the one job here it does better than code.

Even with the right facts, a model writing a reply can still add something: a helpful "ships in 1–2 business days", or a price it remembers from somewhere. So the last step checks the reply. Anything that looks like a number, an order number, or a product name has to appear in the email or the facts:

It's simple on purpose. It doesn't understand the reply. It just refuses to let through a value that came from nowhere, and that covers the hallucinations that cost money: prices, dates, quantities, and products. Nothing in the reply can talk it out of a result.

The writer runs the check on every draft:

If a draft fails, the model gets one retry and is told exactly which values weren't in the facts. If the retry fails too, the customer gets the facts as a plain list. That's less charming, but it's true.

A check like this will raise false alarms, and that's fine. On my first run, it read a line about the tent's 2.1 kg weight as a product called "Weighs 2" and sent the draft back for a retry. The weight was right, and it was the only thing flagged. A false alarm costs a few seconds. A missed one costs a customer. I tightened the patterns (the version above has the fix), but I'd still rather it flag too much than too little.

It's the same thing that separates the better agentic AI coding tools from autocomplete: they run the tests and read the errors instead of trusting their first draft. Model output isn't finished until something other than the model has checked it.

This script sends each email through all three versions, saves every reply, and runs the check from Fix 5 over all of them. Versions 1 and 2 are checked against everything the store knows:

The check caught one thing in version 1: the "1–2 business days" it made up for processing an order. It missed the "up to 24 hours" for tracking in the same reply, but only because $24 is the rain cover's price, so 24 counted as a known number. And it couldn't catch the invented returns page or live chat at all, because they aren't numbers. That's why the check is the last fix, not the only one.

Reading all 18 replies, here's how each version did:

Version 3 was less helpful than version 2 in one place. It couldn't give the rain cover's price, because decide() only adds facts about the product the email names. Version 2 had the whole store to look at. In version 3, the fix is a rule you write once: when a product has an add-on, include the add-on's facts too.

Here's version 3's reply to the price match email:

No model is free of hallucinations, so you can't stop one from ever making something up. What you can do is build so a made-up detail can't reach a customer: let code own the facts, and check the model's output against them before it's used. Where it matters, send anything your rules don't cover to a person.

It cuts them a lot, because the model no longer has to guess facts it was never given. In our test, putting the store's data in the prompt took the bot from no useful answers to six right ones. It doesn't stop the model misreading what it retrieved, mixing up two records, or filling a gap the retrieved text leaves. Pair it with a way out and a check on the output.

Not really. Temperature controls how random the wording is. A low temperature makes the model give the same answer more often, including the same wrong answer.

Collect inputs that have tripped your app (or are likely to), write down the right answer for each, and run every version of your prompt or pipeline over the same set. Automated checks like the one in this tutorial catch made-up values. Read the replies yourself for the rest, because the made-up returns page above would pass any number check.

Hallucinations come from asking a model to do jobs it isn't built for: knowing facts it was never given, and running small programs in its head. The five fixes take those jobs away:

The model still does what it's best at: reading messy text and writing a friendly reply. It just isn't trusted with the facts anymore. From here, try a seventh email that asks two things at once, add the add-on rule to decide(), or extend the check to catch colours and sizes as well as numbers.

#10516. Save serious money with RedPocket’s Black Friday plans that start at $5 a month

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

I even made you a table to make it easier to understand: 👇

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.

During the Black Friday deal period, you can get a $150 rebate on the following phones:

Legal stuff: Rebates are paid out 90 days after service begins on a new line activation with an eligible plan. Full rebate terms at redpocket.com/rebate. Devices are available while supplies last.

Usually with sponsored content, I have to spend the whole time convincing you that the product is worth buying. But RedPocket’s Black Friday deals do all that work on their own.

Not only do you get amazing discounts for the first 12 months of service by prepaying for a full year, but RedPocket’s “Lock-in Low” promise means that you get to stay as close to that promotional price as possible once the first year is over.

There’s no contract and no port-in requirement. You can keep your number, keep your phone, and activate with an eSIM on compatible devices. During the event, there’s even a Refer-a-Friend credit that gets you $25 for every person you bring to the service.

Explore RedPocket’s plans or compare the annual offers. This is one of the best Black Friday decisions you’re going to make this month, and maybe even ever.

#10514. Powering the Agent Development Lifecycle with any framework, from ADK to LangChain and beyond

End to end walkthrough of the new Agents CLI Extension System, with a worked LangChain example and a custom deploy policy

When we launched Agents CLI at Google Cloud Next '26, we built it around one strong opinion: going from an idea to a production agent on Google Cloud should take a single unified command surface. For teams building with Agent Development Kit (ADK), that opinionated path has worked. You run agents-cli create, write the agent logic, and ship to Cloud Run with observability and evals wired up, in about thirty minutes.

The agent framework landscape has grown more diverse in the months since. In addition to ADK, developers today pick between LangChain and LangGraph, specialized newcomers like Deep Agents, or in-house frameworks tailored to a specific business domain. Different teams prefer different frameworks, and the right choice for a greenfield prototype is rarely the right choice for a production migration. Enterprise admins may also have governance policies they want to enforce across the ADLC for all of their development teams, such as: a compliance scan, an SSO gate, a Model Risk Management hop.

Can a team benefit from the deployment, evaluation, and observability work in Agents CLI without being forced to adopt ADK, or without being forced to accept the default behavior of every command? Starting with version 1.5.0, the answer is yes. Agents CLI now ships with an Extension System that lets developers override or add commands without forking the tool. Version 1.8.0 ships the first-party LangChain extension, which replaces the framework-coupled commands with their LangChain equivalents and leaves the rest of the lifecycle untouched.

We will walk through two journeys below. First, to show you the ADLC with LangChain agents, we'll show you how you can scaffold, run, deploy, and evaluate a LangChain agent through the Agents CLI lifecycle using the first-party extension. Then we build our own small extension that wraps the deploy command with a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) scan, demonstrating the pattern enterprise platform teams can use to enforce their own policies across every project. Both journeys use the same mechanism.

Most agent development toolchains available today share a limitation: they are opinionated about one specific framework, and the surrounding capabilities (deployment targets, CI/CD pipelines, evaluation harnesses, observability wiring) assume the developer has adopted that framework. When your framework matches the tool's assumption, the opinionated path works. When it does not, you're left with three bad options.

The first is to fork the CLI, maintaining a divergent copy and re-syncing it on every upstream release. You pay a permanent maintenance tax, and every upstream improvement has to be hand-merged into the fork. The second is to wrap the CLI behind custom scripts, building a parallel command surface that invokes the underlying tool. The problem is that developers (and their coding assistants) forget about the wrapper and call the underlying tool directly, bypassing the intended abstraction. The third is to walk away and build everything from scratch, which can consume several engineer-months of work that the team had not planned for, and which rarely produces tooling as polished as the upstream project.

The problem shows up in two scenarios. One is enterprise customers adopting Agents CLI: in regulated industries, a central platform team often requires that every deploy invocation be wrapped with a compliance check, that login route through corporate single sign-on, or that evaluations flow through a Model Risk Management pipeline. Each of these requirements, implemented today, means either forking the CLI or building a wrapper layer. The other is teams that use ADK most of the time but need to run a non-ADK framework on a specific project. Within the Agents CLI command surface, six commands assume the ADK runtime: playground, run, and four eval subcommands. The other eighteen or so (scaffold, infra, deploy, publish, login, and friends) are framework-agnostic. Without extensions, choosing a non-ADK framework for a single project meant losing access to all twenty-four.

The Extension System handles both with one mechanism.

An Agents CLI extension is a directory in a Git repository that contains a single file called agents-cli-extension.yaml. The file declares which built-in commands the extension overrides and which new commands it adds. From the official documentation:

Extensions take two shapes, described in the Using Extensions page. A command override replaces the behavior of a built-in like playground, deploy, or eval generate. The overridden command receives the user's arguments verbatim, so the user experience stays consistent with the native command. A new command exposes a workflow the CLI does not currently provide as agents-cli . Both shapes can coexist in the same extension, and most non-trivial extensions use a combination of the two.

Extensions are distributed through Git repositories rather than through a dedicated package registry. The design draws from the extension model used by the gemini-cli project. There is no central index to publish to, no package format to maintain, and no ownership roster to apply for. Any directory inside any Git repository can serve as an extension, and Git handles the versioning, pinning, and distribution. For enterprise platform teams, this means creating an internal platform-extensions repository and pointing their developers at it, with no external registry to coordinate with.

An extension can be referenced in several ways, depending on where it lives and how the developer wants to use it:

The # syntax lets a single Git repository hold multiple extensions, with the fragment identifier selecting one of them. Enterprise platform repositories often bundle together a family of related extensions (one for deploy, one for login, one for scaffold create) under a single repo with shared ownership and release cadence.

Extensions install at two scopes. The default is the project scope, where the extension and its lockfile entry are committed to the project's own repository. Teammates and CI pipelines pick it up on checkout. The alternative is the user scope, enabled via the --global flag, where the extension is stored in the user's home configuration directory and applies to every project on the machine. The user scope is the one you reach for when an extension needs to be available before any project exists, such as a framework bootstrap extension that configures agents-cli create itself.

When both scopes define the same command, the project scope wins, and agents-cli info surfaces the source of each active command. Scoping is deliberately explicit: the Extension System will not silently merge or override extensions, and any conflict surfaces to the developer at install time or in the extension list output.

The Extension System follows the discipline of package managers like uv or npm: it resolves a Git reference to an exact commit SHA at install time, records the resolved SHA in a lockfile, and uses the lockfile to re-materialize the exact reviewed code on every fresh checkout. The agents-cli install command restores the vendored working copy from the pinned SHA without advancing the pin, which guarantees that CI and local development run bit-for-bit identical code. To advance a pin to the latest commit on the tracked reference, the developer runs agents-cli extension update, which re-resolves the reference and re-prompts for third-party trust. The complete lifecycle is in the Using Extensions → Lifecycle section.

The Extension System is marked experimental in v1.8.0. The manifest schema and the command surface may still change in breaking ways before the system is finalized, so teams depending on it should pin their Agents CLI version.

The LangChain first-party extension is a project template: one command scaffolds a working LangChain agent wired into the Agents CLI lifecycle. The resulting project has zero google-adk dependency and deploys to Cloud Run through the same code path an ADK project uses. The template's own description reads:

In modern LangChain (version 1.0 and later), the state management layer is provided by LangGraph, so the three frameworks share a common graph representation. The template serves all three communities with the same code.

A Google Cloud project with billing enabled is also required. If you do not have one yet, it can be created at console.cloud.google.com.

If Agents CLI is not already installed on the development machine, it can be installed in a single command:

Finally, we authenticate with Google Cloud and enable the APIs the agent will depend on at runtime:

Scaffolding and extension installation are the same operation. Rather than installing an extension first and then scaffolding a project, we point agents-cli create directly at the extension's template, pinned to a specific release tag:

The output confirms the scaffold succeeded:

The scaffolded project already contains an agents-cli-extension.yaml at its root, which is auto-loaded at project scope the next time any command is invoked. There is nothing to install manually, because the project itself is the extension's installation. A developer scaffolding a LangChain project does not need to know the Extension System exists. The command looks and feels like a native agents-cli create invocation that happens to produce a LangChain project instead of an ADK one.

The structure looks similar to an ADK project: same app/, same tests/, same Terraform under deployment/, same manifest at the root. The two differences are the agents-cli-extension.yaml at the top (which carries the LangChain overrides) and the langgraph.json file (which configures the LangGraph dev server used by the local playground).

The main agent definition lives in app/agent.py:

The agent is created with the langchain.agents.create_agent helper, which compiles into a LangGraph CompiledStateGraph. The type annotation on root_agent makes this explicit. Modern LangChain uses LangGraph as its state management layer, which is why the template is positioned as supporting LangChain agents, LangGraph graphs, and Deep Agents through the same lifecycle: all three compile to the same underlying graph.

The scaffolded project contains no dependency on the Agent Development Kit. We can verify this directly:

The grep returns no matches, and the exit code of 1 confirms the absence of ADK references. The google-* packages that do appear in pyproject.toml are the ones any Google Cloud Python project would include: google-cloud-aiplatform[evaluation], google-cloud-logging, langchain-google-genai, and the OpenTelemetry Google Cloud exporters.

Behind the scenes, Gemini-3.8-flash on Vertex AI has invoked the compiled graph in-process, called get_weather("San Francisco"), received the simulated foggy response, and reasoned the final sentence back to the user. The warning line tells us which extension commands are active, with one entry per overridden command. The warning is deliberate: an override quietly changes native behavior, so the loader always announces which commands are being intercepted and by whom.

For a local inspector, agents-cli playground boots LangGraph Studio on port 8080, replacing the ADK web UI that would otherwise appear.

To confirm which commands the extension has modified, we can inspect the help output:

The [↑ langchain] suffix is the Extension System's way of signalling that a command has been overridden, together with the name of the extension responsible. Both human developers and coding assistants reading the terminal can tell at a glance which commands behave differently in this project.

For a complete inventory, agents-cli info lists every active extension together with the exact command vector it executes:

Six overrides total, falling into three categories: four framework-coupled replacements (playground, run, eval.generate, and publish.gemini-enterprise), two explicit refusals (eval.dataset and eval.optimize need an ADK agent object to introspect, so the LangChain project refuses them cleanly rather than producing invalid results), and the publish.gemini-enterprise override additionally refuses only on the Agent Runtime deployment target while delegating to the native command on Cloud Run and GKE. The First-party frameworks page has the official breakdown.

The deploy command is not overridden by the LangChain extension. Deploying a LangChain project to Cloud Run uses exactly the same code path as deploying an ADK project:

The underlying mechanism shells out to gcloud run deploy --source . against the project's Dockerfile, which includes:

That CMD is identical to the one in an ADK project's Dockerfile. The LangChain template serves A2A (Agent-to-Agent) protocol traffic over FastAPI, exposing the application under app.fast_api_app:app. The deployment path does not need to know which framework is powering the agent, because uvicorn and gcloud run deploy operate on the FastAPI contract.

The same holds for infra, scaffold enhance, scaffold upgrade, install, info, setup, lint, and login. All unchanged in a LangChain project. A year of Agents CLI work on these commands lands in LangChain projects for free, with no divergence to maintain between the ADK and LangChain code paths.

Evaluations in a LangChain project follow the familiar pattern from the ADK walkthrough, with one difference: the eval generate step is overridden to drive the LangChain graph, while the subsequent evaluation steps use the native commands:

The overridden eval generate command produces output in the standard EvaluationDataset format, which the native eval grade, eval compare, and eval analyze commands know how to consume. The evaluation service and the grading rubrics are framework-agnostic.

If a developer invokes one of the refused eval subcommands, the extension responds with a clear explanation rather than failing silently:

A clear refusal is more useful than a silent failure, especially when a coding assistant is driving the terminal and would otherwise retry a broken command multiple times before giving up.

The LangChain template is the first-party demonstration of the Extension System, but the same mechanism is open to any team that wants to customize the Agents CLI command surface. Enterprise platform teams can author and distribute extensions internally, encoding their policies as code and shipping them through the Git infrastructure they already use for everything else. Every developer on the team then follows the same checks by default.

To illustrate the pattern, we will build a small extension called sbom-deploy that wraps the deploy command with a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) scan, refusing to deploy if any critical vulnerabilities are found. The authoritative reference for the manifest schema is the Authoring Extensions page.

We begin by creating a directory for our extension and adding the manifest file at its root:

The manifest contains five top-level keys, and no others are allowed. The schema uses strict validation, so a typo like reqires: fails install with a did-you-mean suggestion rather than silently dropping the override.

The schema field identifies the manifest schema version. The name field is a single path component (slashes and parent-directory references are rejected) and becomes the directory under extensions/ where the vendored copy is stored. The requires block declares which versions of Agents CLI the extension supports, together with a policy (warn or error) that controls behavior on a version mismatch. The commands block is where the actual overrides and additions live.

The Rules that matter section of the authoring docs enumerates additional constraints worth knowing about. Two in particular: you can override a specific subcommand (such as eval.generate) but not a top-level command group (such as eval as a whole), and run: is a command vector, not a shell command, so any chaining of commands has to happen inside a wrapper script rather than inside the manifest.

Because run: is a command vector executed without a shell, chaining multiple operations means writing a wrapper script that sequences them explicitly:

AGENTS_CLI_DISABLE_OVERRIDES=1 is what makes this work. Agents CLI sets it in the environment of any script invoked through an override. When our wrapper calls agents-cli deploy recursively, the call lands in the native deploy command rather than looping back to our own override. No special syntax, no recursion.

We can verify this directly. For demonstration, we swap the SBOM scan for a few echo statements that confirm the environment and arguments:

The --project foo flag passed through to the wrapper verbatim, and the environment variable was set by the loader before our script began executing. The wrapper has everything it needs to delegate to the native command.

With the manifest and wrapper in place, we can install the extension. During development, point at the local path:

Once the extension has been pushed to a Git repository, the same syntax works with a remote reference:

After installation, we verify the extension is active by listing the installed extensions and inspecting the help output:

The [↑ sbom-deploy] tag next to each overridden command is the Extension System's user-facing signal that the behavior has been modified.

Behind the scenes, extension add resolved our Git reference to an exact commit SHA and recorded the resolution in a lockfile at the project root:

Both the lockfile and the vendored working copy under extensions/ should be committed. On a fresh checkout, agents-cli install re-materializes the exact reviewed code from the pinned SHA, the way uv sync or npm install restore pinned dependencies. The install command never advances the pin, so an extension cannot change its behavior between two deployments of the same commit.

To advance the pin to the latest commit on the tracked reference (branch, tag, or SHA), the developer runs agents-cli extension update sbom-deploy. To switch to a different tag or branch, re-run agents-cli extension add with a new --ref. The complete lifecycle is detailed in the Using Extensions → Lifecycle section.

Agents CLI began life as an opinionated path to shipping ADK agents on Google Cloud. With the Extension System in place, those opinions are now yours to override, replace, or augment.

Install Agents CLI and scaffold a LangChain agent in two commands:

Then explore the official documentation: the Extensions overview, Using extensions, Authoring extensions, and First-party extensions. The full source code of Agents CLI, including the first-party templates, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

#10511. Google Fi brings back network switching with ‘secondary’ partner, expands W+ beyond Pixel

Google Fi Wireless is rolling out a big update today with the return of network switching thanks to a “secondary” partner.

Google Fi is now bringing back a second partner as part of its new “super network.” Most users will be on the “Primary network,” but Google Fi will automatically switch users to the “Secondary network” if it meaningfully improves performance with so-called “smart thresholds” for doing so. At launch, there is no manual switching.

Google is not naming the second network today, but did share that Ultra Wideband is supported. Officially, you are switching between “two of America’s fastest 5G networks.”

Network switching will first be supported on Pixel 9 and newer devices that have a Google Fi eSIM (and no other SIM active). It makes use of Android’s Multiple Enabled Profiles (MEP) eSIM technology that allows for two carrier profiles on a single chip.

It might be important to note that T-Mobile actually bought out Sprint, and added their towers to their network so its safe to say that Google Fi still works with the same coverage as before the merger.

The Google Fi app gains a “Your super network” card in the Home tab that shows whether you’re connected to the Primary or Secondary network. “See activity” lets you view switching history and when it last occurred. Google will also note if you’re in range of W+ networks.

Google today is also expanding its Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ network beyond Pixel phones to select Samsung Galaxy (S23, Fold/Flip 5, and newer) and modern Motorola phones. These premium Wi-Fi networks are available in millions of airports, malls, stores, and restaurants. The connection happens automatically, with the Google Fi Virtual Carrier Network encrypting data.

The final update today sees the companion app gain “Ask Fi.” This AI-powered chat is meant to quickly provide answers and guided troubleshooting for common issues. You can request human support with seamless handoff that keeps the chat context.

#10498. How Text-to-Image AI Generators Work, and 5 Tools That Deliver Good Results

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Type “a lighthouse on a cliff at dusk, oil painting, warm light” into a text box, wait ten seconds, and a picture that never existed appears. It feels like magic, but it is a well-understood pipeline of language models, neural networks and a great deal of math. Knowing how that pipeline works makes you a better prompter, helps you diagnose bad results, and makes it easier to choose the right tool for a job.

This guide explains how text-to-image generators turn words into pixels, why they fail in predictable ways, and how to write prompts that work with the model instead of against it. It then compares five tools that consistently produce good results, starting with Pollo AI, and finishes with a practical workflow, a troubleshooting table and an FAQ. Plans, credits and model versions change often, so confirm current details on each vendor’s site before you commit to a subscription.

Early image generators were mostly GANs, or generative adversarial networks. A GAN pits two networks against each other: a generator that produces images and a discriminator that tries to spot fakes. GANs could produce sharp faces and textures, but they were hard to train, often collapsed into repeating the same few outputs, and were poor at following free-form text.

A second line of research used autoregressive models, which generate an image piece by piece the way a language model generates words. These handled text prompts better but were slow at high resolution.

The shift came with diffusion models. Researchers showed that a network trained to reverse a gradual noising process could generate images with excellent quality and diversity, and that it was far more stable to train than a GAN. Combine that with text conditioning and a compressed image representation, and you get the systems behind most of today’s tools. Newer models increasingly swap the traditional convolutional network for transformers, but the core idea of iterative denoising is still the foundation.

Under the hood, mainstream generators combine three ideas: a way to understand text, a way to represent images compactly, and a way to generate them gradually. The sections below follow a prompt from keystroke to finished picture.

Neural networks cannot read words, so your prompt is first split into tokens, small chunks of text such as whole words or word fragments. A text encoder then converts those tokens into an embedding, a grid of numbers that captures meaning and context. Many systems use encoders trained jointly on images and their captions, so that the text “a red bicycle” and pictures of red bicycles end up close together in the same mathematical space. Some newer systems add a large language model to understand longer, more detailed instructions.

This is why wording matters. The encoder only sees what you wrote, and it has a limited capacity. A short, specific prompt produces a clear signal. A long, rambling one dilutes each instruction.

Instead of drawing on a blank canvas, the model begins with random static. The pattern of that static is controlled by a seed number. The same prompt with a different seed gives a different image, because it starts from different noise. If a tool lets you fix the seed, you can make results repeatable and change just one variable at a time.

During training, engineers take millions of real images and add noise to them in small steps until only static remains. The network is then trained to predict the noise that was added at each step, which teaches it to reverse the process. At generation time, the model applies that skill to pure noise, removing a little at each step. Because it has learned what real images look like, the “noise” it removes gradually reveals something that looks plausible.

Your text embedding is fed into the network through a mechanism called cross-attention. At each step, parts of the evolving image can “look at” relevant parts of the prompt and adjust accordingly. A region that will become a lighthouse attends to the word “lighthouse”. A region that will become the sky attends to “dusk”.

A setting called classifier-free guidance, often shown as guidance scale or prompt strength, controls how strongly each step is pulled toward the prompt. The model computes one prediction with the prompt and one without it, then exaggerates the difference. A low value gives the model freedom and often more natural, varied results. A high value follows the words more literally but can produce oversaturated, over-sharpened or distorted images.

Running diffusion directly on full-resolution pixels would be very expensive. The idea behind Stable Diffusion, known as latent diffusion, is to compress images into a much smaller latent representation using an autoencoder. Denoising happens in that compact space, and a decoder turns the final latent back into a full image. This is a major reason high-quality generation now runs in seconds on a cloud GPU instead of minutes.

The number of denoising steps and the scheduler, which decides how much noise to remove at each step, shape the speed and look of the result. More steps usually add detail up to a point, then give diminishing returns. Many tools hide these settings, but the trade-off is always the same: fewer steps are faster and cheaper, more steps can look more refined.

Most pipelines finish with an upscaler or refinement pass that sharpens detail and fixes small artifacts. Many tools also offer inpainting, which regenerates only a selected region, outpainting, which extends the canvas, and variations, which restart from a related image.

A generator can only produce what it has learned to represent. Models are trained on very large collections of images paired with captions, and the quality, diversity and licensing of that data affect both the output and the legal picture. Models trained on more photographs tend to produce convincing photorealism. Models tuned on illustration and concept art lean toward painterly looks.

This also explains a few behaviors. Common subjects look great because the model has seen thousands of examples. Rare or very specific subjects, such as an unusual machine part or a niche cultural item, often look generic or wrong. Biases in the data can show up as stereotypes, for example in how “a doctor” or “a CEO” is depicted, so it helps to describe the people and setting you actually want.

Understanding the process explains the familiar failures:

Pollo AI’s text to image AI is a browser-based generator built around a simple loop: write a prompt, pick a style, generate, and download. Its page highlights HD output and a wide range of looks, from photorealistic scenes to cute cartoons, anime, pencil sketches, isometric art, abstract compositions and surreal visuals. Files can be downloaded as WebP, PNG or JPEG, a practical spread for web work: WebP keeps pages light, PNG preserves clean edges and transparency-friendly graphics, and JPEG works almost everywhere.

Pollo is also part of a wider platform that covers AI video, so a still image you like can become the starting point for motion without switching ecosystems. That matters for teams producing social content, because one workflow can cover both the image and the short clip built from it.

Things to check: the page does not list specific model names or credit amounts, so review the current plan details before relying on it for high-volume work. Commercial use requires a paid plan.

Best for: quick, versatile image generation across many styles, especially if you also plan to make video.

Midjourney has built its reputation on strong aesthetics. Images tend to arrive with polished composition, dramatic lighting and a distinctive painterly feel, which is why it is popular for concept art, mood boards, album-style visuals and editorial illustration. It offers style references and personalization, so you can steer results toward a consistent look across a project. It also supports editing and variation tools for refining a chosen image.

The trade-offs are practical. It is a paid service, and its prompts reward experimentation over literal instruction following, so you sometimes get something beautiful that is not exactly what you described.

Best for: artistic, high-impact visuals where mood matters more than exact specification.

Adobe Firefly focuses on professional workflows and commercial confidence. Adobe describes Firefly’s own models as trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public-domain material, and it attaches Content Credentials metadata that records AI involvement. It connects with tools many teams already use, including Photoshop features like generative fill and generative expand, which suits designers who edit as much as they generate.

Firefly also provides controls such as style and composition references. Its results can feel cleaner and more commercial than Midjourney’s, which is an advantage for marketing assets and a limitation if you want a wild artistic look.

Best for: marketing and design teams that need editing tools and clearer licensing.

Ideogram is known for rendering legible text inside images, a task where many generators struggle. That makes it a strong choice for posters, social graphics, logo concepts, packaging mockups and product visuals that need a headline or short phrase to read correctly. It also offers style controls and a community gallery that doubles as a prompt library.

Even so, long paragraphs of on-image text can still go wrong. Keep the copy to a few words, put it in quotation marks in the prompt, and describe the lettering style.

Best for: graphics that combine imagery with short, readable text.

Leonardo AI is built for creators who want more control. It offers multiple models, fine-tuning options, image-to-image guidance and a canvas for editing, with a particular following among game artists and designers who need consistent characters and assets. Features such as reference-based generation help keep a character recognizable across many scenes.

The wide set of options is powerful but means a steeper learning curve than a one-box generator, and credit systems can take time to understand.

Best for: consistent characters, game and concept assets, and users who like tuning settings.

A reliable prompt has five parts. Put the most important part first, because it often carries the most weight.

How do text-to-image generators work? They convert your prompt into a numerical representation, then use a diffusion model to remove noise from a random starting image step by step, guided by that representation, until a matching picture appears.

Which text-to-image tool is best? It depends on the job. Pollo AI suits fast, varied generation, Midjourney suits artistic work, Adobe Firefly suits commercial design, Ideogram suits images with text and Leonardo AI suits tuned, consistent assets.

Why does the same prompt give different results? Each run starts from a different random seed. Fixing the seed, where a tool allows it, makes results more repeatable.

Can I use AI-generated images commercially? Often yes, but the rules differ by tool and plan. Pollo, for example, notes that commercial use requires a paid plan. Always check the current terms.

How do I get readable text in an AI image? Use a tool strong at text, keep the wording to a few words, and add the text in a design editor if accuracy is critical.

What file format should I download? WebP for lightweight web pages, PNG for graphics and sharp edges, JPEG for broad compatibility.

Can I turn a generated image into a video? Many platforms support it. Pollo AI, for example, also covers AI video, so you can move from a still to motion in one place.

Text-to-image generators work by turning language into numbers and using those numbers to guide a model that denoises random static into a picture. Once you understand that, prompts stop being guesswork: you give the model a clear subject, style and lighting, you iterate one change at a time, and you fix small flaws locally. Start with a versatile option such as Pollo AI, then add Midjourney, Firefly, Ideogram or Leonardo when a project needs their particular strengths.

#10486. Streaming comes to API Gateway: real-time and AI-ready APIs, without changing your spec

For most of the last decade, an API call looked like a transaction. A client sent a request, waited, and got a complete response back in one piece. That model is exactly what most API management tooling — including API Gateway — was built to handle, and it still describes a huge amount of production traffic.

But that no longer describes all traffic types. A model generating a response emits tokens as it produces them, not in one batch at the end. An agent working through a multi-step task reports progress as it goes. A dashboard, a collaborative editor, a live inference endpoint — these all need a connection that stays open and carries data in both directions.

Until now, developers building those experiences in API Gateway had to make an uncomfortable choice: put API Gateway in front of their APIs and give up streaming, or route streaming endpoints around the gateway and give up the authentication, API key enforcement, and observability that made the gateway essential in the first place. Teams ended up with a split estate — governed APIs on one path, ungoverned streaming traffic on another.

We're announcing that streaming support in API Gateway is available in Public Preview. Your streaming endpoints can now sit behind the same gateway, with the same policies, as everything else you serve.

For AI applications, streaming also strengthens API Gateway's connection to Gemini platform. A gateway that uses model routing now streams responses from Gemini, Claude and OpenAI gpt-oss models in model garden in Gemini platform as they're generated, and streaming backends you host anywhere else work the same way. Alongside Apigee and Agent Gateway in Gemini platform, API Gateway is the lightweight entry point to Google Cloud's AI gateway capabilities.

API Gateway now supports the full range of streaming patterns modern APIs rely on:

Streams can stay open for up to one hour. As with any long-lived connection, clients should implement reconnection logic so that a session survives the end of a stream — a well-behaved WebSocket or SSE client should be doing this anyway.

This is the part we are most pleased with: there is nothing new to learn.

Streaming is not a different product, a different resource type, or a different way of describing your API. There are no new extensions to add, no streaming-specific annotations, no separate spec for streaming operations. The same document that describes your existing API describes your streaming API.

Everything you already rely on continues to work exactly as before:

During Public Preview, you opt-in when creating the gateway:

That single flag is the entire difference. At GA, it is planned that streaming will simply be how API Gateway behaves, with no opt-in required and, as during Preview, no changes to your API config.

Alongside streaming, gateways get a deterministic default hostname:

Previously the default gateway.dev hostname included a generated hash, which meant you could not know your gateway's URL until after you had created it. That is a small thing that causes a lot of friction: you can't write the endpoint into a client config, a Terraform module, a CI pipeline, a firewall allowlist, or a partner integration doc until the resource exists — and if you tear the environment down and rebuild it, the URL changes underneath you.

Now the hostname is a pure function of values you already chose. You can generate it, template it, document it, and commit it to source control before the gateway exists, and you get the same URL every time you rebuild the same gateway in the same project and region. For anyone managing environments as code, or promoting a configuration from dev to staging to prod, this removes an entire category of bootstrapping problem.

Streaming isn't a niche feature for AI applications — it's the expected interaction pattern.

Users expect to see a model's response appear as it's generated, not after a thirty-second pause. Agents need to report intermediate steps. Tool-calling protocols and remote MCP servers depend on long-lived, incrementally-delivered connections. An API management layer that can't stream severely limits the user experience of AI applications.

With streaming, API Gateway becomes a viable front door for that agentic traffic — and the value of a front door is exactly the same as it's always been, only more urgent. AI endpoints are expensive, they consume quota, they need per-consumer authentication and rate limiting, and they need to be observable. These are the governance problems that an AI gateway is intended to solve. The alternative — every team standing up its own proxy in front of its own model endpoint — is how organizations end up unable to answer basic questions about who is calling what.

Model routing in API Gateway lets you expose a single OpenAI-compatible endpoint and dispatch requests to Gemini, Claude, or OpenAI gpt-oss models based on the model field in the request payload. Model routing plus streaming means clients get a familiar, standards-based streaming interface, while you retain centralized control over which models are reachable, by whom, and under what policy.

Streaming, model routing and MCP support are all features within API Gateway that provide a lightweight on-ramp to AI gateway capabilities within Google Cloud. These AI gateway capabilities allow teams to monitor, secure and control agentic traffic of tools, models and agents in their ecosystem, and they're delivered as a composable suite so you can choose the right gateway for each use case. For more expansive AI policies and full lifecycle management, use Apigee. To manage and secure agent connectivity, use Agent Gateway in Gemini platform. You can also combine these gateways: API Gateway or Apigee can sit in front of your agents and APIs, or behind Agent Gateway so your agents can reach models, tools, and MCP servers. For example, you can route your agent's egress through Agent Gateway for strict security governance, and then pass the request to API Gateway to handle model routing.

A few things to plan around while the feature is in Preview:

If you're already using API Gateway, the fastest way to see this working is to point a new streaming-enabled gateway at an existing backend that emits SSE and watch the events arrive incrementally. No spec changes needed.

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

#10484. I’ve waited a long time for the Boox Picco, and I’m not disappointed

At around $100, the Boox Picco offers one of the most portable e-reader experiences you can get. You add MagSafe-like capability with a refreshingly simple UI, and you have a delightful way to read, literally wherever you are.

There is an argument to be made for every single shape and size e-readers come in, and they come in so many different sizes it’s hard to keep track. I love the idea of having a 10-inch display that I can skim, but even if it’s perfect for sitting at home and reading anything from novels to comics, I don’t think it provides a very good travel experience

But even smaller devices like the Boox Palma make you choose between bringing a second device or sticking with your phone. Most often, the phone wins out – especially when you have a flexible approach with a foldable like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 I’ve been using daily.

Unlike the other phone-sized e-readers from Boox, the Picco stands at just 4.15 inches tall. The screen measures at just under that – 3.97 inches. It’s essentially the size of a MagSafe or Qi2 wallet – just small enough to fit on the back of your phone with the included magnetic case.

It’s thin, too. A 5.5mm side profile means it slips anywhere a minimal wallet would without issue. That goes for pockets just as well as crossbody bags; perfect for travel.

And that’s where I think I find so much more joy in using this over what would otherwise be considered a small e-reader, like the Palma. I don’t have a lot of excuses not to carry it around when I’m on the go. I’ll throw it in my Bellroy bag when I’m carrying what I’d considered my minimal EDC. It slides into any internal pocket, and it weighs next to nothing.

The back of the Boox Picco isn’t magnetic, but the included case is. If you want the thinnest possible footprint, forego the case. But adding it lets you do one of the coolest – and slightly copium-inducing – things with your phone: attach the e-ink device directly to your OLED-laden phone.

It does not work well with an unconventional device like my Galaxy Z Fold 8, but if you have nearly any other device like the Pixel 11, you should get a rather perfect fit. The Picco squares up to the back of any slab phone with Qi2 magnets, whether internal or via a case.

But, why? Why slap another device and add weight/thickness to your phone when it’s perfectly capable of displaying e-books?

Because it’s cool! That’s the driving reason. You can make the argument that it doubles your phone’s usability or that it gives you an easy place to store the tiny e-reader. The truth is that it does add extra thickness to your phone, and using your phone with the Picco piggybacking along can be uncomfortable, since your fingers now rest on another display and not a case or phone backing.

You can get used to it, and, as unnecessary as it may seem, it’s a fun way to carry around a much more eye-friendly screen.

The Picco diverts from other Boox devices in another way. It doesn’t run Android, which means you have no access to third-party apps like Google Play Books, Amazon Kindle, or any other store/library. Instead, the Picco uses a companion app to transfer EPUBs from your phone to the tiny e-reader.

I initially opposed this idea when I got a sneak peek during IFA in Berlin this year. But after using it for a few weeks, I’ll admit I’ve come around. But that all comes down to how you consume your media.

I try to source EPUBs no matter what device I use. On Android-powered e-readers, that means downloading Google Play Books and reading from there; I have my EPUBs uploaded to my own Google Play Books library, so they’re available universally.

On the Picco, my approach hasn’t changed much. I take those same EPUBs I have in storage and transfer them from the companion app.

I can understand how the lack of third-party libraries would make the Boox Picco moot. Still, I’ve been a proponent of buying digital books I can store in my own drives, so I can read them wherever, whether that’s the Google Play Books library or from a device like the Picco.

All of that is a roundabout way of saying the Picco, though it doesn’t let you use apps to read, makes it easy to get digital copies loaded and ready to flip through.

It uses a handshake connection that requires you to open the companion app on your phone, hit connect, and do the same on the Picco. Once connected, transferring EPUB files is as easy as selecting them from your phone’s physical or cloud storage and queueing them up. The process takes seconds, and the only issue I’ve had was solved by a force stop and trying again.

The homescreen is simply a carousel of your library, with a delightful little progress bar under each title as they’re sorted. You can access and manage the bookshelf from that page, as well as the minimal “apps” and settings. I appreciate the focus app and reading stats, but the countdown and to-do options require using the companion app. I don’t see the point of those.

It doesn’t even have a control panel. Swiping down from the top surfaces screen brightness and color controls. Even though this little guy is just over $100, it still has a front light controlled by a physical switch on the side. Compare that to e-readers at much higher price points without that game-changing feature.

The buttons opposite don’t control volume. Instead, they’re there for page turning.

It’s just simple and well-designed. Features are there to make reading a better experience, down to granular text controls for your digital books. It does exactly what it needs to do – nothing more, nothing less.

As far as battery goes, it meets my expectations. It’s not massive, so the battery is small in comparison to a smartphone. It still holds its own easily, with an easy week of use if you’re reading for an hour or two a day. Beyond that, it takes little time to charge and does great on idle, which is perfect for such a passive device.

The Picco will hit home for you if you want an ultra-portable option for your e-books. The same goes for those who don’t mind the smaller screen and lack of Android or third-party app support.

I get genuine joy every time I pick it up to read where I left off, and I think that’s because it’s so simple and nothing about it feels bloated, even the companion app, which is also straight to the point.

I think it’s been a while since a device has offered that experience for me. Adding the form factor, optional magnetic attachment for your phone, and price tag just gets me to that conclusion sooner.

The Boox Picco is the most adorable e-reader I’ve used, and it’s $109 on Amazon.

#10480. Building Reliable Product Emails Into Your Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password reset email is part of your login flow. An invoice notification is part of your billing experience. Yet development teams often treat these messages as background tasks: render a template, call a sending API, and move on. That approach leaves an important part of the product experience untested until a user cannot complete the action they started.

Testing the application interface alone leaves a gap. A successful request to an email provider does not establish that the recipient can find the message, understand it, or use its links. Product emails need explicit success criteria, useful diagnostic records, and recovery options when something fails.

A reliable workflow combines application tests, controlled mailbox checks, and pre-send diagnostics for authentication and inbox placement. A comparison of available tools can help you identify which checks your existing setup covers and where additional testing would be useful. The goal is to investigate specific gaps rather than add another service without a clear purpose.

Start with the action the recipient needs to complete. A weekly activity summary can tolerate a delay that would make a sign-in code useless. An invoice needs accurate amounts and a clear payment destination. A security alert needs enough context for someone to recognize the event without exposing sensitive information.

For each message, document its trigger, expected timing, required data, and expiration behavior. Decide what the application should display if submission fails or the user requests another copy. These decisions give developers and testers concrete acceptance criteria.

For a password reset, test the complete journey: requesting the message, receiving it in a controlled mailbox, following the link, and setting a new password. Also check what happens when the link expires or has already been used.

Template tests should cover the data your application actually encounters. Try long names, missing optional fields, non-Latin characters, and large order totals. Escape user-provided values appropriately, and make sure conditional sections disappear cleanly when their data is absent.

Inspect both HTML and plain-text versions. Instructions should remain understandable when images are blocked, while descriptive links and readable text should make the next action clear. Check that every URL points to the correct environment, especially when staging and production share template code.

A development sandbox lets you inspect generated messages without contacting customers. Use it to check headers, attachments, and content. Keep a separate rendering check for the email clients your audience uses, because a sandbox preview cannot reproduce every client’s behavior.

A background queue can prevent a slow sending API from delaying a web request. It also creates another failure boundary: a worker may time out after the provider has accepted the email but before your application records the result.

If the job retries blindly, the recipient may receive two messages. Assign a stable identifier to each logical notification and record the provider’s message identifier when available. Where supported, reuse an idempotency key for retries of the same send operation, following the provider’s documented retention window.

Distinguish retries from intentional resends. A user asking for a new reset link is a different event from a worker repeating an uncertain request. Define which link remains valid when another is issued, and test that behavior explicitly.

Retries also need limits. Temporary service failures may justify another attempt with backoff. A permanently invalid address needs a correction or suppression workflow, rather than repeated sending.

Provider events describe different stages of the sending process. An acceptance response means the provider accepted the request. A delivery event generally means the receiving mail server accepted the message; it does not prove inbox placement or that a person read it.

Capture the events your provider exposes, including bounces, delays, and complaints. Associate them with the original notification so support staff can investigate a particular report. Confirm each event’s documented meaning instead of treating similar labels across providers as interchangeable.

Webhook handlers should verify incoming requests using the provider’s documented mechanism and tolerate duplicate notifications. Avoid assuming events always arrive in chronological order. Record enough history to explain what happened without allowing a late event to overwrite a more informative state.

Before releasing a significant template or infrastructure change, send controlled messages through the production sending configuration to test accounts at commonly used mailbox providers. Use synthetic data, and check placement, rendering, link behavior, and how long the message takes to arrive.

Inspect authentication results in the received headers. Published DNS records are only part of the configuration; the actual message must use the expected signing domain and sending infrastructure. If you change providers or domains, repeat these checks rather than assuming the previous results still apply.

Treat test mailbox results as samples. They can reveal problems, but they cannot guarantee identical placement for every recipient. Mailbox settings, filtering decisions, and recipient history can produce different outcomes.

Provide a resend option with sensible rate limits and explain when users should expect the message. Where appropriate, let signed-in users verify or correct their address. For public account-recovery forms, avoid responses that reveal whether a particular address has an account.

Give support staff a way to distinguish a notification that was never generated from a failed submission or a message accepted by the receiving server. Protect that diagnostic access, limit retention, and avoid logging reset tokens, private message bodies, or unnecessary personal information.

Reliable product email depends on the whole journey, from the triggering event to the recipient’s next action. Testing each stage and making failures observable helps teams resolve problems before users repeatedly click resend or abandon the task.

Nintendo Switch users have until January 25 to change how they sign in to the Nintendo Switch app on their smartphones.

Nintendo is going all-in on passkeys in a scheduled update to the Nintendo Switch app and will be sunsetting 3rd party logins after January 25, 2027. The news was made available on an official Nintendo support page. That means you won’t be able to use your Google or Apple accounts to log in to your Nintendo account as of January 26. After January 26:

Instead of using 3rd-party account linking, Nintendo is recommending that users register a passkey for an exclusive, secure login experience through the Nintendo Switch app. If you forget to do this before January 26, you can always head to the Nintendo account page and run through the “forgot password” instructions. Nintendo recommends making sure you can log in to your account ASAP via non-Google or Apple links to ensure that you don’t run into problems before January.

Nintendo didn’t elaborate on the policy change, although some surmise it’s based on security-related measures. The advancement of AI has created bots that can hack into systems with unprecedented speed, so it’s possible that Nintendo is simply trying to shore up any possible area it cannot 100% control. Either way, if you use the Nintendo Switch app or have a Nintendo account of any kind, you should change how you log in immediately.
